On sampling two spin models using the local connective constant Thanks: Funding: Research supported by EPSRC New Investigator Award, grant EP/V050842/1, and Centre of Discrete Mathematics and Applications (DIMAP), University of Warwick, UK. Acknowledgement : The author would like to thank Daniel Štefankovič, Eric Vigoda and Kostas Zampetakis for the comments and the fruitful discussions.
Charilaos Efthymiou

Abstract
. This work establishes novel optimum mixing bounds for the Glauber dynamics on the Hard-core and Ising models. These bounds are expressed in terms of the local connective constant of the underlying graph G G . This is a notion of effective degree for G G . Our results have some interesting consequences for bounded degree graphs: (1) They include the max-degree bounds as a special case (2) They improve on the running time of the FPTAS considered in [Sinclair, Srivastava, Štefankonič and Yin: PTRF 2017] for general graphs (3) They allow us to obtain mixing bounds in terms of the spectral radius of the adjacency matrix and improve on [Hayes: FOCS 2006]. We obtain our results using tools from the theory of high-dimensional expanders and, in particular, the Spectral Independence method [Anari, Liu, Oveis-Gharan: FOCS 2020]. We explore a new direction by utilising the notion of the k k -non-backtracking matrix H G , k \UpH_{G,k} in our analysis with the Spectral Independence. The results with H G , k \UpH_{G,k} are interesting in their own right.



1.
Introduction

This work focuses on counting and sampling problems that arise from the study of Gibbs distributions.
We typically consider Gibbs distributions
\mu
which are defined with respect to an underlying
graph
G=(V,E)
. Unless otherwise specified, graph
G
is finite, undirected and connected.

We parametrise
\mu
by using the numbers
\beta\geq 0
and
\gamma,\lambda>0
, while each configuration
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}

gets probability mass

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mu(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\times\lambda^{\#\textrm{assignments ``1" in $\sigma$ }}\times\beta^{\#\textrm{edges with both ends ``1" in $\sigma$ }}\times\gamma^{\#\textrm{edges with both ends ``-1" in $\sigma$}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Z}(G,\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
is the normalising quantity called the
partition function
.

A natural problem in the study of Gibbs distributions is computing the partition function
\mathcal{Z}
.
In many cases, this is a computationally hard problem, e.g., see
[
40
,
21
]
. Typically,
the focus is on efficiently obtaining good approximations of
\mathcal{Z}
.

To keep our discussion concise in this introduction, we restrict our attention to the technically most interesting
case, the
Hard-core model
. The reader can find further results in the subsequent sections.
The Hard-core model is a probability distribution over the
independent sets

\sigma
of an underlying graph
G
such that each

\sigma
gets probability mass
\mu(\sigma)
, which is proportional to
\lambda^{|\sigma|}
, where
|\sigma|

is the cardinality of the independent set
\sigma
.
Using the formulation in (
1.1
), this distribution corresponds to having
\beta=0
,
\gamma=1
and
\lambda>0
.
Typically, the parameter
\lambda>0
is called
fugacity
.

The seminal works of Weitz in
[
42
]
and Sly in
[
38
]
(together with the improvements on
[
38
]
obtained in

[
39
,
20
]
) establish a beautiful connection between
phase transitions
and the performance
of counting algorithms for the Hard-core model. This connection implies that for graphs
G
of maximum degree
{\Delta}

and the Hard-core model with fugacity
\lambda
on such graphs,
there exists a critical value
{\lambda_{c}}({\Delta}-1)
such that following is true: for any fugacity
\lambda<{\lambda_{c}}({\Delta}-1)
,
there exists an FPTAS for estimating the partition function, whereas, for

\lambda>{\lambda_{c}}({\Delta}-1)
it is NP-hard to estimate the partition function even within an exponentially large factor.
Notably, the critical value depends on the
maximum degree

{\Delta}
of
G
.

Recall that, for integer
k>1
, the critical value
{\lambda_{c}}(k)=\frac{k^{k}}{(k-1)^{(k+1)}}
signifies the uniqueness/non-uniqueness
phase transition for the Hard-core model on the
k
-ary tree, established by Kelly in
[
28
]
.
Also, recall that FPTAS stands for Fully Polynomial Time Approximation Scheme.
An FPTAS computes an estimation which is within a factor
(1\pm\upepsilon)
from
\mathcal{Z}

in time which is polynomial
n
and
\log\frac{1}{\upepsilon}
.

An important improvement on the aforementioned connection between phase transitions and hardness comes with the seminal
works of Sinclair, Srivastava and Yin in
[
36
]
and the subsequent sharper analysis provided by Sinclair, Srivastava, Štefankovič
and Yin in
[
35
]
. These works propose an FPTAS for estimating the partition function of the Hard-core
model for any fugacity
\lambda<{\lambda_{c}}(\upd)
, where
\upd
is the
connective constant
of the
underlying graph
G
. The connective constant is a natural measure of “effective degree” for
G
. Recall that
it is defined by
\upd=\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{v\in V}\pi(v,k)^{1/k}
, where

\pi(v,k)
is the number of self-avoiding walks of length
k
emanating from vertex
v
. The connective constant is
a well-known quantity, especially studied in mathematics and physics (with the breakthrough result in
[
12
]
).
For many families of graphs, the connective constant
\upd
is significantly smaller than
the maximum degree
{\Delta}
. For such families,
[
36
,
35
]

provide a tighter analysis than
[
42
]
.

Interestingly, the results in
[
36
,
35
]
refine the aforementioned connection between phase transitions
and hardness, implying that the dependence of the critical value should be on
\upd
instead of
{\Delta}
.
In that respect, the hardness results in
[
39
,
20
]
are sufficient to imply that it is NP-hard to estimate the partition function for

\lambda>{\lambda_{c}}(\upd)
(see also discussion later in this introduction).

The bound
\upd\leq{\Delta}-1
implies that the results with respect to the maximum degree
correspond to considering the worst-case graph instances with regard to the relation between
{\Delta}
and

\upd
, i.e., assume
\upd={\Delta}-1
. On the other hand, the use of the connective constant
\upd

provides a
finer
classification of the hard instances of the problem, i.e., since graphs with the same maximum degree
can have different connective constants.

Impressive as they may be, the aforementioned algorithmic results are primarily of theoretical importance rather than
practical. Their running times, even though polynomial in
n
, have a rather
heavy
dependence on the maximum
degree
{\Delta}
. Typically, the exponent of the polynomial bound is an increasing function of
{\Delta}
, e.g., we
have
O(n^{C\sqrt{{\Delta}}})
running time.
This motivates the use of the Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) approach to the problem which, typically,
yields
significantly
faster algorithms.
For the distributions we consider here, the aim is to use the MCMC approach and get a Fully Polynomial Randomized Time
Approximation Scheme (FPRAS) for the partition function
\mathcal{Z}
. An FPRAS computes with probability
1-\updelta
an
estimation which is within a factor
(1\pm\upepsilon)
from
\mathcal{Z}
, in time which is polynomial
in
n
,
\log\frac{1}{\upepsilon}
and
\log\frac{1}{\updelta}
.

To this end, we use
Glauber dynamics
. This is a popular Markov chain utilised for sampling from high-dimensional
Gibbs distributions such as the Hard-core model. In this setting, the measure of the efficiency is the
mixing time
,
i.e., the rate at which the Markov chain converges to the equilibrium.

In recent years, there have been significant developments with regard to analysing the mixing time of
Glauber dynamics using ideas from the theory of
high dimensional expanders
e.g., see Alev and Lau in
[
2
]
.
Advances in the area, such as the work of Anari, Liu and Oveis-Gharan in
[
3
]
, which
introduces the well-known
Spectral Independence
method, as well as the subsequent work of Chen, Liu and Vigoda in
[
11
]
, establish
optimum
mixing
for Glauber dynamics on the Hard-core model for any
\lambda<{\lambda_{c}}({\Delta}-1)
. Recall that Glauber
dynamics exhibits optimum mixing when the mixing time attains its (asymptotically) minimum value, which
is
O(n\log n)
; see Hayes and Sinclair
[
25
]
.
Optimum mixing for Glauber dynamics implies an
O^{*}(n^{2})
time FPRAS for the partition function (where
O^{*}(\cdot)

omits the poly-logarithmic terms).

It is also worth mentioning the further improvements in
[
9
,
8
]
,
which obtain mixing bounds for graphs of unbounded maximum degree.

In light of all the aforementioned results which establish mixing bounds expressed in terms of the maximum
degree
{\Delta}
, the works in
[
36
,
35
]
motivate us to ask whether
it is possible to obtain similar bounds expressed in terms of the connective
constant
\upd
, i.e., establish optimum mixing for Glauber dynamics for
\lambda<{\lambda_{c}}(\upd)
.
Our aim is to obtain such results using tools from the theory of high-dimensional expanders and, in particular, the
Spectral Independence method.
To the extent that we are aware, such an approach has not been considered before for
general graphs
.

Obtaining mixing bounds of Glauber dynamics in terms of the connective constant is a well-studied problem in the area
and results are already known in the literature for
special
families of graphs, e.g. see the seminal works in

[
34
,
41
]
for the Hard-core model on the integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Using standard arguments like those in
[
7
,
15
]
, one can show that

[
36
,
35
]
imply
O(n\log n)
mixing for Glauber dynamics on
the Hard-core model with
\lambda<{\lambda_{c}}(\upd)
on
amenable graphs

1
1

1

Roughly speaking, an infinite graph
G=(V,E)
is amenable if
\inf\frac{|\partial W|}{|W|}=0
,
where the infimum ranges over all finite
W\subset V
, while
\partial W
is the boundary of
W
. If
G
is finite,
we typically have
|\partial W|\ll|W|
, for sufficiently large blocks
W\subset V
.
.

Another well-studied case where the connective constant arises naturally in the mixing bounds is that
of the Hard-core model on the sparse random graph
G(n,d/n)
. The state-of-the-art bound for
this distribution is from the work of Efthymiou and Feng in
[
17
]
which obtains
O(n^{1+\frac{C}{\log\log n}})

mixing of Glauber dynamics for any
\lambda<{\lambda_{c}}(d)
, improving on the works in
[
4
,
18
]
.

As opposed to the previous works which focus on
special
families of graphs, our endeavour here is to obtain
optimum mixing bounds
without any assumptions
about the underlying graph, i.e., we don’t necessarily assume that
the graph is amenable or a typical instance of
G(n,d/n)
.

For our algorithmic applications, it makes sense to use a finite version of the connective constant, i.e.,
rather than the limiting object. For integer
k\geq 1
, we introduce the notion of the

radius-
k
connective constant

\upd_{k}
defined by
\upd_{k}=\sup_{v\in V}\pi(v,k)^{1/k}
.
The connective constant
\upd
corresponds to
\upd_{k}
for
k\to\infty
.

One of the main results in this paper is the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
{\Delta}>1
,
k\geq 1
and
\upd_{k}>1
consider graph
G=(V,E)

of maximum degree
{\Delta}
such that the radius-
k
connective constant is
\upd_{k}
.
Also, let
\mu
be the Hard-core model on
G
with fugacity
\lambda\leq(1-\varepsilon){\lambda_{c}}(\upd_{k})
.

There is a constant
C=C({\Delta},k,\upd_{k},\varepsilon)
such that the mixing time of Glauber dynamics on
\mu

is at most
Cn\log n
.

Handling directly
\upd_{k}
for a general graph
G
is an extremely challenging task
(at least for the authors of this work). We obtain our results indirectly by utilising the notion of the

k
-non-backtracking matrix
\UpH_{G,k}
. We establish rapid mixing bounds in terms of

\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}
, where
N
is a large number.

The
k
-non-backtracking matrix
\UpH_{G,k}
:

Perhaps, the best known “incarnation” of the
k
-non-backtracking matrix
\UpH_{G,k}
is the

Hashimoto non-backtracking matrix

\UpH_{G}
, introduced in
[
22
]
. Matrix

\UpH_{G}
is typically studied in (mathematical) physics. Recently it has found application
in spectral algorithms for network
inference problems, e.g. see
[
13
,
1
]
(with the breakthrough result in
[
5
]
).

Recall that
\UpH_{G}
is a 0/1 matrix indexed by the
ordered pairs
of the adjacent vertices
in
G
such that for any
e=uw
and
f=zy
, we have

\displaystyle\UpH_{G}(e,f)=\mathds{1}\{w=z\}\times\mathds{1}\{u\neq y\}\kern 5.0pt.

That is,
\UpH_{G}(e,f)
is equal to
1
, if
f
follows the edge
e
without creating a loop;
otherwise, it is equal to zero.

\UpH_{G,k}
is a generalisation of
\UpH_{G}
. It is a 0/1 matrix indexed by the
k
-non-backtracking walks
in
G
. For
e
and
f
, two
k
-non-backtracking walks,
we have
\UpH_{G,k}(e,f)=1
if walk
f
extends walk
e

by one vertex without creating a loop. Otherwise, the entry is zero.
For a formal definition of
\UpH_{G,k}
, see

Section

5.5
.
Note that
\UpH_{G}
corresponds to
\UpH_{G,k}
for
k=1
.

We obtain the following optimum mixing results using
\UpH_{G,k}
.

Theorem 1.2
.

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, for
\uprho>1
and integers
k,N>0
,
{\Delta}>1
, consider graph

G=(V,E)
of maximum degree
{\Delta}
such that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.
Also, let
\mu
be the Hard-core model on
G
with fugacity
\lambda\leq(1-\varepsilon){\lambda_{c}}(\uprho)
.

There is a constant
C={C({\Delta},\uprho,k,\varepsilon)}
such that the mixing time of Glauber dynamics on
\mu

is at most
Cn\log n
.

Related to
Theorem

1.2
, are the rapid mixing bounds in the literature that are expressed in terms of norms of
{\UpA}_{G}
,
the
adjacency matrix
of
G
.
In his seminal work in
[
23
]
, Hayes obtains rapid mixing bounds in terms of
\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}
.
Further results with respect to
\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}
can be obtained using other approaches, e.g.,
using the
stochastic localisation
method to analyse the mixing of Glauber dynamics
[
8
]
.
Using
\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}
in our setting has major drawbacks compared to

\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}
. E.g.,
\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}
tends to be strongly correlated
with the maximum degree
{\Delta}
. On the other hand, it is well-known that for small values of
N,k
, the norm
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}
captures
information related to the degree sequence, whereas, for large values of these two parameters, the norm expresses

structural properties
of graph
G
.
This makes it highly desirable to work with
\UpH_{G,k}
. It turns out that it is more challenging to handle matrix
\UpH_{G,k}

than
{\UpA}_{G}
in the analysis. This is because
\UpH_{G,k}
does not possess standard symmetries, e.g., it is
not
a normal matrix etc.
To our knowledge, this is the first rapid mixing result obtained in terms of norms of the
k
-non-backtracking
matrix
\UpH_{G,k}
.

Theorem

1.1
follows easily from
Theorem

1.2
. Specifically, it suffices to use the fact that
for large
N
we essentially have
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}\leq\upd_{k}
(see
Proposition

14.3
for
the exact relation) and note that the function
{\lambda_{c}}(z)
is decreasing in
z
.

We further show that the above mixing bound in terms of
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}
is unlikely to
be improved.

Theorem 1.3
.

Unless
{\rm NP}={\rm RP}
, for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
{\Delta}\geq 3
,
N,k\geq 1
and
\uprho>1
,
the following is true:

For any
\lambda>(1+\varepsilon){\lambda_{c}}(\lceil\uprho\rceil)
, there is no FPRAS for estimating the partition function
of the Hard-core model for graphs
G
of maximum degree at most
{\Delta}
such that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.

For any
\lambda<(1-\varepsilon){\lambda_{c}}(\uprho)
, there is an FPRAS for estimating the partition function of the Hard-core model
for graphs
G
of maximum degree at most
{\Delta}
such that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.

The results in
[
39
,
20
]
that we utilise to show
Theorem

1.3
, require the argument
of
{\lambda_{c}}(\cdot)
to be an integer.
For this reason, we use
{\lambda_{c}}(\lceil\uprho\rceil)
for the lower bound in the theorem above.

1.1.
Further results for the Ising model

Using the formalism in (
1.1
), the Ising model corresponds to taking
\beta=\gamma
. Usually, we refer to the
parameters
\beta
as the
inverse temperature
and
\lambda
as the
external field
.
Recall that when
\beta>1
, the Ising model is called
ferromagnetic
, while for

\beta<1
, it is called
antiferromagnetic
.

For integer
k\geq 2
, it is well-known that the Ising model on the infinite
k
-ary tree, with external field
\lambda>0

and inverse temperature
\beta\geq 0
, exhibits uniqueness for

\displaystyle\frac{k-1}{k+1}<\beta<\frac{k+1}{k-1}\kern 5.0pt.

For
z>1
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, we let the interval

(1.2)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(z,\delta)=\textstyle\left[\frac{z-1+\delta}{z+1-\delta},\frac{z+1-\delta}{z-1+\delta}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

We obtain the following result for the Ising model.

Theorem 1.4
.

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
{\Delta}>1
,
k\geq 1
,
\upd_{k}>1
and
\lambda>0

consider graph
G=(V,E)
of maximum degree
{\Delta}
such that the radius-
k
connective constant is
\upd_{k}
.
Let
\mu
be the Ising model on
G
with external field
\lambda
and inverse temperature
\beta\in{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(\upd_{k},\varepsilon)
.

There is a constant
C={C({\Delta},\upd_{k},\varepsilon)}
such that the mixing time of Glauber dynamics
on
\mu
is at most
Cn\log n
.

It is not hard to obtain an FPTAS for the Ising model using parameterizations in
terms of the connective constant, i.e., like the Hard-core model in

[
36
,
35
]
.
This readily implies
O(n\log n)
mixing for Glauber dynamics on amenable graphs.
A related result in this area (and one of the best understood cases) is the Ising model on
the
r
-dimensional square lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
,
for
r>0
. Optimum bounds for the mixing time of Glauber dynamics for the Ising on
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
have been known since the 1990s from
the influential work of Martinelli and Olivieri in
[
31
,
32
]
. These results are
better than those one obtains with the connective constant.

The mixing time of Glauber dynamics for the Ising model on
G(n,d/n)
is also well-studied.
It is known that Glauber dynamics on typical instances of this distribution is rapidly mixing for any

\beta\in{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(\upd,\varepsilon)
and any
\varepsilon>0
. The ferromagnetic case follows from the
work of Mossel and Sly in
[
33
]
. The bounds for the antiferromagnetic Ising are implied by the work of
Efthymiou, Hayes, Štefankovič and Vigoda in
[
18
]
, while explicit bounds appear in
[
19
]
.

As opposed to the previous works which focus on special families of graphs, our endeavour here is to obtain
optimum mixing bounds
without
any assumptions about the underlying graph, i.e., we don’t necessarily assume that
the underlying graph is amenable or a typical instance of
G(n,d/n)
.

As in the case of
Theorem

1.1
, we obtain
Theorem

1.4
by establishing
optimum mixing bounds expressed in terms of
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}
. In particular, we establish the following result.

Theorem 1.5
.

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\lambda>0
,
\uprho>1
and integers
N>0
,
{\Delta}>1
,
consider graph
G=(V,E)
of maximum degree
{\Delta}
such that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.
Let
\mu
be the Ising model on
G
with external field
\lambda
and inverse temperature
\beta\in{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}({\uprho},\varepsilon)
.

There is a constant
C={C({\Delta},{\uprho},\varepsilon)}
such that the mixing time of Glauber dynamics
on
\mu
is at most
Cn\log n
.

There is no hardness region for the ferromagnetic Ising model. This is due to the algorithm of
Jerrum and Sinclair in
[
21
]
. However, we get the following hardness results for the
antiferromagnetic
Ising model.

Theorem 1.6
.

Unless
{\rm NP}={\rm RP}
, for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
{\Delta}\geq 3
,
N,k\geq 1
and
\uprho>1

the following is true:

For any
0<\beta\leq\frac{\uprho-1+\varepsilon}{\uprho+1-\varepsilon}
and
\lambda
such that
we are not in the tree uniqueness region, there is no FPRAS for estimating
the partition function of the antiferromagnetic Ising model
for graphs
G
of maximum degree at most
{\Delta}
such that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.

For any
\frac{\uprho-1+\varepsilon}{\uprho+1-\varepsilon}\leq\beta\leq 1
and any
\lambda>0
,
there is an FPRAS for estimating the partition function of the antiferromagnetic Ising model
for graphs
G
of maximum degree at most
{\Delta}
such that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.

1.2.
Results with backtracking walks

We explore the direction of using
backtracking
walks to establish rapid mixing bounds, i.e.,
rather than the
k
-non-backtracking ones. This approach naturally formalises in terms of the
adjacency matrix

{\UpA}_{G}
.
Recall that
{\UpA}_{G}
is a 0/1 matrix indexed by the vertices of
G
such that for any
u,w
, we have

\displaystyle{\UpA}_{G}(u,w)=\mathds{1}\{\textrm{$u,w$ are adjacent in $G$}\}\kern 5.0pt.

The rapid mixing bounds we obtain are expressed in terms of
\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}
,
which is equal to the
spectral radius
of the matrix.
Utilising matrix
{\UpA}_{G}
and its norms to obtain rapid mixing bounds has been considered before in the literature. The seminal work
of Hayes in
[
23
]
first introduces this approach by considering
\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}
. Subsequently, further extensions
were introduced in
[
14
,
24
]
.

Compared to
\UpH_{G,k}
, matrix
{\UpA}_{G}
is very well-behaved, e.g., it is symmetric, irreducible, etc. This nice behaviour of

{\UpA}_{G}
gives rise to a more elegant analysis than what we have with
\UpH_{G,k}
.
In that regard, we obtain the following result for the Hard-core model.

Theorem 1.7
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, for
{\Delta},{\varrho}>1
, consider graph
G=(V,E)

of maximum degree
{\Delta}
such that
\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}={\varrho}
.
Also, let
\mu
be the Hard-core model on
G
with fugacity
\lambda\leq(1-\varepsilon){\lambda_{c}}({\varrho})
.

There is a constant
C={C({\Delta},{\varrho},\varepsilon)}
such that the mixing time of Glauber dynamics on
\mu

is at most
Cn\log n
.

The maximum degree bounds that apply in our setting are those from
[
3
,
11
]
.

Theorem

1.7
improves on these bounds when
{\rho}({\UpA}_{G})<{\Delta}-1
.

Table

1
shows some notable families of graphs where
Theorem

1.7

gives significant improvements over the maximum degree bounds.

Theorem

1.7
also improves on the results in
[
23
]
, which establishes optimum mixing for the Hard-core model with fugacity
\lambda<\frac{1}{{\varrho}}
. The bound
we obtain here is, roughly,
\lambda<\frac{e}{{\varrho}}
. That is, we get an improvement of factor
e

for the bounded degree graphs.
Unlike our approach that utilises the Spectral Independence method, the results in
[
14
,
23
,
24
]

rely on the
path coupling
technique by Bubley and Dyer in
[
6
]
.

-degenarate

Bounds on the spectral radius of the Adjacency matrix, from
[

,

]

As far as the Ising model and the adjacency matrix are concerned, we obtain the following
result.

Theorem 1.8
.

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\lambda>0
,
{\varrho}>1
and integer
{\Delta}>1
,
consider graph
G=(V,E)
of maximum degree
{\Delta}
such that
\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}={\varrho}
.
Let
\mu
be the Ising model on
G
with external field
\lambda
and inverse temperature
\beta\in{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}({\varrho},\varepsilon)
.

There is a constant
C={C({\Delta},{\varrho},\varepsilon)}
such that the mixing time of Glauber dynamics
on
\mu
is at most
Cn\log n
.

The results we obtain in
Theorem

1.8
for the Ising model match those in
[
23
]
.

Notation

Consider graph
G=(V,E)
, we a Gibbs distribution
\mu_{G}
on

\{\pm 1\}^{V}
.
For
\Lambda\subset V
, we let
\mu_{\Lambda}
denote the marginal of
\mu
at set
\Lambda
.
We also let
\mu_{G}(\cdot\ |\ M,\sigma)
and
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{G}
denote the distribution
\mu
conditional on
the configuration at
M\subset V
being
\sigma
.
Sometimes, we use the term “pinning” to refer to
(M,\sigma)
. Also, we interpret the conditional
marginal
\mu_{\Lambda}(\cdot\ |\ M,\sigma)
in the natural way. Similarly, for
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{\Lambda}
.

For vertex
w\in V
, we let
N_{G}(w)
be the set of vertices which are adjacent to
w
in
G
.

Figure 1.
Initial graph
G

Figure 2.
T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)

2.
Approach

We utilise the Spectral Independence method to show our rapid mixing results.
Central to this method is the notion of the
pairwise influence matrix

{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.
Let us briefly visit this notion.

Let graph
G=(V,E)
and a Gibbs distribution
\mu
on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
.
For
\Lambda\subset V
and a configuration
\tau
at
\Lambda
,
the
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
is a matrix indexed by the vertices in

V\setminus\Lambda
such that for any
w,u\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

(2.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,+1))-\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,-1))\kern 5.0pt.

The Gibbs marginal
\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,+1))
indicates the probability that vertex
u
gets
+1
,
conditional on the configuration at
\Lambda
being
\tau
and the configuration at
w
being
+1
.
We have the analogous for the marginal
\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,-1))
.

For the cases we consider here if the maximum eigenvalue of
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
is bounded,
then the corresponding Glauber dynamics on
\mu
has

O(n\log n)
mixing time. This follows from
[
11
]
.
The rapid mixing results in
Section

1
are obtained by establishing such bounds on
the maximum eigenvalue of
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. In particular, we establish that the spectral radius

{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})
is
O(1)
.

Before showing how we obtain such bounds for
{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})
, let us visit
the notion of the tree of self-avoiding walks that we use in the analysis with
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

The
T_{\rm SAW}
construction:

It is a well-known fact that each entry
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)
can
be expressed in terms of a topological construction called the
tree of self-avoiding walks

together with weights on the paths of this tree that we call
influences
.
It is worth giving a high-level (hence imprecise) description of this relation. For a formal description,
see
Section

5.6
.

Recall that a walk is
self-avoiding
if it does not repeat vertices. For vertex
w
in
G
, we
define
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
, the tree of self-avoiding walks starting from
w
, as follows: Consider
the set of walks
v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
in graph
G
that emanates from vertex
w
, i.e.,

v_{0}=w
, while one of the following two holds

(1)

v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
is a self-avoiding walk,

(2)

v_{0},\ldots,v_{r-1}
is a self-avoiding walk, while there is
j\leq r-3
such that
v_{r}=v_{j}
.

Each of these walks corresponds to a vertex in
T
. Two vertices in this tree are adjacent if the
corresponding walks are adjacent, i.e., one extends the other by one vertex. The vertex
z
in

T
corresponding to walk
v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
in
G
is called a
copy
of vertex
v_{r}

in tree
T
.

Fig.

2
shows an application of the above construction on the graph shown in

Fig.

2
. The tree of self-avoiding walks in this example starts from vertex
w
.
The vertices with label
v
in the tree correspond to the copies of vertex
v
of the initial
graph
G
, e.g., all the highlighted vertices in
Fig.

2
are copies of vertex
u
.

Each path in
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
is associated with a real number that we call
influence
.
The influences depend on the parameters of the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}
.
For the path of length
1
, which corresponds to edge
e
in
T
, we let
\upalpha(e)
denote
its influence. For a path
P
of length
>1
, the influence
\upalpha(P)
is given by

\displaystyle\upalpha(P)

\displaystyle=\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}\upalpha(e)\kern 5.0pt,

i.e.,
\upalpha(P)
is equal to the product of influences of the edges in this path
2
2

2

In the
related literature, influences are defined w.r.t. the vertices of the tree, not the edges. Our formalism
here is equivalent.
.

The entry
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)
can be expressed
as a sum of influences over paths in
T
, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{P}\upalpha(P)\kern 5.0pt,

where
P
varies over the paths from the root to the copies of vertex
u
in
T
.
The
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction is quite helpful for the analysis as it allows us to compute
the entries of
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
recursively.

Bounds from matrix norms for
{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})
:

We use matrix norms
3
3

3

Unless otherwise specified, the norm
\|\UpM\|_{p}
for matrix
\UpM
corresponds to the induced
\ell_{p}
to
\ell_{p}
matrix norm, i.e.,

\|M\|_{P}=\max_{\|{\bf x}\|_{p}=1}\|\UpM{\bf x}\|_{p}
.
to bound the spectral radius
{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})
.

Let us start by considering the results for the Hard-core model, expressed in terms of the adjacency matrix
{\UpA}_{G}
.
From standard linear algebra, we have

\displaystyle{\rho}\left({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\|\UpD^{-1}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\cdot\UpD\|_{\infty}\kern 5.0pt,

for any non-singular matrix
\UpD
such that
\UpD
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
are conformable
for multiplication.

Choosing
\UpD
to be the identity matrix, one recovers the norm used to obtain the max-degree
bounds in
[
10
]
. Here, we make a non-trivial choice for this matrix. We set
\UpD

to be diagonal, such that
\UpD(v,v)=\left(\upphi_{1}(v)\right)^{1/s}
, with
\upphi_{1}
being the
principal eigenvector of
{\UpA}_{G}
. The quantity
s
is a parameter of the potential function
we use in the analysis. Typically, we have
s>1
.

Since we assume that
G
is connected, matrix
{\UpA}_{G}
is
irreducible
.
The Perron-Frobenius theorem implies that all entries in
\upphi_{1}
are strictly positive;
hence,
\UpD
is non-singular.

The estimation of
\|\UpD^{-1}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\cdot\UpD\|_{\infty}
is carried
out by exploiting the
T_{\rm SAW}
-constructions for
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. Specifically, for each
vertex
w
of graph
G
, we estimate the weighted influence of the root of
T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
to the
vertices at each level
h
of the tree. Note that the influences are weighted according to the entries of matrix
\UpD
.
To illustrate this, consider the example in
Fig.

3
. Instead of just calculating the total influence of the
root to the vertices at level
4
in
T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
, in this example, we have the extra weight

\frac{\UpD(u,u)}{\UpD(w,w)}
on the influence from the root to each copy of vertex
u
at
level
4
in the tree.
This calculation gives rise to some natural tree recursions.

Utilising potential functions, we subsequently obtain

\displaystyle\|\UpD^{-1}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\cdot\UpD\|_{\infty}\leq C\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\left(\delta^{\ell}\cdot\|{\UpA}^{\ell}_{G}\|_{2}\right)^{1/s}\kern 5.0pt,

where
C>0
is a bounded number, while
\delta
depends on the parameters of
the Gibbs distribution.
Specifically, for any
\lambda<{\lambda_{c}}(\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2})
, we show that
\delta\leq\frac{1-\epsilon}{\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}}
,
where
\epsilon
increases with the distance of
\lambda
from
{\lambda_{c}}(\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2})
.
For such
C,\delta
and
s
, we obtain that
{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})
is bounded.

We would like to use a similar idea for our results with
\UpH_{G,k}
. It turns out that the weighting we
need to use in this case is more involved. As illustrated in the example for
{\UpA}_{G}
in

Fig.

3
, all copies of vertex
u
in tree
T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
get the same weight

\frac{\UpD(u,u)}{\UpD(w,w)}
. Working with
\UpH_{G,k}
, the weight of each copy of vertex

u
needs to also depend on the path that connects this vertex with the root of the tree. This means
that the various copies of vertex
u
in
T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
get different weights. This somehow precludes
the use of norm
\|\UpD^{-1}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\cdot\UpD\|_{\infty}
(or a similar norm) for our
endeavours.
To this end, rather than using
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, we introduce a new influence matrix to work with that
we call the
extended influence matrix

{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
.

Let us take things from the beginning.
For brevity, set
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
, for some choice
of
k,N\geq 1
, while assume that
\lambda<{\lambda_{c}}(\uprho)
.
We obtain our results for the Hard-core model in terms of
\UpH_{G,k}

by showing

(2.2)

\displaystyle{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\left(\delta^{\ell}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\right)^{1/s}\kern 5.0pt,

where
C>0
is a constant,
s>1
is a parameter of the potential functions we are using in the analysis
and
\delta\leq\frac{1-\epsilon}{\uprho}
, where
\epsilon>0
increases with the distance of

\lambda
from
{\lambda_{c}}(\uprho)
.
For such
C,\delta
and
s
, it is not hard to show that the above inequality implies
that
{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})
is
O(1)
. We have to note, though, that this is not
entirely straightforward as the value of the quantity
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|^{1/\ell}_{2}
varies with
\ell
.

We introduce the notion of the extended influence matrix
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
to establish (
2.2
).
This is a matrix induced by
G
and the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, while it
is indexed by the self-avoiding walks of length
k
in
G
that do not intersect with
\Lambda
.

We think of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
as a matrix that expresses influences with respect to the Gibbs distribution

\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, but in a more refined way than
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.
Intuitively,
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)
accounts for the amount of information that propagates from
vertex
w
to vertex
u
over the paths of graph
G
.
In that respect,
for
P
and
Q
, two self-avoiding walks of length
k
that emanate from
w
and
u
, respectively,
the entry
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}(P,Q)
expresses the amount of information propagating from
w
to
u

over the paths of graph
G
that overlap with
P
and
Q
.

In what follows, we give a more concrete description of the extended influence matrix.

Figure 3.
Weighted Influences

The extended influence matrix
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
:

The definition of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
can get too technical for this high-level exposition.
For this reason, we focus on the simplest case by considering the parameter
k=1
.
For a formal definition of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
, see
Section

9
.

To define the extended influence matrix, we first introduce the notion of the
extension
.
An extension is an operation that we typically apply to a graph
G
or a Gibbs distribution on this graph.
We start with the description of the graph
extension. To keep the description simple, assume that we are given the graph
G
shown in

Fig.

2
. W.l.o.g. assume that there is a total ordering of the vertices in
G
.

Every extension of graph
G
is specified with respect to a self-avoiding walk
P
of length
k

in this graph (here
k=1
). The resulting graph is called the
P
-extension of
G
and is
denoted as
{G}_{P}
. For our example, let
P=wb
. Then,
{G}_{P}
is a new
graph obtained by applying the following operations on
G
:

(1)

remove all the edges that are incident to vertex
w
, apart from the one that connects

w
and
b
,

(2)

for each neighbour
z
of vertex
w
that has been disconnected, insert a new vertex

wz
and connect it only to vertex
z
.

Fig.

5
shows the resulting graph after applying the above operations to graph
G
in

Fig.

2
.

Compared to
G
, graph
{G}_{P}
has an extra vertex which we denote with two letters, i.e.,
vertex
wd
. This new vertex is called
split-vertex
. Split-vertices are important when
we consider the extensions of Gibbs distributions.

Let us now describe the extensions of Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. As in the case
of graph
G
, the extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
is always specified with respect to a
self-avoiding walk
P
of the underlying graph
G
, while walk
P
is not allowed to intersect
with
\Lambda
, the set of pinned vertices.

The
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
is a new Gibbs distribution with underlying graph
{G}_{P}
, i.e.
the
P
-extension of
G
, and it has the same specification as
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, i.e., the same parameters

\beta,\gamma
and
\lambda
.

Letting
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{G_{P}}
be the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
,
the set of pinned vertices
M
corresponds to the union of the vertices in
\Lambda
and the split-vertices of graph
G_{P}
.
The pinning
\sigma
is specified as follows: for any
v\in\Lambda
, we have

\sigma(v)=\tau(v)
, while for each split-vertex
vz
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma(vz)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}+1&&\textrm{if $x>z$}\kern 5.0pt,\\
-1&&\textrm{if $x<z$}\kern 5.0pt,\end{array}\right.

where
x
is the vertex after
v
in path
P
. The comparison between
x
and
z
is with respect to the
total ordering of the vertices in
G
.

Before concluding the description of the extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, we note that the pinning of
the split-vertices is reminiscent of the pinning we have in Weitz’s
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction in
[
42
]
.

For two self-avoiding paths
P,Q
in
G
, each of length
k=1
, which do not intersect with each
other, we also consider the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of graph
G
. We obtain this graph by first getting

{G}_{P}
, i.e., the
P
-extension of
G
, and then we take the
Q
-extension of graph
G_{P}
.
Similarly, we obtain the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. That is,
we first take the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
and then, apply the
Q
-extension to the
resulting Gibbs distribution.

The order in which we consider
P
and
Q
to obtain the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of graph
G
does not matter.
As long as
P
and
Q
do not intersect, one obtains the same graph
{G}_{P,Q}

by either taking the
P
-extension of
{G}_{Q}
or the
Q
-extension of
{G}_{P}
. Similarly
for
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

Figure 4.

wb
-extension

Figure 5.
\{wb,ux\}
-extension

We now proceed with the definition of the extended influence matrix
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
.
Setting
k=1
, matrix
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
is indexed by self-avoiding walks of length
1

(ordered pairs of adjacent vertices) in
V\setminus\Lambda
.

For
P
and
Q
self-avoiding walks in graph
G
, each of length
k=1
, which do not intersect with each other
and do not intersect with
\Lambda
, for matrix
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
, the following holds:
letting
\zeta^{P,Q}
be the
\{P,Q\}
-extension
of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1\ |\ (w,+1))-\zeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1\ |\ (w,-1))\kern 5.0pt,

where
w
and
u
are starting vertices of walks
P,Q
, respectively.

Letting
{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}
the influence matrix of
\zeta^{P,Q}
, the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
,
the above implies that

(2.6)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}(w,u)\kern 5.0pt,

where, as mentioned earlier,
w
and
u
are the starting vertices of walks
P,Q
, respectively.

The above definition implies that each entry of
{\mathcal{L}}
is a standard influence, i.e., in
the sense of the influences we have with the entries in the pairwise influence matrix. This allows us to, e.g., utilise the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction in the analysis with

{\mathcal{L}}
and exploit nice algebraic properties that emerge for this matrix.

Connections between
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
with
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
:

It is interesting to compare the entries of matrix
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
with those in
{\mathcal{I}}={\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

Consider a Gibbs distribution
\mu_{G}
on the graph
G
shown in
Fig.

2
. As before, consider

k=1
, while for simplicity, let
\Lambda=\emptyset
. Consider walks
P=wb
and
Q=ux
in
G
.
It is natural to compare the entry of the extended influence matrix
{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)
with entry

{\mathcal{I}}(w,u)
. Note that the entry in
{\mathcal{I}}
is specified by the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively.

Consider the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction for
{\mathcal{I}}(w,u)
shown in
Fig.

2
. Then,
as argued earlier,
{\mathcal{I}}(w,u)
corresponds to the sum of influences over the paths from the root to the copies
of vertex
u
in this tree, i.e., the highlighted vertices.

Recalling that
P=wb
and
Q=ux
, the entry
{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)
can be described in terms
of the same tree too. It is
approximately equal
to the sum of influences over the paths from the
root to the copies of
u
, with the
extra constraint
that the second vertex of each path
is a copy of vertex
b
, while the vertex prior to the last one is a copy of vertex
x
.
This corresponds to the influence from the root to the highlighted vertex in the subtree encircled by the dotted line in
Fig.

2
.

Using a line of arguments similar to what we describe above, we show that there exists a constant
C_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{I}}\|_{2}\leq C_{0}\cdot\||{\mathcal{L}}|\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

where
|{\mathcal{L}}|
is the matrix with entries
|{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)|
.

From the above, we get (
2.2
) by showing that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0

such that

\displaystyle\||{\mathcal{L}}|\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\left(\delta^{\ell}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\right)^{1/s}\kern 5.0pt.

where the quantities
\delta,s
were specified when we introduce (
2.2
).

Perturbed influences in
T_{\rm SAW}
:

The analysis we use to calculate norms of
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
is not too different from what we had
for the case of
{\UpA}_{G}
and the influence matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.
Even though we do not use norms like
\|\UpD^{-1}\cdot|{\mathcal{L}}|\cdot\UpD\|_{\infty}
in the analysis,
we set up tree
recursions based on the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction with appropriate weighting of the influences.

A point that requires some care is that the definition of
{\mathcal{L}}
implies that the Gibbs distribution we
consider at each entry
{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)
depends on the choice of
P
and
Q
. Hence, different
entries might consider different Gibbs distributions. This implies that when we calculate the influence of the root
to the vertices at level
h
of the tree of self-avoiding walks, the influence of each path comes from a different
Gibbs distribution. This observation is important in the analysis and could cause problems when we use amortisation
and potential functions.

We circumvent the problem by establishing that the corresponding influences that arise from the different Gibbs
distributions can be viewed as one being a small perturbation of the other. We establish this by utilising a
correlation decay

argument.

3.
Spectral Bounds for
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
from likelihood ratios

Consider tree
T=(V,E)
of maximum degree at most
{\Delta}
, while let vertex
r
be the root.
Let the Gibbs distribution
\mu
on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
be specified as in (
1.1
) with respect to parameters
\beta,\gamma

and
\lambda
.

For
K\subseteq V\setminus\{r\}
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{K}
, let the
ratio of marginals
at the root

{R}^{K,\tau}_{r}
be defined by

(3.1)

\displaystyle{R}^{K,\tau}_{r}=\frac{\mu^{K,\tau}_{r}(+1)}{\mu^{K,\tau}_{r}(-1)}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that
\mu^{K,\tau}_{r}(\cdot)
denotes the marginal of
\mu^{K,\tau}(\cdot)

at the root
r
. The above allows for
{R}^{K,\tau}_{r}=\infty
when

\mu^{K,\tau}_{r}(-1)=0
.

For vertex
u\in V
, we let
T_{u}
be the subtree of
T
that includes
u
and all its descendants.
We always assume that the root of
T_{u}
is vertex
u
.
With a slight abuse of notation, we let
{R}^{K,\tau}_{u}
denote the ratio of marginals at the root for
the subtree
T_{u}
, where the Gibbs distribution is with respect to
T_{u}
, while we impose
pinning
\tau(K\cap T_{u})
.

Suppose that the vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}
are the children of the root
r
, for an integer
d>0
.
It is standard to express
{R}^{K,\tau}_{r}
in terms of
{R}^{K,\tau}_{v_{i}}
’s
by having
{R}^{K,\tau}_{r}=F_{d}({R}^{K,\tau}_{v_{1}},\ldots,{R}^{K,\tau}_{v_{d}})
, where

(3.2)

\displaystyle F_{d}:[0,+\infty]^{d}\to[0,+\infty]

s.t.

\displaystyle(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\mapsto\lambda\prod^{d}_{i=1}\frac{\beta{x}_{i}+1}{{x}_{i}+\gamma}\kern 5.0pt.

To get cleaner results in the analysis, we work with log-ratios of
Gibbs marginals. Let
H_{d}=\log\circ F_{d}\circ\exp
, which means that

(3.3)

\displaystyle H_{d}:[-\infty,+\infty]^{d}\to[-\infty,+\infty]

s.t.

\displaystyle(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\mapsto\log\lambda+\sum^{d}_{i=1}\log\left(\frac{\beta\exp(x_{i})+1}{\exp(x_{i})+\gamma}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

From (
3.2
), it is elementary to verify that
\log{R}^{K,\tau}_{r}=H_{d}(\log{R}^{K,\tau}_{v_{1}},\ldots,\log{R}^{K,\tau}_{v_{d}})
.

We let the function

(3.4)

\displaystyle h:[-\infty,+\infty]\to\mathbb{R}

s.t.

\displaystyle x\mapsto-\frac{(1-\beta\gamma)\cdot\exp(x)}{(\beta\exp(x)+1)(\exp(x)+\gamma)}\kern 5.0pt.

For any
i\in[d]
, we have
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}H_{d}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})=h(x_{i})
,
where recall that set
[d]=\{1,\ldots,d\}
.
Furthermore, let the interval
J_{d}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be defined by

\displaystyle J_{d}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}[\log(\lambda\beta^{d}),\log(\lambda/\gamma^{d})]&&\textrm{if $\beta\gamma<1$}\kern 5.0pt,\\
\\
[\log(\lambda/\gamma^{d}),\log(\lambda\beta^{d})]&&\textrm{if $\beta\gamma>1$}\kern 5.0pt.\end{array}\right.

Standard algebra implies that
J_{d}
contains all the log-ratios for a vertex with
d
children.
Also, let

(3.8)

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\bigcup\nolimits_{1\leq d<{\Delta}}J_{d}\kern 5.0pt.

The set
J
contains all log-ratios in the tree
T
.

3.1.
First attempt

We present the first set of our results, which we use to establish spectral independence. These results rely on the notion of

\delta
-contraction.

Definition 3.1

(
\delta
-contraction)
.

Let
\delta\geq 0
and the integer
{\Delta}\geq 1
. Also, let
\beta,\gamma,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
be
such that
0\leq\beta\leq\gamma
,

\gamma>0
and
\lambda>0
.
The set of functions
\{H_{d}\}_{1\leq d<{\Delta}}
, defined as in (
3.3
)
with respect to the parameters
\beta,\gamma,\lambda
,
exhibits
\delta
-contraction if it satisfies the following condition:

For any
1\leq d<{\Delta}
and any
({\bf y}_{1},\ldots,{\bf y}_{d})\in[-\infty,+\infty]^{d}
, we have

\|\nabla H_{d}({\bf y}_{1},\ldots,{\bf y}_{d})\|_{\infty}\leq\delta.

The
\delta
-contraction condition is equivalent to having
h(z)\leq\delta
, for any
z\in[-\infty,+\infty]
, where

h(z)
is defined in (
3.4
).

(

For matrix
\UpH_{G,k}

3.2.
Second Attempt

We introduce a second set of results by utilising the notion of the potential functions in the analysis.
It is worth mentioning that we use the sharp results for potential functions from
[
35
]
.

Definition 3.5

(
(s,\delta,c)
-potential)
.

Let
s\geq 1
,
\delta,c>0
and let the integer
{\Delta}>1
.
Also, let
\beta,\gamma,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
0\leq\beta\leq\gamma
,

\gamma>0
and
\lambda>0
.

Consider
\{H_{d}\}_{1\leq d<\Delta}

3.3
) with respect to

(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
. The differentiable, strictly increasing function
{\Psi}
, with image
S_{{\Psi}}
, is called
(s,\delta,c)
-potential
if it satisfies the following two conditions:

Contraction:

For
1\leq d<{\Delta}
, for
(\widehat{\bf y}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\bf y}_{d})\in(S_{{\Psi}})^{d}
,
and
{\bf m}=({\bf m}_{1},\ldots,{\bf m}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
we have that

(3.9)

\displaystyle\chi(H_{d}({\bf y}_{1},\ldots,{\bf y}_{d}))\cdot\sum^{d}_{j=1}\frac{|h\left({\bf y}_{j}\right)|}{\chi\left({\bf y}_{j}\right)}\cdot{\bf m}_{j}\leq\delta^{\frac{1}{s}}\cdot\|{\bf m}\|_{s}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\chi={\Psi}^{\prime}
,
{\bf y}_{j}={\Psi}^{-1}(\widehat{\bf y}_{j})
, while
h(\cdot)
is the function defined in (
3.4
).

Boundedness:

We have that

(3.10)

\displaystyle\max_{z,x\in J}\left\{\chi(z)\cdot\frac{|h(x)|}{\chi(x)}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq c\kern 5.0pt.

The notion of the
(s,\delta,c)
-potential function is a generalisation of the
so-called “
(\alpha,c)
-potential function” that is introduced in
[
10
]
. Note that
the notion of
(\alpha,c)
-potential function implies the use of the
\ell_{1}
-norm in the analysis.
The setting we consider here is more general. The condition in (
3.9
)
implies that we are using the
\ell_{r}
-norm, where
r
is the Hölder conjugate of the
parameter
s
in the
(s,\delta,c)
-potential function
4
4

4

r^{-1}+s^{-1}=1
.
.

5.
Preliminaries for the Analysis

5.1.
Glauber dynamics and mixing times.

Suppose that we are given a graph
G=(V,E)
and a Gibbs distribution
\mu
on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
.
We use the discrete-time, (single site)
Glauber dynamics

{\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}}
to approximately sample from
\mu
. Glauber dynamics is a very simple to describe
Markov chain. The state space is the support of
\mu
.
We assume that the chain starts from an arbitrary configuration
X_{0}
. For

t\geq 0
, the transition from the state
X_{t}
to
X_{t+1}
is according to the following steps:

(1)

Choose uniformly at random a vertex
v\in V
.

(2)

For every vertex
w
different than
v
, set
X_{t+1}(w)=X_{t}(w)
.

(3)

Set
X_{t+1}(v)
according to the marginal of
\mu
at
v
, conditional on
the neighbours of
v
having the configuration specified by
X_{t+1}
.

For two distributions
\upnu
and
\hat{\upnu}
on the discrete set
\Omega
,
the
total variation distance
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\upnu-\hat{\upnu}\|_{tv}

\displaystyle=(1/{2})\ {\sum\nolimits_{x\in\Omega}}|\upnu(x)-\hat{\upnu}(x)|\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\UpP
be the transition matrix of an
ergodic
Markov chain
{\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}}
on a finite state space

\Omega
with stationary distribution
\mu
. Recall that
\UpP
is called ergodic if it is irreducible and aperiodic.
For
t\geq 0
and
\sigma\in\Omega
, we let
\UpP^{t}(\sigma,\cdot)
be the distribution of
X_{t}
when
X_{0}=\sigma
. Then, the
mixing time
of
\UpP
is defined by

\displaystyle{\rm T}_{\rm mix}(\UpP)=\min\{t\geq 0\ :\ \forall\sigma\in\Omega\ \ \|\UpP^{t}(\sigma,\cdot)-\mu(\cdot)\|_{tv}\leq 1/4\}\kern 5.0pt.

We use the mixing time as a measure of the rate of convergence to equilibrium for Glauber dynamics.
For the cases we consider here, Glauber dynamics is trivially ergodic.

5.2.
Spectral Independence

In this section, we introduce the basics of the Spectral Independence method. Specifically, we start by formally
introducing the notion of the
pairwise influence matrix

{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. Subsequently, we
present some rapid mixing results we can obtain from the Spectral Independence method.

Influence Matrix:

We have seen the definition of the influence matrix before. However, since it is such an important notion for
our results, we state it once more.

Let graph
G=(V,E)
and a Gibbs distribution
\mu
on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
. For
\Lambda\subset V
and a configuration

\tau
at
\Lambda
, the
pairwise influence matrix

{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
is indexed by the vertices
in
V\setminus\Lambda
such that, for
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

(5.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,+1))-\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,-1))\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,+1))
is the probability of the event that vertex
u
has configuration

+1
, conditional on that the configuration at
\Lambda
is
\tau
and the configuration at
w
is
+1
. We have
the analogous for
\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,-1))
.

When we consider Gibbs distributions with hard constraints, e.g., the Hard-core model, it is possible that for a
vertex
v\notin\Lambda
to have
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{v}(+1)\in\{0,1\}
. For vertices
w,u
such that
at least one of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{w}(+1),\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{u}(+1)
is in
\{0,1\}
, we have

{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)=0
.

In the analysis, we use the following folklore result which is standard to prove (e.g. see
Section

A.1
).

Claim 5.1
.

For graph
G=(V,E)
and a Gibbs distribution
\mu
on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
,
for
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}
, let
\UpM
be the
(V\setminus\Lambda)\times(V\setminus\Lambda)

diagonal matrix such that

\displaystyle\UpM(v,v)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sqrt{\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{v}(+1)\cdot\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{v}(-1)}

\displaystyle\forall v\in V\setminus\Lambda\kern 5.0pt.

Then, for the influence matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
induced by
\mu
, the following is true:
if
\UpM
is non-singular, then matrix
\UpM\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\cdot\UpM^{-1}
is symmetric.

Spectral Independence and Mixing Times:

The main focus of the Spectral Independence method is on the maximum eigenvalue of the influence
matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, denoted as
\upxi_{1}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})
.

Definition 5.2

(Spectral Independence)
.

For a real
\eta>0
, the Gibbs distribution
\mu
on
G=(V,E)
is
\eta
-spectrally
independent, if for every
0\leq k\leq|V|-2
,
\Lambda\subset V
of size
k
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}

we have that
\upxi_{1}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})\leq 1+\eta
.

The notion of
\eta
-spectral independence for
\mu
can be utilised to bound the mixing time of the corresponding
Glauber dynamics.

Theorem 5.3

(
[
3
]
)
.

For
\eta>0
, there is a constant
C\geq 0
such that if
\mu
is an
\eta
-spectrally independent distribution,
then the Glauber dynamics for sampling from
\mu
has mixing time which is at most
Cn^{2+\eta}
.

Note that
Theorem

5.3
is quite general and implies that for bounded
\eta
, the mixing time is
polynomial in
n
. However, this polynomial can be very large. There have been improvements on

Theorem

5.3
since its introduction in
[
3
]
, e.g., see

[
11
,
8
,
9
]
.

We use
Theorem

5.4
, proved in
[
11
]
, to turn our spectral independence results into
optimum mixing ones.
We need to introduce a few useful concepts from
[
11
]
, to formally state the result we are using.

Let graph
G=(V,E)
. For a non-empty set
S\subset V
, let
{\tt H}_{S}
be the graph whose vertices correspond to
the configurations in
\{\pm 1\}^{S}
. Two configurations in
\{\pm 1\}^{S}
are considered adjacent
if and only if they differ at the assignment of a single vertex.
Similarly, any subset
\Omega_{0}\subseteq\{\pm 1\}^{S}
is considered to be connected if the subgraph
induced by
\Omega_{0}
is connected.

A distribution
\mu
on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
is considered to be
totally
connected
if for every nonempty
\Lambda\subset V
and every boundary condition
\tau
at
\Lambda
,
the set of configurations in the support of
\mu(\cdot\ |\ \Lambda,\tau)
is connected.
We remark here that all Gibbs distributions with soft-constraints such as the Ising model are
totally connected in a trivial way. The same holds for the Hard-core model and this follows from standard arguments.

The following result is a part of Theorem 1.9 from
[
11
]
(arxiv-version).

Theorem 5.4

(
[
11
]
)
.

Let the integer
{\Delta}\geq 3
and
b,\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
Consider graph
G=(V,E)
on
n
vertices and maximum degree
{\Delta}
. Also, let
\mu
be a totally connected Gibbs
distribution on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
.

If
\mu
is both
b
-marginally bounded and
\eta
-spectrally independent, then there are constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such
the Glauber dynamics for
\mu
exhibits mixing time

\displaystyle{\rm T}_{\rm mix}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\times\left({{\Delta}}/{b}\right)^{C_{2}\left(\frac{\eta-1}{b^{2}}+1\right)}\times\left(n\log n\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem

5.4
implies
O(n\log n)
mixing, provided that
{\Delta}
,
\eta
and
b^{-1}
are bounded numbers.

5.3.
(Local) Connective Constant:

We start by introducing a few standard notions related to the (standard)
connective constant
.
We consider an infinite, locally finite graph
G
.
Locally finite means that all vertices have finite degree. For a vertex
v
of graph
G
, we denote by
\pi(v,k)
the
number of
self-avoiding walks
of length
k
starting at
v
. Recall that a walk is called self-avoiding if it does not repeat vertices.
We let

\displaystyle c_{k}=\sup\{\pi(v,k)\ |\ v\in V\}\kern 5.0pt.

Definition 5.5

(connective constant)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a locally finite, infinite graph. The connective constant
\upd=\upd(G)
is defined by

\upd=\lim_{k\to\infty}c^{1/k}_{k}.

It is well-known that the above limit always exists.

For our algorithmic applications, we use the notion of
radius-
k
connective constant
.

Definition 5.6
.

For integer
k>0
and graph
G=(V,E)
, the radius-
k
connective constant
\upd_{k}=\upd_{k}(G)
is defined by

\upd_{k}=c^{1/k}_{k}
.

The above definitions imply that
\upd=\lim_{k\to\infty}\upd_{k}
.

The use of infinite graph
G
was only to formally introduce the notion of the connective constant.
From now on, unless otherwise specified, we assume that the graph
G
is always
finite
.

5.4.
The adjacency matrix
{\UpA}_{G}

For graph
G=(V,E)
, the
adjacency matrix

{\UpA}_{G}
is a zero-one,
V\times V
matrix
such that, for any
u,w\in V
, we have

\displaystyle{\UpA}_{G}(w,u)=\mathds{1}\{\textrm{$u,w$ are adjacent in $G$}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that a
walk
in graph
G
is any sequence of vertices
w_{0},\ldots,w_{\ell}

such that each consecutive pair
(w_{i-1},w_{i})
is an edge.
The length of the walk is equal to the number of consecutive pairs
(w_{i-1},w_{i})
.

The adjacency matrix has the property that for any
u,w\in V
and
\ell\geq 1
,
we have

(5.2)

\displaystyle{\UpA}^{\ell}_{G}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\textrm{$\#$ length $\ell$ walks\ from}\ w\ \textrm{to}\ u\kern 5.0pt.

For undirected
G
, matrix
{\UpA}^{\ell}_{G}
is symmetric for any
\ell\geq 0
. Hence,
{\UpA}^{\ell}_{G}
has real eigenvalues, while
the eigenvectors corresponding to distinct eigenvalues are orthogonal with each other.
We denote with
\upphi_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
the eigenvector of
{\UpA}_{G}

that corresponds to the eigenvalue
\upxi_{j}({\UpA}_{G})
.
Unless otherwise specified, we always take
\upphi_{j}
such that
\|\upphi_{j}\|_{2}=1
.

Since
{\UpA}_{G}
is symmetric, the
spectral radius
satisfies that
{\rho}({\UpA}_{G})=\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}
.
Recall that
\|{\UpA}_{G}\|_{2}=\max_{{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}:{\bf x}\neq{\bf 0}}\frac{\|{\UpA}_{G}\cdot{\bf x}\|_{2}}{\|{\bf x}\|_{2}}
.

When
G
is connected, then
{\UpA}_{G}
is irreducible. Then, the Perron-Frobenius Theorem
implies that

(5.3)

\displaystyle{\rho}({\UpA}_{G})

\displaystyle=\upxi_{1}({\UpA}_{G})

and

\displaystyle\upphi_{1}(u)

\displaystyle>0\qquad\forall u\in V\kern 5.0pt.

It is an easy exercise to show that
\sqrt{{\Delta}}\leq{\rho}({\UpA}_{G})\leq{\Delta}
.

Figure 10.
Example graph
G
for
\UpH_{G,k}

5.5.
Matrices on oriented paths & PT-Invariance

For graph
G=(V,E)
and integer
k>0
, we let
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
be the set of self-avoiding walks of length
k
.

For
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
, we let
P^{-1}
denote the walk with the opposite direction. E.g.,
suppose
P=u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{k-1},u_{k}
, then
P^{-1}=u_{k},u_{k-1},\ldots,u_{1},u_{0}
.

In this paper, we work with matrices defined on (subsets of)
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
. Typically, the matrices we consider
are not symmetric. However, they exhibit other symmetries that we utilise in the analysis. The most important one is
the so-called
PT-invariance
, where PT stands for “parity-time”.

Let
L\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
be such that if
P\in L
, then
P^{-1}\in L
, too.
For
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{L}
, let the vector
\check{\bf x}
be such that

(5.4)

\displaystyle\check{\bf x}(P)

\displaystyle={\bf x}(P^{-1})

\displaystyle\forall P\in L\kern 5.0pt.

Also, let
\UpR_{L}
be the involution on
\mathbb{R}^{L}
such that

(5.5)

\displaystyle\UpR_{L}\cdot{\bf x}=\check{\bf x}\kern 5.0pt.

The
L\times L
matrix
\UpZ
is PT-invariant if we have

(5.6)

\displaystyle\UpZ\cdot\UpR_{L}

\displaystyle=\UpR_{L}\cdot\overline{\UpZ}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\overline{\UpZ}
is the matrix transpose of
\UpZ
. The above implies
that
\UpZ\cdot\UpR_{L}
is symmetric, while we also have

(5.7)

\displaystyle\UpZ(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\UpZ(Q^{-1},P^{-1})\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\forall P,Q\in L\kern 5.0pt.

5.5.1.
The
k
-non-backtracking matrix
\UpH_{G,k}

For graph
G=(V,E)
and integer
k>0
, recall that
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
is the set of self-avoiding walks of length
k
.

The (order)
k
-non-backtracking matrix
\UpH_{G,k}
is an
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
, zero-one matrix
such that for any
P=x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}
and
Q=z_{0},\ldots,z_{k}
in
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
, we have

(5.8)

\displaystyle\UpH_{G,k}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}\{x_{0}\neq z_{k}\}\times\prod\nolimits_{0\leq i<k}\mathds{1}\{z_{i}=x_{i+1}\}\kern 5.0pt.

That is,
\UpH_{G,k}(P,Q)
is equal to
1
if the walk
x_{0},\ldots,x_{k},z_{k}
does not repeat vertices, i.e.,
it is self-avoiding. Otherwise,
\UpH_{G,k}(P,Q)
is zero.
When
k=1
,
\UpH_{G,k}
to the well-known
Hashimoto non-backtracking matrix

[
22
]
.

For the graph
G
in
Fig.

10
, consider
\UpH_{G,3}
.
Then, for
P=abx
,
Q=bxd
and
R=xde

we have
\UpH_{G,3}(P,Q)=1
, also, we have
\UpH_{G,3}(Q,R)=1
, while note that we have

\UpH_{G,3}(P,R)=0
.

For any integer
r>k
, we say that walk
R=w_{0},\ldots,w_{r}
is a
k
-non-backtracking
walk
in
G
, if every
(k+2)
-tuple of consecutive vertices
w_{i},\ldots,w_{i+k+1}

in
R
is a self-avoiding walk.
Furthermore, for any integer
\ell>0
and any
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
, we have

(5.9)

\displaystyle\UpH_{G,k}^{\ell}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\textrm{ $\#$ $k$-non-backtracking walks from $P$ to $Q$ having length $\ell$}\kern 5.0pt.

The above entry counts all the
k
-non-backtracking walks
R=w_{0},\ldots,w_{\ell+k}
such that the first
k+1
vertices
in
R
correspond to walk
P
, while the last
k+1
vertices correspond to walk
Q
.
Note that
R
is a walk with
\ell+k+1
vertices.

Norms & Singular Values of
\UpH_{G,k}
:

In the general case,
\UpH_{G,k}
is not normal, i.e.,
\UpH_{G,k}\cdot\overline{\UpH}_{G,k}\neq\overline{\UpH}_{G,k}\cdot{\UpH_{G,k}}
.

It is elementary to show that it is PT-invariant. More specifically, for any integer
\ell\geq 0
, we have

(5.10)

\displaystyle\UpH_{G,k}^{\ell}\cdot\UpR

\displaystyle=\UpR\cdot\overline{\UpH}^{\ell}_{G,k}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\UpR
is the involution on
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}
defined as in (
5.5
).
The PT-invariance emerges from the simple observation that for any two
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
,
the number of
k
-non-backtracking walks of length
\ell
from
P
to
Q
in graph
G
is the
same as the number of
k
-non-backtracking walks of length
\ell
from
Q^{-1}
to
P^{-1}
.

Notably (
5.10
) implies that
matrix
\UpH_{G,k}^{\ell}\cdot\UpR
is symmetric, i.e., the r.h.s. of (
5.10
) is
the transpose of the l.h.s. since we have
\overline{\UpR}={\UpR}
.

For integers
k,\ell>0
, let
\upsigma_{k,\ell}
be the maximum
singular value
of matrix

(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}
, i.e.,
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}=\upsigma_{k,\ell}
.
It is standard that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}=\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\cdot\UpR\|_{2}=\upsigma_{k,\ell}\kern 5.0pt,

Since
(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\cdot\UpR
is symmetric,

\sigma_{k,\ell}
is equal to the spectral radius of matrix
(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\cdot\UpR
.

For the analysis, we use of the following two results about
\upsigma_{k,\ell}
, which are standard to prove.

Claim 5.7
.

For integers
\ell,k>0
and any
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
, we have

\UpH_{G,k}^{\ell}(P,Q)\leq\sigma_{k,\ell}
.

The proof of
Claim

5.7
appears in
Section

A.2
.

Claim 5.8
.

For any integers
k,N>0
and
z\in(0,1)
, there is
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(N,z,{\Delta})
such that
for any integer
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
, we have

\left(\upsigma_{k,\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}\leq(1+z)(\upsigma_{k,N})^{\frac{1}{N}}\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of
Claim

5.8
appears in
Section

A.3
.

5.6.
T_{\rm SAW}
Construction for
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}

Figure 11.
Initial graph
G

Figure 12.
T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)

The exposition here relies on results from
[
3
,
10
]
.
Let graph
G=(V,E)
and a Gibbs distribution
\mu_{G}
on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
.
Assume w.l.o.g. that there is a
total ordering
of the vertices in
G
.

We start by introducing the notion of the
tree of self-avoiding walks
in
G
.
For each vertex
w
in
G
, we define
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
, the tree of self-avoiding walks
starting from
w
, as follows:
Consider the set consisting of every walk
v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
in graph
G
that emanates
from vertex
w
, i.e.,
v_{0}=w
, while one of the following two holds

(1)

v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
is a self-avoiding walk,

(2)

v_{0},\ldots,v_{r-1}
is a self-avoiding walk, while there is
j\leq r-3
such that
v_{r}=v_{j}
.

Each of these walks corresponds to a vertex in
T
. Two vertices in
T
are adjacent
if the corresponding walks are adjacent, i.e., one walk extends the other by one vertex.

The vertex in
T
that corresponds to the walk
v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
,
is called “copy of vertex
v_{r}
”, i.e.,
v_{r}
is the last vertex in the path.
For each vertex
v
in
G
, we let
{\tt A}(v)
be the set of its copies in
T
.
Note that vertex
v
may more than one copies in
T
.

For an example of the above construction, consider the graph
G
in
Fig.

12
.
In
Fig.

12
, we have the tree of self-avoiding walks that starts from vertex
w
in
G
.
The vertices with label
v
in the tree correspond to the copies of vertex
v
of the initial
graph
G
, e.g. the root if a copy of vertex
w
.

For
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm\}^{\Lambda}
, let
the influence matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
induced by
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.
We describe how the entry

{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)
can be expressed using an appropriately defined
spin-system on
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
.

Let
\mu_{T}
be a Gibbs distribution on
T
which has the same specification as
\mu_{G}
.
Each
z\in{\tt A}(x)
in the tree
T
, such
that
x\in\Lambda
, is pinned to configuration
\tau(x)
. Furthermore, if
vertex
z
in
T
corresponds to walk
v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell}
in
G
such that

v_{\ell}=v_{j}
, for
0\leq j\leq\ell-3
, then we set a pinning at vertex
z
, as well. This pinning
depends on the total ordering of the vertices. Particularly, we set at
z

(1)

-1
if
v_{j+1}>v_{\ell-1}
,

(2)

+1
otherwise.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma(G,\Lambda)
be the set of vertices in
T
which have been pinned
in the above construction, while let
\sigma=\sigma(G,\tau)
be the pinning at
\Gamma
.

For each edge
e
in
T
, we specify weight
\upalpha(e)
as follows: letting
e=\{x,z\}
be such
that
x
is the parent of
z
in
T
, we set

\displaystyle\upalpha(e)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}0&&\textrm{if there is a pinning at either $x$ or $z$},\\
\textstyle h\left(\log{R}^{\Gamma,\sigma}_{z}\right)&&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

The function
h(\cdot)
is from (
3.4
), while
{R}^{\Gamma,\sigma}_{z}

is a ratio of Gibbs marginals at
z
(see definitions in Section
3
).
Then, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 5.9

(
[
3
,
10
]
)
.

For every
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
we have that

(5.14)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{P}\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}\upalpha(e)\kern 5.0pt,

where
P
varies over all paths from the root of
T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
to the set of vertices in
{\tt A}(u)
.

6.
Proof of
Theorem

3.2
- SI with
\delta
-contraction &
{\UpA}_{G}

Recalling that
\delta=(1-\varepsilon)/{\varrho}
, let
{{\UpB}}
be the
V\times V
matrix defined by

(6.1)

\displaystyle{{\UpB}}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{0\leq\ell\leq n}\left(\delta\cdot{\UpA}_{G}\right)^{\ell}\kern 5.0pt.

Since the adjacency matrix
{\UpA}_{G}
is symmetric,
{{\UpB}}
is symmetric, as well. We have

\displaystyle{\rho}\left({{\UpB}}\right)

\displaystyle=\|{{\UpB}}\|_{2}=\|\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}(\delta\cdot{\UpA}_{G})^{\ell}\|_{2}\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\delta^{\ell}\cdot\|({\UpA}_{G})^{\ell}\|_{2}\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}(1-\varepsilon)^{\ell}=\varepsilon^{-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Let
{{\UpB}}_{V\setminus\Lambda}
be the principal submatrix of
{{\UpB}}
, which is obtained by
removing rows and columns that correspond to the vertices in
\Lambda
.
Cauchy’s interlacing theorem, e.g., see
[
26
]
, implies that

(6.2)

\displaystyle{\rho}\left({{\UpB}}_{V\setminus\Lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{\rho}\left({{\UpB}}\right)\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem

3.2
follows by showing that
{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})\leq{\rho}\left({{\UpB}}_{V\setminus\Lambda}\right)
.
To this end, it suffices to show

(6.3)

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)|

\displaystyle\leq{{\UpB}}_{V\setminus\Lambda}(w,u)

\displaystyle\forall u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda\kern 5.0pt.

To see why the above implies
{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})\leq{\rho}\left({{\UpB}}_{V\setminus\Lambda}\right)
see
Lemma

B.1
in the Appendix,

It is immediate that for any
u\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have
|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(u,u)|=1
.
Also, we have
{{\UpB}}(u,u)\geq 1
, as the summand in (
6.1
) for
\ell=0
corresponds to
the identity matrix. This proves that (
6.3
) is true when
u=w
.

Fix
w,u\in V\setminus\Lambda
such that
w\neq u
. Let
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
and let
\{\upalpha(e)\}
be the weights
on the edges of
T
specified as in (
5.6
) with respect to
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.
Proposition

5.9
implies

(6.4)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\sum\nolimits_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)}\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}\upalpha(e)\kern 5.0pt,

where set
{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)
consists of the paths of length
\ell
from the root of
T
to
{\tt A}(u)
.

The
\delta
-contraction assumption implies that
|\upalpha(e)|\leq\delta
,
for each
e\in T
. Then,
from (
6.4
), we have

(6.5)

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}|{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)|\cdot\delta^{\ell}\kern 5.0pt.

Let
{\rm SAW}_{\ell}(w,u)
be the set of walks of length
\ell
from
w
to
u
, in graph
G
, that correspond to the elements in

{\tt A}(u)
. Let
{\rm Walks}_{\ell}(w,u)
be the set of walks of length
\ell
from
w
to
u
, in graph
G
. We have

{\rm SAW}_{\ell}(w,u)\subseteq{\rm Walks}_{\ell}(w,u)

i.e., since each element in
{\rm SAW}_{\ell}(w,u)
is also a walk in graph
G
from
w
to
u
. We have

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)|

\displaystyle=|{\rm SAW}_{\ell}(w,u)|\leq|{\rm Walks}_{\ell}(w,u)|={\UpA}^{\ell}_{G}(w,u)\kern 5.0pt.

The last equality is from (
5.2
).
Plugging the above bound into (
6.5
), we get

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\delta^{\ell}\cdot{\UpA}^{\ell}_{G}(w,u)={{\UpB}}_{V\setminus\Lambda}(w,u)\kern 5.0pt.

Hence, (
6.3
) is also true when
w\neq u
.

Theorem

3.2
follows.
\square

7.
Proof of
Theorem

3.6
- SI with Potentials &
{\UpA}_{G}

We let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the set of ordered pairs of adjacent vertices in
V
. Consider the zero-one

{\mathcal{E}}\times V
matrix
\UpC
such that, for any
wx\in{\mathcal{E}}

and any
u\in V
, we have

(7.1)

\displaystyle\UpC(wx,u)

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}\{x=u\}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
s\geq 1
,
\delta,c>0
and the integer
{\Delta}>1
. Also, let

\beta,\gamma,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
\gamma>0
,
0\leq\beta\leq\gamma
and
\lambda>0
.

Consider graph
G=(V,E)
of maximum degree
{\Delta}
, while let

\mu
the Gibbs distribution on
G
specified by the parameters
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
.
Suppose there is a
(s,\delta,c)
-potential function
{\Psi}
with respect to
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
.

For any
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
, for any diagonal, non-negative,
non-singular
\UpD
, such that
\UpD
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
are conformable for multiplication
the following is true: for any
w\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

(7.2)

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}|(\UpD^{-1}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\cdot\UpD)(w,u)|

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{c}{\UpD(w,w)}\left(\sum\nolimits_{z\in N(w)\setminus\Lambda}\UpD(z,z)+\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2}\left(\delta^{\ell-1}\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\left(\UpC\cdot{\UpA}^{\ell-1}_{G}\right)(wz,u)\cdot\UpD^{s}(u,u)\right)^{{1}/{s}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
{\UpA}_{G}
is the adjacency matrix of
G
and matrix
\UpC
is defined in (
7.1
).

The proof of
Theorem

7.1
appears in
Section

7.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.6
.

Let
\upphi_{1}
be the principal eigenvector of
{\UpA}_{G}
, while let
\upxi_{1}
be the
corresponding eigenvalue. Since
G
is assumed to be connected, matrix
{\UpA}_{G}
is
non-negative and irreducible. The Perron-Frobenius theorem implies

(7.3)

\displaystyle{\varrho}

\displaystyle=\upxi_{1}

and

\displaystyle\upphi_{1}(u)>0\qquad\forall u\in V\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\Upxi
be the
(V\setminus\Lambda)\times(V\setminus\Lambda)
diagonal matrix such
that for any
u\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

(7.4)

\displaystyle\Upxi(u,u)=\upphi_{1}(u)\kern 5.0pt.

Equation

7.3
implies that
\Upxi
is
non-singular
.

We prove
Theorem

3.6
by applying
Theorem

7.1
, where

\UpD=\Upxi^{1/s}
. Specifically, we show that

(7.5)

\displaystyle\|\Upxi^{-1/s}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\cdot\Upxi^{1/s}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 1+c\cdot{\Delta}^{1-(1/s)}\cdot{\varrho}^{{1}/{s}}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}(\delta\cdot{\varrho})^{{\ell}/{s}}\kern 5.0pt.

Then,
Theorem

3.6
follows by substituting
c=\frac{\zeta}{{\varrho}}
and

\delta=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{{\varrho}}
and noting that the norm on the l.h.s. is an upper bound for

{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})
. It remains to show that (
7.5
)
is true.

For
w\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we let
\mathbold{q}(w)
be equal to the absolute row sum for the row of matrix

\Upxi^{-1/s}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\cdot\Upxi^{1/s}
that corresponds to vertex
w
.

Theorem

7.1
and (
7.4
) imply

(7.6)

\displaystyle\mathbold{q}(w)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{c}{\left(\upphi_{1}(w)\right)^{1/s}}\cdot\left(\sum\nolimits_{z\in N(w)}\left(\upphi_{1}(z)\right)^{1/s}+\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2}\left(\delta^{\ell-1}\cdot\left(\UpC\cdot{\UpA}^{\ell-1}_{G}\cdot\upphi_{1}\right)(wz)\right)^{1/s}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Recalling that
\upphi_{1}
is the principal eigenvector of
{\UpA}_{G}
, (
7.3
) implies

(7.7)

\displaystyle\UpC\cdot{\UpA}^{\ell-1}_{G}\cdot\upphi_{1}

\displaystyle={\varrho}^{\ell-1}\cdot\UpC\cdot\upphi_{1}\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging the above into (
7.6
) and rearranging, we get

(7.8)

\displaystyle\mathbold{q}(w)

\displaystyle=1+c\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 1}\left(\delta\cdot{\varrho}\right)^{{(\ell-1)}/s}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{z\in N_{G}(w)}\left({\textstyle\frac{\upphi_{1}(z)}{\upphi_{1}(w)}}\right)^{1/s}\kern 5.0pt.

We need to bound the rightmost sum in the equation above.
Letting
d=|N_{G}(w)|
and applying Hölder’s inequality, we get

(7.9)

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{z\in N_{G}(w)}\left({\textstyle\frac{\upphi_{1}(z)}{\upphi_{1}(w)}}\right)^{1/s}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum\nolimits_{z\in N_{G}(w)}{\textstyle\frac{\upphi_{1}(z)}{\upphi_{1}(w)}}\right)^{1/s}d^{1-(1/s)}\ =\ {\Delta}^{1-(1/s)}\cdot{\varrho}^{1/s}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last equality follows from the observations that
\sum_{z\in N_{G}(w)}\upphi_{1}(z)={\varrho}\cdot\upphi_{1}(w)

and
d^{1-(1/s)}\leq{\Delta}^{1-(1/s)}
.
Plugging (
7.9
) into (
7.8
)
we get

\displaystyle\mathbold{q}(w)

\displaystyle\leq 1+c\cdot{\Delta}^{1-(1/s)}\cdot{\varrho}^{1/s}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\left(\delta\cdot{\varrho}\right)^{\ell/s}\kern 5.0pt.

The above holds for any
w\in V\setminus\Lambda
, implying that (
7.5
) is true.

Theorem

3.6
follows.
∎

7.1.
Proof of
Theorem

7.1

We abbreviate
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
to
{\mathcal{I}}
and the diagonal element
\UpD(u,u)
to
\UpD(u)
.

Let
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
, while consider the weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}
over the edges of
T
obtain
by applying the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction to the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

For every path
P
of length
\ell\geq 0
that starts from the root of tree
T
, i.e.,
P=e_{1},\ldots e_{\ell}
, let

(7.10)

\displaystyle{\tt weight}(P)

\displaystyle=\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\upalpha(e_{i})\kern 5.0pt.

Note that we have written path
P
using its edges.

The following result is useful to involve the potential function
{\Psi}
in our derivations.

Claim 7.2
.

For integer
\ell>1
and any path
P=e_{1},\ldots e_{\ell}
such that
{\tt weight}(P)\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle|{\tt weight}(P)|

\displaystyle=\uppsi(e_{\ell})\cdot\frac{|\upalpha(e_{1})|}{\uppsi(e_{1})}\cdot\prod^{\ell}_{i=2}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i-1})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt,

where
\uppsi(e)=\chi(\upalpha(e))
and
\chi(\cdot)
is the derivative of the potential function
{\Psi}
, i.e.,
\chi={\Psi}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Our assumptions for the potential function
{\Psi}
imply that, for every
e_{i}\in P
, we have
\uppsi(e_{i})\neq 0
.

Using a simple telescopic trick, we get

\displaystyle|{\tt weight}(P)|

\displaystyle=\prod^{\ell}_{i=1}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\ =\ \uppsi(e_{\ell})\cdot\frac{|\upalpha(e_{1})|}{\uppsi(e_{1})}\cdot\prod^{\ell}_{i=2}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i-1})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt.

The claim follows.
∎

For
u\in V\setminus\Lambda
and
\ell\geq 0
, let
{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)
be the set of paths
P
from the root of
T
to
the set of copies of vertex
u
which are at level
\ell
of the tree such that
{\tt weight}(P)\neq 0
. Also, let

{\tt A}(u,\ell)
be the set of copies of
u
at level
\ell
in
T
that are connected to the root with a path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)
.

Let

(7.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\sum\nolimits_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)}|{\tt weight}(P)|\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt.

Recalling that
Proposition

5.9
implies

(7.12)

\displaystyle\textstyle\left(\UpD^{-1}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\UpD\right)(w,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\UpD(w)}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\sum\nolimits_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)}{\tt weight}(P)\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt,

we have

(7.13)

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}|\left(\UpD^{-1}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\UpD\right)(w,u)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\UpD(w)}\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\kern 5.0pt.

Fix
\ell>1
. For a vertex
v
at level
h
of
T
, where
h=0,\ldots,\ell
,
let the quantity
{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}
be defined as follows:
For
h=\ell
, we let

(7.14)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\mathds{1}\{v\in{\tt A}(u,\ell)\}\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt.

For a vertex
v
, which is at level
0<h<\ell
such that vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}
are its children, we let

(7.15)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\uppsi(b_{v})\cdot\sum\nolimits_{j}\frac{|\upalpha(b_{j})|}{\uppsi(b_{j})}\cdot{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v_{j}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
b_{v}
is the edge that connects
v
to its parent, while
b_{j}
is the edge that connects
v

to its child
v_{j}
. Since we assume that
h>0
,
v
has a parent, hence
b_{v}
is well-defined.
Also, recall
\uppsi(\cdot)
from
Claim

7.2
.

Finally, for
h=0
, i.e.,
v
and the root of
T
are identical, we let

(7.16)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}\ =\ {{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}

\displaystyle=\max_{\bar{b},\hat{b}\in T}\left\{{\textstyle\uppsi\left(\bar{b}\right)\cdot\frac{|\upalpha\left(\hat{b}\right)|}{\uppsi\left(\hat{b}\right)}}\right\}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{z_{j}}{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{z_{j}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}
are the children of the root of
T
.

Claim 7.3
.

For any
\ell>1
, we have
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\leq{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}
.

The proof of
Claim

7.3
appears immediately after the proof of
Theorem

7.1
.

The Boundedness property of the
(s,\delta,c)
-potential
{\Psi}
, together with (
7.16
)
imply

(7.17)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot\sum\nolimits_{z_{j}}{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{z_{j}}\kern 5.0pt.

The Contraction property of the
(s,\delta,c)
-potential
{\Psi}
and (
7.15
)
imply that, for every vertex
v
at level
0<h<\ell
, we have

(7.18)

\displaystyle\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}\right)^{s}
