\displaystyle\leq\delta\cdot\sum\nolimits_{v_{j}}\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v_{j}}\right)^{s}\kern 5.0pt,

where recall that
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}
are the children of
v
.
Then, (
7.18
) and (
7.14
), imply

(7.19)

\displaystyle\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{\ell-h}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}|{\tt A}(u,\ell)\cap T_{v}|\cdot\left(\UpD(u)\right)^{s}\kern 5.0pt,

where, recall that
{\tt A}(u,\ell)\subseteq{\tt A}(u)
is the set of copies of vertex
u
in
T

that are at level
\ell
.
T_{v}
is the subtree of
T
that includes
v
and its descendants.

Applying (
7.19
) for the children of the root and plugging the result into (
7.17
), we get

(7.20)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot\sum\nolimits_{z_{j}}\left(\delta^{\ell-1}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}|{\tt A}(u,\ell)\cap T_{z_{j}}|\cdot\left(\UpD(u)\right)^{s}\right)^{1/{s}}\kern 5.0pt,

where note that each
z_{j}
is at level
h=1
of
T
.

Suppose that
z_{j}
is a copy of vertex
z
in
G
. Then, since
\ell>1
, we have

(7.21)

\displaystyle|{\tt A}(u,\ell)\cap T_{z_{j}}|\leq\left(\UpC\cdot{\UpA}^{\ell-1}_{G}\right)(wz,u)\kern 5.0pt,

where recall matrix
\UpC
from (
7.1
), while
{\UpA}_{G}
is the
adjacency matrix of
G
.
It is not hard to check that both quantities in (
7.21
) count walks of length
\ell
in
G
starting
from the oriented edge
wz
and ending at vertex
u
. To see that (
7.21
) is true,
we note that the l.h.s. considers only self-avoiding walks, while the
r.h.s. considers simple walks., i.e., including the self-avoiding ones.

Combining (
7.21
) with (
7.20
) and
Claim

7.3
,
for
\ell>1
, we have

(7.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot\sum\nolimits_{z\in N_{G}(w)}\left(\delta^{\ell-1}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\left(\UpC\cdot{\UpA}^{\ell-1}_{G}\right)(wz,u)\cdot\left(\UpD(u)\right)^{s}\right)^{1/s}\kern 5.0pt.

As far as
\mathcal{H}_{1}
is concerned, note that for any edge
e\in T
, we have

(7.23)

\displaystyle|\upalpha(e)|=\frac{\uppsi(e)}{\uppsi(e)}\cdot|\upalpha(e)|\leq\max_{\bar{e},\hat{e}\in T}\left\{\uppsi(\bar{e})\cdot\frac{|\upalpha(\hat{e})|}{\uppsi(\hat{e})}\right\}\leq c\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality follows from the Boundedness condition of the
(s,\delta,c)
-potential function
{\Psi}
.
Applying the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
for
\ell=1
, we get

(7.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{z\in N_{G}(w)}\UpD(z)\cdot|\upalpha(e_{z})|\leq c\cdot\sum\nolimits_{z\in N_{G}(w)}\UpD(z)\kern 5.0pt,

where
\upalpha(e_{z})
corresponds to the weight in
T
for the edge
e_{z}
that connects the root
of
T
with the copy of vertex
z
. The last inequality uses (
7.23
).

In light of all the above, we get (
7.2
) by plugging (
7.22
)
and (
7.24
) into (
7.13
) and noting that
\mathcal{H}_{0}=\UpD(w)
.

Theorem

7.1
follows.
\square
.

Proof of
Claim

7.3
.

For brevity, we let

\displaystyle{\tt Max}

\displaystyle=\max_{\bar{e},\hat{e}\in T}\left\{{\textstyle\uppsi(\bar{e})\cdot\frac{|\upalpha(\hat{e})|}{\uppsi(\hat{e})}}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that
\ell>1
. From the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
in (
7.11
) and
Claim

7.2
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\sum\nolimits_{P=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})\in{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)}\left(\uppsi(e_{\ell})\cdot\frac{|\upalpha(e_{1})|}{\uppsi(e_{1})}\right)\cdot\prod\nolimits_{2\leq i\leq\ell}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i-1})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt.

For
u\in V\setminus\Lambda
and
\ell\geq 0
, recall that
{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)
is the set of paths
P
from the root of
T
to
the set of copies of vertex
u
which are at level
\ell
of the tree such that
{\tt weight}(P)\neq 0
. Also, recall that

{\tt A}(u,\ell)
is the set of copies of
u
at level
\ell
in
T
that are connected to the root with a path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)
.

Noting that
\left(\uppsi(e_{\ell})\cdot\frac{|\upalpha(e_{1})|}{\uppsi(e_{1})}\right)\leq{\tt Max}
, we get

(7.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq{\tt Max}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\sum\nolimits_{P=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})\in{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)}\prod\nolimits_{2\leq i\leq\ell}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i-1})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt.

As far as
{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}
is concerned, recalling (
7.16
), we have

(7.26)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}

\displaystyle={\tt Max}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{1\leq j\leq r}{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{z_{j}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}
are the children of the root, for some integer
r>0
.

We show that for any vertex
v
at level
h=1,\ldots,\ell-1
of the tree
T
, we have

(7.27)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\sum\nolimits_{P=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell-h+1})\in{\mathcal{P}}(v,u,\ell)}\prod\nolimits_{2\leq i\leq\ell-h+1}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i-1})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt,

where
{\mathcal{P}}(v,u,\ell)
is the set of paths of length
\ell-h+1
from the parent of vertex
v
to

{\tt A}(u,\ell)\cap T_{v}
.

Before proving that (
7.27
) is true, let us show how it implies
Claim

7.3
.
For every child
z_{j}
of the root, (
7.27
) implies

(7.28)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{z_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\sum\nolimits_{P=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})\in{\mathcal{P}}(z_{j},u,\ell)}\prod\nolimits_{2\leq i\leq\ell}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i-1})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt.

Summing the above over all the children of the root, we get

(7.29)

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{z_{j}}{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{z_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\sum\nolimits_{P=(e_{1},\ldots e_{\ell})\in{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)}\prod\nolimits_{2\leq i\leq\ell}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i-1})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt.

For the above, we use the observation that the sets
{\mathcal{P}}(z_{j},u,\ell)
’s partition
{\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)
, i.e., we have

\cup_{z_{j}}{\mathcal{P}}(z_{j},u,\ell)={\mathcal{P}}(u,\ell)
, while for distinct
{j},i
the sets

{\mathcal{P}}(z_{j},u,\ell)
and
{\mathcal{P}}(z_{i},u,\ell)
do not intersect.

Plugging (
7.29
) into (
7.26
), we see that

{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}
is equal to the r.h.s. of the inequality in (
7.25
). This implies
that
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\leq{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}
, which also proves the claim.

We conclude the proof of
Claim

7.3
by showing that (
7.27
) is true.
To this end, we use induction on the level
h
of vertex
v
in (
7.27
).
The basis of the induction corresponds to taking
h=\ell-1
. For the inductive step, we assume that there is

h\leq\ell-1
such that that (
7.27
) is true. We then prove that (
7.27
)
is also true for any vertex
v
at level
h-1
of tree
T
.

We start with the basis and show (
7.27
) for a vertex
v

at level
\ell-1
.
As per standard notation, let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}
be the children of
v
. These children are vertices at level
\ell
.
From (
7.14
) and (
7.15
), we have

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{j}\frac{\uppsi(b_{v})}{\uppsi(b_{j})}\cdot|\upalpha(b_{j})|\cdot{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v_{j}}

(7.30)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\sum\nolimits_{j}\frac{\uppsi(b_{v})}{\uppsi(b_{j})}\cdot|\upalpha(b_{j})|\cdot\mathds{1}\{v_{j}\in{\tt A}(u,\ell)\}\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt,

where we let
b_{v}
be the edge that connects
v
to its parent at
T
, while edge
b_{j}
connects

v
to its child
v_{j}
. For the second equality, we substitute
{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v_{j}}
by using (
7.14
).

For each
u\in V\setminus\Lambda
in (
7.30
),
the summand
\frac{\uppsi(b_{v})}{\uppsi(b_{j})}\cdot|\upalpha(b_{j})|\cdot\mathds{1}\{v_{j}\in{\tt A}(u,\ell)\}
is non-zero only if the edges
b_{v}
and
b_{j}

form a path which belongs to set
{\mathcal{P}}(v,u,\ell)
, i.e., we have

(b_{v},b_{j})\in{\mathcal{P}}(v,u,\ell)
.

We use this observation and write (
7.30
) as follows:

(7.31)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\sum\nolimits_{P=(e_{1},e_{2})\in{\mathcal{P}}(v,u,\ell)}\frac{\uppsi(e_{1})}{\uppsi(e_{2})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{2})|\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt.

It is not hard to see that (
7.31
) corresponds to (
7.27
)
for
h=\ell-1
. This concludes the proof of the base of the induction.

The induction hypothesis is that there exists
1<h\leq\ell-1
, such that (
7.27
)
is true for any vertex at level
h
of tree
T
. We show that the hypothesis implies that
(
7.27
) is true for any vertex
v
at level
h-1
.
As per standard notation, we let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}
be the children of
v
,
while edge
b_{v}
connects
v
to its parent and edge
b_{j}
connects

v
to its child
v_{j}
. From (
7.15
) we have

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{j}\frac{\uppsi(b_{v})}{\uppsi(b_{j})}\cdot|\upalpha(b_{j})|\cdot{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v_{j}}\kern 5.0pt.

The children of
v
are at level
h
.
Using the hypothesis, we substitute each
{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v_{j}}
using (
7.27
) and get

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\sum\nolimits_{j}\frac{\uppsi(b_{v})}{\uppsi(b_{j})}\cdot|\upalpha(b_{j})|\cdot\sum\nolimits_{P=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell-h+1})\in{\mathcal{P}}(v_{j},u,\ell)}\prod\nolimits_{2\leq i\leq\ell-h+1}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i-1})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt.

It is elementary that for any
1\leq j\leq d
and for every path
P=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell-h+1})\in{\mathcal{P}}(v_{j},u,\ell)
,
there is exactly one path
Q=(\overline{e}_{1},\ldots,\overline{e}_{\ell-h+2})\in{\mathcal{P}}(v,u,\ell)
, with
\overline{e}_{2}=\{v,v_{j}\}
,
such that

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{2\leq i\leq\ell-h+2}\frac{\uppsi(\overline{e}_{i-1})}{\uppsi(\overline{e}_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(\overline{e}_{i})|

\displaystyle=\frac{\uppsi(b_{v})}{\uppsi(b_{j})}\cdot|\upalpha(b_{j})|\cdot\prod\nolimits_{2\leq i\leq\ell-h+1}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i-1})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt

and vice versa. Combining the two relations above, we get

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{u\in V\setminus\Lambda}\sum\nolimits_{Q=(\overline{e}_{1},\ldots,\overline{e}_{\ell-h+2})\in{\mathcal{P}}(v,u,\ell)}\prod\nolimits_{2\leq i\leq\ell-h+2}\frac{\uppsi(\overline{e}_{i-1})}{\uppsi(\overline{e}_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha(\overline{e}_{i})|\cdot\UpD(u)\kern 5.0pt,

which proves the inductive steps and concludes the inductive proof.

Claim

7.3
follows.
∎

Figure 13.
Initial graph
G

Figure 14.
Graph
{G}_{P}
, for
P=w,b

8.
Taking Extensions

In this section, we introduce the notion of extension. An extension is an operation that we typically apply to a
graph
G
or a Gibbs distribution on this graph. Subsequently, we use extensions to define the extended influence
matrix
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
, in
Section

9
.

8.1.
The
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
G
:

We consider graph
G=(V,E)
and a self-avoiding walk
P
of length
k\geq 1
in this graph. We introduce the notion of

P
-extension of
G
, denoted as
{G}_{P}
. This is a graph obtained after a set of
operations on
G
. We use induction on the length
k
of walk
P
to define
{G}_{P}
.

First, consider
P
of length
1
, while let
P=u,v
. Let
\partial\UpS
be the set of
all neighbours of vertex
u
in graph
G
apart from vertex
v
, i.e.,
\partial\UpS=N_{G}(u)\setminus\{v\}
.
Graph
{G}_{P}
is obtained by working as follows:

(1)

remove each edge of
G
that connects
u
to a vertex
z\in\partial\UpS
,

(2)

for each
z\in\partial\UpS
, insert the new vertex
uz
in the graph which is only connected to
z
.

Figures

14
and
14
show an example of the above operations.

Fig.

14
shows the initial graph
G
.

Fig.

14
shows the
P
-extension of
G
, for walk
P=w,b
.

We call
split-vertices
all vertices introduced in step (b).
We let
\UpS_{P}
denote the set of split-vertices in
{G}_{P}
.
In the example shown in
Figures

14
and
14
, we have
\UpS_{P}=\{wa,wx\}
.

Suppose, now, that
Px
is a self-avoiding walk in
G
, which is of length
k>1
.
With
Px
, we indicate that vertex
x
is the last vertex of the walk, while
P
corresponds
to the first
k
vertices.

P
is a self-avoiding walk of length
k-1
in
G
. We show how to obtain
{G}_{Px}
using
{G}_{P}
.

It is easy to see that
Px
is also a self-avoiding walk in
{G}_{P}
. Let
u
be the last vertex in
P
, i.e., vertex
u

is just before
x
in
Px
. Let
\partial\UpS
contain all neighbours of
u
in
{G}_{P}

apart from
x
, the vertex in
P
prior to
u
and the vertices in
\UpS_{P}
.
If
\partial\UpS\neq\emptyset
, then
{G}_{Px}
is the result of the following operations on
{G}_{P}
:

(3)

remove each edge in
{G}_{P}
that connects
u
to a vertex
z\in\partial\UpS
,

(4)

for each
z\in\partial\UpS
, we insert the split-vertex
uz
, which is only connected to
z
.

If
\partial\UpS=\emptyset
, graphs
{G}_{P}
and
{G}_{Px}
are identical.

For graph
{G}_{Px}
, the set of split vertices
\UpS_{Px}
corresponds to the union of
the split-vertices
\UpS_{P}
and the newly introduced split-vertices at step (d).
We follow the convention to denote with
uz
the split-vertex obtained from
vertex
u
of the initial graph
G
and is connected to vertex
z
.

Figures

16
and
16
provide further examples of extensions of
the graph shown in
Fig.

14
. Specifically,
Fig.

16
illustrates
graph
{G}_{P}
for the self-avoiding walk
P=w,b,e
. In
Fig.

16
, we have
graph
{G}_{P}
for walk
P=w,b,e,f
.

We view graph
{G}_{P}
as consisting of the original set of vertices,
i.e., those from the initial graph
G
, and
\UpS_{P}
. In the example of
Fig.

16
, graph

{G}_{P}
consists of the set of vertices of the initial graph
G
from
Fig.

14
and the
set of split-vertices
\UpS_{P}=\{wa,ba,bx,wx\}
.

Having defined the
P
-extension of
G
, we introduce the related
notion of the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
G
.
For integer
k\geq 1
, let
P
and
Q
be two self-avoiding walks in
G
, each of length
k
.
Let
w
and
u
be the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively. We say that
P
and

Q
are
compatible
when
{\rm dist}_{G}(u,w)\geq 2k
.

For compatible
P
and
Q
, the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of graph
G
, denoted as
{G}_{P,Q}
, is obtained
by taking the
Q
-extension of graph
{G}_{P}
.

The order in which we consider
P
and
Q
to obtain the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of graph
G

does not matter. It is easy to check that, as long as
P
and
Q
are compatible, one obtains the same
graph
{G}_{P,Q}
by either taking the
P
-extension of
{G}_{Q}
or the
Q
-extension of
{G}_{P}
.

The notion of
\{P,Q\}
-extension is well-defined since the compatibility of
P,Q
ensures that

Q
is a self-avoiding walk of
{G}_{P}
and
P
is a self-avoiding walk of
{G}_{Q}
.
For
P
and
Q
that are not compatible, we do not define
{G}_{P,Q}
.

Figure 15.
{G}_{P}
, for
P=w,b,e

Figure 16.
{G}_{P}
, for
P=w,b,e,f

8.2.
The
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
:

Consider graph
G=(V,E)
and a Gibbs distribution
\mu_{G}
for this graph.
Without loss of generality, assume a
total ordering
for the vertices in graph
G
.

Let
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
.
Also, let the self-avoiding walk
P
in graph
G
, which is of length
k\geq 1
.
We take
P
such that it does not intersect with set
\Lambda
.
Then, consider
{G}_{P}
, i.e., the
P
-extension of
G
.
Recall that we follow the convention to call
uz
the split-vertex in
\UpS_{P}
obtained from
vertex
u
of the initial graph
G
and is connected with vertex
z
.

The
P
-extension of the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}

is a new Gibbs distribution with underlying graph
{G}_{P}
having the same specifications as

\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

More specifically, we let
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{{G}_{P}}
be the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}

such that the set of pinned vertices
M=\Lambda\cup\UpS_{P}
while pinning
\sigma
satisfies the
following:
for
s\in\Lambda\subseteq M
, we have
\sigma(s)=\tau(s)
, while
for
qx\in\UpS_{P}
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma(qx)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}+1&&\textrm{if $z>x$}\kern 5.0pt,\\
-1&&\textrm{if $z<x$}\kern 5.0pt,\end{array}\right.

where
z
is the vertex after
q
in path
P
. The comparison between
z
and
x
is with respect to the total ordering
of the vertices in
G
.

Having defined the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
,
we introduce the related notion of the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.
For integer
k\geq 1
, let
P
and
Q
be two self-avoiding walks in
G
, each of
length
k
. Let
w
and
u
be the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively.
We assume that
P
and
Q
are compatible, i.e.,
{\rm dist}_{G}(u,w)\geq 2k
.

We obtain the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
by first taking
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{{G}_{P}}
, i.e.,
the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, and then taking the
Q
-extension of
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{{G}_{P}}
.
The
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
is a Gibbs distribution on graph
{G}_{P,Q}
.

As in the case of graphs, for
P
and
Q
which are compatible, the order in which we consider them to obtain the

\{P,Q\}
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
does not make any difference to the outcome.
For
P,Q
that are not compatible, we do not define the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

8.3.
Properties of the extensions

The results in this section are technical and are used later for the proofs of

Theorems

9.1
and
11.1
.
The reader may skip this section on the first read of this paper.

We consider two different settings for the extensions and we study their properties.

8.3.1.
Two simple properties:

The results presented in
Section

8.3.1
are used to prove
Theorem

9.1
.

Let integer
k>0
, graph
G=(V,E)
, set
\Lambda\subset V
and
w\in V\setminus\Lambda
.
We let
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
be the set of all self-avoiding walks in
G
of length
k
and do not intersect
with
\Lambda
.

Consider
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
. For each walk
P=x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}
in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that
emanates from
w
, there is a path
R_{P}=u_{0},\ldots,u_{k}
in tree
T
, which emanates from the root,
while
u_{i}
is a copy of
x_{i}
, for all
0\leq i\leq k
.

Let
t_{P}
be the subtree of
T
induced by the following set of vertices:

(1)

the vertices in path
R_{P}
,

(2)

all neighbours of
R_{P}
which are copies of a vertex in
P
,

(3)

all the descendants of vertex
u_{k}
in
T
, recall that
u_{k}
is the last vertex in
R_{P}
.

Also, let
T_{P}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},w)
. Tree
T_{P}
is well-defined since
w
is also a vertex in
{G}_{P}
.

In what follows, we show that we can
identify

t_{P}
with
T_{P}
. This means that there is
a bijection
{\uph}
from the set of vertices in
t_{P}
and those in
T_{P}
,
such that the following holds:

(1)

\uph
preserves adjacencies, i.e., if vertices
x
and
z
are adjacent in
t_{P}
, then
\uph(x)
and
\uph(z)
are adjacent in
T_{P}
,

(2)

for a vertex
u
in
t_{P}
, which is a copy of vertex
v
in
G
, we have that
\uph(u)
is a copy of vertex
v
, or a
copy of a split-vertex in
{G}_{P}
, generated by
v
.

Lemma 8.1
.

For walk
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanates from vertex
w
, we identify
t_{P}
with
T_{P}
, where the two trees are defined above.

The proof of
Lemma

8.1
appears in
Section

19.1
.

For
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
, consider the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.
Also, consider the collection of weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}
over the edges of
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
we obtain as described
in (
5.6
) with respect to
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

Fix
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}(w)
. Using
Lemma

8.1
, we identify
T_{P}
as a subtree of
T
.
We endow
T_{P}
with the edge weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in T_{P}}
.

Applying the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction to the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
gives rise to the weights
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}

over the edges of
T_{P}
. Now we have two sets of weights for the edges of
T_{P}
, i.e.,
\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in T_{P}}
and

\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}_{e\in T_{P}}
.

The following result establishes a connection between these two sets of weights on the edges of
T_{P}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

For walk
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanates from vertex
w
, consider
T_{P}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},w)
and the weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}
and
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}

over the edges of
T_{P}
, as defined above.
For all edges
e
in
T_{P}
, which are at distance
\ell\geq k
from the root of the tree, we have

\upalpha(e)=\upalpha_{P}(e)
.

The proof of
Lemma

8.2
appears in
Section

19.1
.

8.3.2.
Perturbation of weights:

The properties of the extension presented in this section, i.e.,
Section

8.3.2
, are used in the proof
of
Theorem

11.1
. The setting we consider here is different from the one in
Section

8.3.1
.

For integer
k>0
, consider
G=(V,E)
, set
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
. For

w,u\in V\setminus\Lambda
, such that
{\rm dist}_{G}(w,u)\geq 2k
, let walk
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}

that emanates from
w
.

Consider the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. Using the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction for
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
,
we obtain the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
for tree
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
. Also, let
\{\upalpha(e)\}

be the collection of weights over the edges of
T
that arise from
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
.

We use the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction for the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
and get the Gibbs distribution

\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
for tree
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
. Let
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
be the collection
of weights over the edges of
\overline{T}
that arise from
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
.

As opposed to the setting in
Section

8.3.1
, in this section we do
not
start the trees of self-avoiding walks
from vertex
w
, i.e., the first vertex in
P
; we rather start the trees from a distant vertex
u
.

Lemma 8.3
.

We can identify
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
as a subtree of
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
such that both trees have
the same root.

Each vertex in
T\setminus\overline{T}
corresponds to a descendant of a vertex in
{\tt A}_{P}
,
where set
{\tt A}_{P}
consist of each vertex
v
in
T
which is a copy of a vertex in
P
.

The proof of
Lemma

8.3
appears in
Section

19.2
.

We use
Lemma

8.3
and identify
\overline{T}
as a subtree of
T
.
We endow
\overline{T}
with the collection of weights

\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in\overline{T}}
.
Now, we have two sets of weights for
\overline{T}
, i.e.,
\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in\overline{T}}
and

\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}_{e\in\overline{T}}
.

In the following result, we consider a path
W
from the root of
\overline{T}
to a copy of vertex
w

and describe a relation between the weights of this path that are induced by
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
and

\{\upalpha(e)\}
, respectively.

Theorem 8.4
.

Let
{\Delta}>1
,
k\geq 1
and
N>1
be integers,
c>0
,
\uprho>1
,
s\geq 1
, while
let
b,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Also, let
\beta,\gamma,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
\gamma>0
,

0\leq\beta\leq\gamma
and
\lambda>0
.

Consider graph
G=(V,E)
of maximum degree
{\Delta}
such that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.
Assume that
\mu
, the Gibbs distribution on
G
specified by the parameters
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
,
is
b
-marginally bounded. For
\delta=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}
, suppose that there
is a
(s,\delta,c)
-potential function
{\Psi}
with respect to
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
.

There are constants
\ell_{0}>2k
,
\widehat{x}_{1}\geq 1
such that, for
\Lambda\subset V
and

\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
, for any
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
with
{\rm dist}(u,w)\geq 2k

and walk
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanates from
w
, the following is true:

For
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
, for path
W=e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}
from the root of
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)

to a copy of vertex
w
, we have

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{x}_{1}\cdot(1+\varepsilon/3)^{\ell/s}\cdot\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell-k}|\upalpha(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of
Theorem

8.4
appears in
Section

13
.

9.
The extended influence matrix
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}

The reader needs to be familiar with the notion of extensions we introduce in
Sections

8.1
and
8.2
to appreciate the content of this section.

Let integer
k\geq 1
. We consider graph
G=(V,E)
and a Gibbs distribution
\mu_{G}
, defined as in (
1.1
). Furthermore,
we have
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
.

Recall that
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
is the set of self-avoiding walks of length
k
in
G
.
Also, set
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
consists of all walks
w_{0},\ldots,w_{k}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
such that

w_{i}\notin\Lambda
, for
i=0,\ldots,k
.

The extended influence matrix
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
is an
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}

matrix such that for
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, we define the entry
{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)
as follows:
We have

(9.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=0\kern 5.0pt,

if
P
and
Q
are not compatible, i.e., the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
are at distance
<2k
.

Consider
P
and
Q
which are compatible, while let
w
and
u
be their starting vertices,
respectively. Letting
\upzeta^{P,Q}
be the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}

we have

(9.2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1\ |\ (w,+1))-\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1\ |\ (w,-1))\kern 5.0pt,

where
\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}
is the marginal of
\upzeta^{P,Q}
at vertex
u
.

Letting
{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}
be the influence matrix induced by
\upzeta^{P,Q}
,
(
9.2
) implies

(9.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}(w,u)\kern 5.0pt,

where, as mentioned above,
w
and
u
are the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively.

Concluding the basic definition of the extended influence matrix, it is useful to note that the entries of matrix

{\mathcal{L}}
do not refer to a single Gibbs distribution. As
P
and
Q
vary, the corresponding

\{P,Q\}
-extensions of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
are Gibbs distributions which are different from each
other. This makes it challenging to estimate matrix norms for
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Connections between
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
:

In the following result, we show that

{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
enjoys a natural relation with the (standard) influence matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
,
which is induced by the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

For integer
k>0
, let
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<k}
be the
(V\setminus\Lambda)\times(V\setminus\Lambda)
matrix
such that, for any
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

(9.4)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<k}(u,w)

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}\{{\rm dist}_{G}(u,w)<k\}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(u,w)\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\UpD_{k}
be the
(V\setminus\Lambda)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, zero-one matrix such that for

r\in V\setminus\Lambda
and
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, we have

(9.5)

\displaystyle\UpD_{k}(r,P)

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}\{\textrm{$r$ is the starting vertex of walk $P$}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Also, let
\UpC_{k}
be the
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times(V\setminus\Lambda)
, zero-one matrix, such that for

r\in V\setminus\Lambda
and
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, we have

(9.6)

\displaystyle\UpC_{k}(P,r)

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}\{\textrm{$r$ is the last vertex of walk $P$}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
{\Delta}>1
and
k>0
, let
\beta,\gamma,\lambda,b\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
\gamma>0
,

0\leq\beta\leq\gamma,\lambda>0
and
b>0
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be of maximum degree
{\Delta}
. Consider
\mu_{G}
the Gibbs distribution on
G

specified by the parameters
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
,
while assume that
\mu_{G}
is
b
-marginally
bounded.

There is a constant
\widehat{C}>0
such that, for any
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
,
there is an
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
matrix
{\mathcal{N}}
over
\mathbb{R}
,
for which we have

(9.7)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}

\displaystyle=\UpD_{k}\cdot\left({\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}\circ{\mathcal{N}}\right)\cdot\UpC_{k}+{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2k}\kern 5.0pt,

while for any
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
we also have
|{\mathcal{N}}(P,Q)|\leq\widehat{C}
.

Recall that
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}\circ{\mathcal{N}}
is the Hadamard product of the two matrices.
Matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2k}
is defined in (
9.4
).

The proof of
Theorem

9.1
appears in
Section

18.1
.

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
{\Delta}>1
and
k>0
, let
\beta,\gamma,\lambda,b\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
\gamma>0
,

0\leq\beta\leq\gamma,\lambda>0
and
b>0
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be of maximum degree
{\Delta}
. Consider
\mu_{G}
the Gibbs distribution on
G

specified by the parameters
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
,
while assume that
\mu_{G}
is
b
-marginally
bounded.

There are constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that, for any
\Lambda\subset V
and any
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
,
we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\|_{2}\leq C_{1}+C_{2}\cdot\||{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}|\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that, for matrix
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
, we let
|{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}|
denote the matrix
having entries
|{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}(P,Q)|
.
The proof of
Proposition

9.2
appears in
Section

19.4
.

10.
Proof of
Theorem

3.4
- SI with
\delta
-contraction &
\UpH_{G,k}

In this section, we assume that the reader is familiar with the notion of extended influence matrix
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}

that was introduced in
Section

9
.

We prove
Theorem

3.4
by using the following result.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
\delta,b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and integers
{\Delta},k\geq 1
.
Also, let
\beta,\gamma,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
0\leq\beta\leq\gamma
,

\gamma>0
and
\lambda>0
.

Consider graph
G=(V,E)
of maximum degree
{\Delta}
while
assume that
\mu_{G}
, the Gibbs distribution on
G
specified by the parameters
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
,
is
b
-marginally bounded.

Suppose the set of functions
\{H_{d}\}_{1\leq d<{\Delta}}

specified by
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
exhibits
\delta
-contraction.
There are constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that, for any
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
,
the influence matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, induced by
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, satisfies

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}+C_{2}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq k}\delta^{\ell}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of
Theorem

10.1
appears in
Section

10.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.4
.

Theorem

10.1
implies that for
\delta=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}
, there are constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

(10.1)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}+C_{2}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq k}\delta^{\ell}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt,

where recall that
\uprho=\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}
.

Claim

5.8
implies that there is a bounded number
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(N,\varepsilon,{\Delta})>k
such that,
for any
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
, we have

(10.2)

\displaystyle\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|^{1/\ell}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+{\varepsilon}/{2})\uprho\kern 5.0pt.

From (
10.1
), we have

(10.3)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}+C_{2}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{k\leq\ell<\ell_{0}}\delta^{\ell}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}+C_{2}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq\ell_{0}}\delta^{\ell}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\ell_{0}
and
C_{1},C_{2}
are bounded numbers, there is
{C}_{0}>1
, which is a bounded number, too, such that

(10.4)

\displaystyle C_{1}+C_{2}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{k\leq\ell<\ell_{0}}\delta^{\ell}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\leq{C}_{0}\kern 5.0pt.

For the above, we use the observation that, for any
0<\ell<\ell_{0}
, we have that

\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\leq({\Delta}-1)^{\ell}
, while

{\Delta}
is also a bounded number.
Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle C_{2}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq\ell_{0}}\delta^{\ell}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq\ell_{0}}\delta^{\ell}\cdot\left((1+\varepsilon/2)\uprho\right)^{\ell}

[from (
10.2
)]

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq\ell_{0}}\left(1-\varepsilon\right)^{\ell}\cdot\left(1+\varepsilon/2\right)^{\ell}

[since
\delta=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}
]

(10.5)

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{2}\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem

3.4
follows by plugging (
10.4
) and (
10.5
) into
(
10.3
) and setting
\widehat{C}=2C_{2}+{C}_{0}
.
∎

10.1.
Proof of
Theorem

10.1

Recall that set
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
consists of the self-avoiding walks of length
k
in
G

which do not intersect with set
\Lambda
.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be the
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
matrix
such that, for any
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, the entry
\mathcal{J}(P,Q)
is defined as follows:
Let
w
and
u
be the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively.
If
P
and
Q
are not compatible, i.e.,
{\rm dist}_{G}(u,w)<2k
, we let
\mathcal{J}(P,Q)=0
.

For
P
and
Q
which are compatible, we consider graph
{G}_{P,Q}
, the
(P,Q)
-extension of
G
,
and let
T_{P,Q}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P,Q},w)
. We let

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\delta^{\ell}\times|\widehat{\tt A}(u,\ell)|\kern 5.0pt,

where we let set
\widehat{\tt A}(u,\ell)
consist of all copies of vertex
u
in
T_{P,Q}
which correspond to
self-avoiding walks of length
\ell
in
{G}_{P,Q}
.

Lemma 10.2
.

We have
|{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}|\leq\mathcal{J}
, where the inequality is entry-wise.

The proof of
Lemma

10.2
appears in
Section

10.2
.

As per standard notation,
|{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}|
in
Lemma

10.2
corresponds
to the matrix having entries
|{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}(P,Q)|
.

Proposition

9.2
implies that there are constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

(10.6)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}+c_{2}\cdot\||{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}|\|_{2}\ \leq\ c_{1}+c_{2}\cdot\|\mathcal{J}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The second inequality follows from
Lemma

10.2
and the observation that

|{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}|
and
\mathcal{J}
have non-negative entries.

Proposition 10.3
.

We have

(10.7)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{J}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq k}\delta^{\ell+k}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of
Proposition

10.3
appears in
Section

10.3
.

Theorem

10.1
follows by plugging (
10.7
) into
(
10.6
) and setting
C_{1}=c_{1}
and
C_{2}=c_{2}\cdot\delta^{k}
.

\square

10.2.
Proof of
Lemma

10.2

When there is no danger of confusion, in what follows we abbreviate matrix
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
to
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Let
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, while let
w
and
u
be their starting vertices, respectively.
For non-compatible
P,Q
, the definitions of matrices
{\mathcal{L}}
and

\mathcal{J}
imply that
{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)=\mathcal{J}(P,Q)=0
.
We consider compatible
P
and
Q
. It suffices to show that
|{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)|\leq\mathcal{J}(P,Q)
.

We let
\upzeta^{P,Q}
be the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, while let
{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}
be
the influence matrix induced by
\upzeta^{P,Q}
.
Recall from (
9.3
) that

(10.8)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)={\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}(w,u)\kern 5.0pt,

where, as mentioned above,
w
and
u
are the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively.

Let
T_{P,Q}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P,Q},w)
.
Also, let
\{\upalpha(e)\}
be the collection of weights over the edges of
T_{P,Q}
we obtain as
described in (
5.6
) with respect to the Gibbs distribution
\upzeta^{P,Q}
.
We have

(10.9)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\sum\nolimits_{W\in T_{P,Q}:|W|=\ell}\prod\nolimits_{e\in W}\upalpha(e)\kern 5.0pt,

where
W
varies over the paths of length
\ell
in
T_{P,Q}
from the root to the copies of vertex
u

corresponding to self-avoiding walks in
{G}_{P,Q}
. Any copies of
u
in
T_{P,Q}
that do not correspond
to self-avoiding walk have a pinning. The influence of the root to vertices with pinning is zero.

The assumption about
\delta
-contraction implies that
\upzeta^{P,Q}
also exhibits
\delta
-contraction, i.e.,
it has the same specifications as
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. Hence, we have

|\prod\nolimits_{e\in W}\upalpha(e)|\leq\delta^{\ell}

for each
W
of length
\ell
.
Combining this observation with (
10.8
) and (
10.9
), we obtain

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)|=|{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}(w,u)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\sum\nolimits_{W\in T_{P,Q}:|W|=\ell}\delta^{\ell}=\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\delta^{\ell}\cdot|\widehat{\tt A}(u,\ell)|=\mathcal{J}(P,Q)\kern 5.0pt.

The above proves that indeed
|{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)|\leq\mathcal{J}(P,Q)
for
P
and
Q
which are compatible.

Lemma

10.2
follows.
\square

10.3.
Proof of
Proposition

10.3

First, we show that the following entry-wise inequality

(10.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq k}\delta^{\ell+k}\cdot((\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\cdot\UpR)_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\UpR
is the involution defined in (
5.5
), while
\left((\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\cdot\UpR\right)_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}
is the principal submatrix of
(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\cdot\UpR

induced by the elements in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
.

Consider
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, which are compatible. Let
w
and
u

be the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively.
Consider
{G}_{P,Q}
, the
(P,Q)
-extension of graph
G
and let
T_{P,Q}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P,Q},w)
.

From the definition of matrix
\mathcal{J}
, we have

(10.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\delta^{\ell}\cdot|\widehat{\tt A}(u,\ell)|\kern 5.0pt,

where recall that
\widehat{\tt A}(u,\ell)
is the set of copies of vertex
u
in
T_{P,Q}
which correspond to
self-avoiding walks of length
\ell
in
{G}_{P,Q}
.
The vertex
u
is the starting vertex of
Q
. Note that the compatibility assumption for
P
and
Q
implies

{\rm dist}_{G}(w,u)\geq 2k
. Hence the copies of
u
in
T
are at distance
\geq 2k
from the root.

For
\ell\geq 2k
, we obtain a bound on
|\widehat{\tt A}(u,\ell)|
in terms of the
k
-non-backtracking
matrix
\UpH_{G,k}
.
W.l.o.g. let
P=v_{0},\ldots,v_{k}
and
Q=x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}
. The cardinality of set
\widehat{\tt A}(u,\ell)
is
equal to the number of self-avoiding walks
R=z_{0},\ldots,z_{\ell}
in
{G}_{P,Q}
, satisfying the following
condition: for
0\leq i\leq k
, we have

•

z_{i}
is a copy of vertex
v_{i}
and
z_{\ell-{i}}
is a copy of
x_{i}
.

Recall that the additional vertices that
{G}_{P,Q}
has compared to
G
are split-vertices, each of which
is of degree
1
. No split-vertex can be a part of a self-avoiding walk from
w
to
u
.
Hence, every self-avoiding walk
R
in
{G}_{P,Q}
that satisfies the above condition also appears in
G
.

Furthermore, since every self-avoiding walk of length
\ell
from
w
to
u
in
G
can also be considered as a

k
-non-backtracking walk of length
\ell-k
from
P
to
Q^{-1}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle|\widehat{\tt A}(u,\ell)|

\displaystyle\leq(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell-k}(P,Q^{-1})=((\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell-k}\cdot\UpR)(P,Q)\kern 5.0pt.

Walk
Q^{-1}
corresponds to
Q
reversed, i.e.,
Q^{-1}=x_{k},x_{k-1},\ldots,x_{0}
.
Plugging the above inequality into (
10.11
), we get that (
10.10
) is
true for compatible walks
P
and
Q
.

For non-compatible
P,Q
, the definition of
\mathcal{J}
specifies that
\mathcal{J}(P,Q)=0
. This implies that
(
10.10
) is true even for non-compatible
P
and
Q
since we always have

((\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell-k}\cdot\UpR)(P,Q)\geq 0
.

All the above establish that (
10.10
) is true. Furthermore, (
10.10
),
the fact that both
\mathcal{J}
and
\left(\UpH_{G,k}\right)^{\ell-k}\cdot\UpR
have non-negative entries and the
triangle inequality imply

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{J}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq k}\delta^{\ell+k}\cdot\|((\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\cdot\UpR)_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}\|_{2}\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq k}\delta^{\ell+k}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\cdot\UpR\|_{2}\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq k}\delta^{\ell+k}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The second inequality follows from Cauchy’s interlacing theorem. Note that matrix
(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\cdot\UpR
is symmetric
due to (
5.10
).
The third inequality follows since
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\cdot\UpR\|_{2}\leq\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\cdot\|\UpR\|_{2}
while, for the involutionary matrix
\UpR
,
we have
\|\UpR\|_{2}=1
.

Proposition

10.3
follows.
\square

11.
Proof of
Theorem

3.7
- SI with Potentials &
\UpH_{G,k}

In this section, we assume that the reader is familiar with the notion of the extended influence matrix
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}

introduced in
Section

9
.

In what follows, for a matrix
\UpD\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
, we let
|\UpD|
denote the matrix having entries
|\UpD_{i,j}|
.

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
{\Delta}>1
and
k,N\geq 1
be integers,
c>0
,
\uprho>1
,
s\geq 1
while
let
b,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Also, let
\beta,\gamma,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
\gamma>0
,

0\leq\beta\leq\gamma
and
\lambda>0
.

Consider graph
G=(V,E)
of maximum degree
{\Delta}
such that

\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.
Assume that
\mu_{G}
, the Gibbs distribution on
G
specified by the parameters
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
,
is
b
-marginally bounded.
For
\delta=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}
, suppose that there
is a
(s,\delta,c)
-potential function
{\Psi}
with respect to
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
.

There exists a fixed constant
\widehat{c}\geq 1
such that
for any
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
, the extended influence matrix

{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
, that is induced by
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, satisfies that

\displaystyle\||{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}|\|_{2s}

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{c}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq k}\left({\textstyle\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}\right)^{\ell}}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\right)^{1/s}\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of
Theorem

11.1
appears in
Section

12.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.7
.

When there is no danger of confusion, in this proof, we abbreviate

{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
to
{\mathcal{I}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}
, respectively.

From
Proposition

9.2
, there are constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

(11.1)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{I}}\|_{2}\leq c_{1}+c_{2}\cdot\||{\mathcal{L}}|\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem

3.7
follows by bounding appropriately
\||{\mathcal{L}}_{k}|\|_{2}
.
To this end, we use the following claim.

Claim 11.2
.

There is a constant
c_{3}>0
such that
\||{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}|\|_{2}\leq c_{3}\cdot\||{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}|\|_{2s}
.

Theorem

11.1
and
Claim

11.2
imply that there exists a fixed number
{c}_{4}\geq 1
, such that

(11.2)

\displaystyle\||{\mathcal{L}}|\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq{c}_{4}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq k}\left({\textstyle\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}\right)^{\ell}}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\right)^{1/s}\kern 5.0pt.

where recall that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.

Claim

5.8
implies that there is a bounded integer
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(N,\varepsilon,{\Delta})>0
such that, for any
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
,
we have

(11.3)

\displaystyle\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|^{1/\ell}_{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon/4)\uprho\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\ell_{0}
is bounded, there exists a constant
c_{5}>0
such that

(11.4)

\displaystyle{c}_{4}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{k\leq\ell<\ell_{0}}\left({\textstyle\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}\right)^{\ell}}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\right)^{1/s}\leq c_{5}\kern 5.0pt.

For the above, we use that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell}\|_{2}\leq\|\UpH_{G,k}\|^{\ell}_{2}\leq({\Delta}-1)^{\ell}
.
Plugging (
11.4
) and (
11.3
) into (
11.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\||{\mathcal{L}}|\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{5}+{c}_{4}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq\ell_{0}}\left({\textstyle(1+\varepsilon/4)(1-\varepsilon/2)}\right)^{\ell/s}\ \leq\ c_{5}+\frac{c_{4}}{1-(1-\varepsilon/4)^{1/s}}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem

3.7
follows by plugging the above into (
11.1
) and
setting
C=c_{1}+c_{2}\cdot(c_{4}+c_{5})
.
∎

Proof of
Claim

11.2
.

For what follows, we abbreviate
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
to
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Consider
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
which are compatible.
Let
w
and
u
be the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively.
Then, as already noticed in (
9.3
), we have

(11.5)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}(w,u),

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(Q,P)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}(u,w)\kern 5.0pt,

where the influence matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}
is with respect to
\upzeta^{P,Q}
, the

\{P,Q\}
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. Note that
{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}
and

{\mathcal{I}}^{Q,P}
correspond to the same influence matrix.

A useful observation is that
Claim

5.1
and (
11.5
) imply that
if
\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(+1),\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1)\notin\{0,1\}
, then we have

(11.6)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(+1)\cdot\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(-1)}{\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1)\cdot\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(-1)}}\cdot{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1)\cdot\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(-1)}{\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(+1)\cdot\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(-1)}}\cdot{\mathcal{L}}(Q,P)\kern 5.0pt.

If at least one of
\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(+1),\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1)
is in
\{0,1\}
,
then we have
{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)={\mathcal{L}}(Q,P)=0
.

Let the
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
matrix
{\mathcal{M}}
be defined as follows:
For
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
with starting vertex
w
and

Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
with starting vertex
u
, such that
P
and
Q

are compatible while

\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(+1),\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1)\notin\{0,1\}
, we let

(11.7)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(+1)\cdot\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(-1)}{\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1)\cdot\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(-1)}}\cdot|{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)|\kern 5.0pt.

On the other hand, if at least one of
\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(+1),\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1)
is in
\{0,1\}
,
then we let
{\mathcal{M}}(P,Q)=0
. Also, we let
{\mathcal{M}}(P,Q)=0
when
P
and
Q
are not compatible.

{\mathcal{M}}
has non-negative entries, while (
11.6
) implies that it is symmetric.
Furthermore, we have

(11.8)

\displaystyle\||{\mathcal{L}}|\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq b^{-2}\cdot\|{\mathcal{M}}\|_{2}\leq b^{-2}\cdot\|{\mathcal{M}}\|_{2s}\leq b^{-4}\cdot\||{\mathcal{L}}|\|_{2s}\kern 5.0pt.

The first inequality follows by noting that (
11.7
) and the
b
-marginal boundedness assumption imply
that
|{\mathcal{L}}|\leq b^{-2}\cdot{\mathcal{M}}
, where the comparison is entry-wise. The second inequality follows since

{\mathcal{M}}
is symmetric. Similarly to the first inequality, the third inequality follows by noting that (
11.7
) and the
b
-marginal
boundedness assumption imply that
{\mathcal{M}}
has non-negative entries, while
{\mathcal{M}}\leq b^{-2}\cdot|{\mathcal{L}}|
,
where the comparison is entry-wise.

Claim

11.2
follows for
c_{3}=b^{-4}
.
∎

12.
Proof of
Theorem

11.1

Theorem

11.1
Proposition

12.1
Claim

12.2
Theorem

8.4

Figure 17.
Proof structure for
Theorem

11.1

For the proof of
Theorem

11.1
, we use the properties of the extensions shown in
Section

8.3.2

and in particular we use
Theorem

8.4
.

Fig.

17
shows the structure of the proof of
Theorem

11.1
.

12.1.
Proof of
Theorem

11.1

For a vector
{\bf v}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}_{\geq 0}
, and
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we let vector
{\bf v}^{r}
be such
that
{\bf v}^{r}(i)=({\bf v}(i))^{r}
, for
1\leq i\leq N
. Also, when there is no danger of confusion, we abbreviate
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
to
{\mathcal{L}}
.

For integer
\ell\geq 2k
, we let
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
be the
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
matrix
such that for
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, the entry
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}(P,Q)
is defined as follows:

Let
w
and
u
be the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively.
Assume, first, that
P
and
Q
are compatible, i.e.,
{\rm dist}_{G}(w,u)\geq 2k
. Let
{G}_{P,Q}

and
\upzeta^{P,Q}
be the
\{P,Q\}
-extensions of
G
and
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, respectively.
Also, let
T_{P,Q}=T_{\rm SAW}(G_{P,Q},w)
and the weights
\{\upalpha_{P,Q}(e)\}
are obtained as specified
in (
5.6
) with respect to
\upzeta^{P,Q}
. We let

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\ell}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{W\in T_{P,Q}:|W|=\ell}\prod\nolimits_{e\in W}|\upalpha_{P,Q}(e)|\kern 5.0pt,

where
W
varies over all the paths of length
\ell
from the root to the copies of
u
in
T_{P,Q}
that correspond to
self-avoiding walks. For non-compatible walks
P
and
Q
, we let
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}(P,Q)=0
.

From the definitions of the extended influence matrix
{\mathcal{L}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
, it is not hard to
verify the entry-wise inequality
|{\mathcal{L}}|\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
, which
implies

\displaystyle\||{\mathcal{L}}|\|_{2s}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\|\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\|_{2s}\kern 5.0pt.

In light of the above,
Theorem

11.1
follows by showing that there exists a constant

\widehat{c}\geq 1
such that

(12.1)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\|_{2s}

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{c}\cdot\left({\textstyle\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}\right)^{\ell-k}}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{{\ell-k}}\|_{2}\right)^{1/s}

\displaystyle\forall\ell\geq 2k\kern 5.0pt.

Before showing that (
12.1
) is true,
we introduce a few concepts and establish some useful technical results.

For
\ell\geq 2k
, we let
\UpZ^{(\ell)}
be the
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}

matrix such that for
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, the entry
\UpZ^{(\ell)}(P,Q)
is defined
as follows:

Let
w
and
u
be the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively. For
P
and

Q
which are compatible, we consider
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},w)
and let

\displaystyle\UpZ^{(\ell)}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\#\textrm{copies of walk $Q$ in $\overline{T}$ which is at distance $\ell$ from the root}\kern 5.0pt.

For vertex
x
in
\overline{T}
, which is connected to the root of the tree with path
W=x_{0},\ldots,x_{\ell}
, i.e.,
x_{0}

is the root and
x_{\ell}
is
x
; we say that
x
is a copy of walk
Q=z_{0},\ldots,z_{k}
, if
x_{\ell-i}
is a copy of

z_{i}
, for all
i=0,\ldots,k
.
For non-compatible
P
and
Q
, we have
\UpZ^{(\ell)}(P,Q)=0
.

For integer
\ell\geq 2k
and
{\bf q}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}_{\geq 0}
,
let vectors
\upf_{{\bf q},\ell},\upg_{{\bf q},\ell},\upk_{{\bf q},\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}_{\geq 0}
be
defined as follows: We let

(12.2)

\displaystyle\upf_{{\bf q},\ell}=\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\cdot{\bf q}\kern 5.0pt.

We also let

(12.3)

\displaystyle\upg_{{\bf q},\ell}

\displaystyle=\left({\textstyle\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}}\right)^{\ell/s}\cdot\left(\UpZ^{(\ell)}\cdot{\bf q}^{s}\right)^{1/s}\kern 5.0pt.

Similarly, we let

(12.4)

\displaystyle\upk_{{\bf q},\ell}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}\right)^{\ell/s}}\cdot\left(((\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell-k}\cdot\UpR)_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}\cdot{\bf q}^{s}\right)^{1/s}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\UpR
is the
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
involutionary matrix defined in (
5.5
).
Also,
((\UpH_{G,k})^{{\ell-k}}\cdot\UpR)_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}
corresponds to the principle submatrix
of
(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell-k}\cdot\UpR
induced by the elements in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
.

Proposition 12.1
.

For
k\geq 1
, there exists a constant
\widehat{c}_{0}\geq 1
such that, for any
\ell\geq 2k

and any
{\bf q}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}_{\geq 0}

(12.5)

\displaystyle\upf^{s}_{{\bf q},\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{c}_{0}\cdot\upg^{s}_{{\bf q},\ell}\kern 5.0pt,

where the inequality between the two vectors is entry-wise.

The proof of
Proposition

12.1
appears in
Section

12.2
.

Letting
{\bf 0}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}
be the all-zeros vector,
for any
{\bf q}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}_{\geq 0}
, for any
\ell\geq 2k
, we have

(12.6)

\displaystyle{\bf 0}\leq\upf_{{\bf q},\ell}\leq\widehat{c}_{0}\cdot\upg_{{\bf q},\ell}\leq\widehat{c}_{0}\cdot\upk_{{\bf q},\ell}\kern 5.0pt,

where the above inequalities are entry-wise, while
\widehat{c}_{0}
is from
Proposition

12.1
.

To see why (
12.6
) is true, note the following: The first inequality implies that the entries of

f_{{\bf q},\ell}
, which is defined in (
12.2
), are non-negative, which is true since both

\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
and
{\bf q}
have non-negative entries.
The second inequality follows from the fact that the entries of
\upg_{{\bf q},\ell}
are non-negative and

Proposition

12.1
.
The third inequality follows from the observation that each entry
\UpZ^{(\ell)}(P,Q)
is upper bounded by the
number of
k
-non-backtracking walks of length
\ell-k
from
P
to
Q
.

Let
{\bf q}^{\star}={\bf q}^{\star}(\ell)\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}_{\geq 0}
be
such that

(12.7)

\displaystyle\|{\bf q}^{\star}\|_{2s}

\displaystyle=1

and

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\|_{2s}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\cdot{\bf q}^{\star}\|_{2s}\kern 5.0pt.

We let
\upf^{\star}_{\ell}=\upf_{{\bf q}^{\star},\ell}
, while let
\upg^{\star}_{\ell}=\upg_{{\bf q}^{\star},\ell}
and

\upk^{\star}_{\ell}=\upk_{{\bf q}^{\star},\ell}
.
Noting that
{\bf q}^{\star}
has non-negative entries because the entries of
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
are non-negative,
from (
12.6
), we get

(12.8)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\|_{2s}

\displaystyle=\|\upf^{\star}_{\ell}\|_{2s}\leq\widehat{c}_{0}\cdot\|\upg^{\star}_{\ell}\|_{2s}\leq\widehat{c}_{0}\cdot\|\upk^{\star}_{\ell}\|_{2s}\kern 5.0pt.

Let vector
\upk^{s,\star}_{\ell}
be such that
\upk^{s,\star}_{\ell}(P)=({\upk}^{\star}_{\ell}(P))^{s}
,
for all
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
. Similarly, define
{\bf q}^{s,\star}
such that
{\bf q}^{s,\star}(P)=({{\bf q}}^{\star}(P))^{s}
.
From the definition of the corresponding vectors, we have

(12.9)

\displaystyle\|\upk^{\star}_{\ell}\|^{2s}_{2s}

\displaystyle=\|\upk^{s,\star}_{\ell}\|^{2}_{2}

and

\displaystyle\|{\bf q}^{s,\star}\|_{2}=\|{\bf q}^{\star}\|_{2s}=1\kern 5.0pt.

Using the above observation, we have

\displaystyle\|\upk^{\star}_{\ell}\|^{2s}_{2s}=\|\upk^{s,\star}_{\ell}\|^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}\right)^{2\ell}}\cdot\|((\UpH_{G,k})^{{\ell-k}}\cdot\UpR)_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}\cdot{\bf q}^{s,\star}\|^{2}_{2}

[from (
12.4
)]

\displaystyle\leq{\textstyle\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}\right)^{2\ell}}\cdot\|{\textstyle((\UpH_{G,k})^{{\ell-k}}\cdot\UpR)_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}}\|^{2}_{2}

[since
\|{\bf q}^{s,\star}\|_{2}=1
]

\displaystyle\leq{\textstyle\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}\right)^{2\ell}}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{{\ell-k}}\cdot\UpR\|^{2}_{2}

[since
(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell-k}\cdot\UpR
is symmetric]

(12.10)

\displaystyle={\textstyle\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}\right)^{2\ell}}\cdot\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{{\ell-k}}\|^{2}_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The third inequality follows from Cauchy’s interlacing theorem, e.g., see
[
26
]
.
Note that matrix
(\UpH_{G,k})^{\ell-k}\cdot\UpR
is symmetric due to (
5.10
).
The last derivation holds since for the involution
\UpR
, we have
\|\UpR\|_{2}=1
.

We get (
12.1
) by plugging (
12.10
) into (
12.8
)
and setting
\widehat{c}=\widehat{c}_{0}\cdot\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/2}{\uprho}\right)^{k/s}
.

Theorem

11.1
follows.

\square

12.2.
Proof of
Proposition

12.1

For integer
\ell\geq 2k
, let
{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}
be the
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
matrix
such that for
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, entry
{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}(P,Q)
is defined as follows:

Let
w
and
u
be the starting vertices of
P
and
Q
, respectively. Assume, first, that
P
and
Q
are compatible.
Let
{G}_{P}
and
\upzeta^{P}
be the
P
-extensions of
G
and
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, respectively.
Also, let
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},w)
while consider the edge-weights

\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
obtained as specified in (
5.6
) with respect to
\upzeta^{P}
.
Let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{W=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})\in\overline{T}}\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell-k}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt,

where
W
varies over all the paths of length
\ell
from the root of
\overline{T}
to set
{\tt A}(Q,\ell)
. Set
{\tt A}(Q,\ell)
consists of the
copies of
Q
in
\overline{T}
which are at distance
\ell
from the root, while for each path
R=e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}
from the root to
such copy, we have
\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell-k}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|\neq 0
.

For non compatible walks
P
and
Q
, i.e.,
{\rm dist}_{G}(u,w)<2k
, we let
{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}(P,Q)=0
.

For vertex
x
in
\overline{T}
, which is connected to the root with path

M=x_{0},\ldots,x_{\ell}
, i.e.,
x_{0}
is the root and
x_{\ell}
is vertex
x
,
recall that we say
x
is a copy of walk
Q=z_{0},\ldots,z_{k}
in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
,
if
x_{\ell-i}
is a copy of
z_{i}
, for
0\leq i\leq k
.

For
{\bf q}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}_{\geq 0}
, let

(12.11)

\displaystyle\upz_{{\bf q},\ell}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}\cdot{\bf q}\kern 5.0pt.

Claim 12.2
.

There exists fixed constant
\widehat{c}_{1}>0
such that
for any
{\bf q}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}_{\geq 0}
, we have

(12.12)

\displaystyle\upf^{s}_{{\bf q},\ell}\leq\widehat{c}_{1}\cdot(1+\varepsilon/3)^{\ell}\cdot\upz^{s}_{{\bf q},\ell}\kern 5.0pt,

where the inequality between the two vectors is entry-wise.

In light of
Claim

12.2
,
Proposition

12.1
follows by showing that there exists constant

\widehat{c}_{2}>0
such that, for any
\ell\geq 2k
and any
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, we have

(12.13)

\displaystyle\upz^{s}_{{\bf q},\ell}(P)

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{c}_{2}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/3)^{\ell}\cdot\upg^{s}_{{\bf q},\ell}(P)\kern 5.0pt.

Specifically, we get (
12.5
) by plugging (
12.13
) into
(
12.12
) and setting
\widehat{c}_{0}=\widehat{c}_{1}\cdot\widehat{c}_{2}
.
It remains to show that (
12.13
) is true.
The high-level approach to obtain (
12.13
)
is not too different from that we use to prove
Theorem

7.1
.

For what follows, we fix
\ell\geq 2k
and vector
{\bf q}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}_{\geq 0}
. Also, fix
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}

while let
w
be the starting vertices of
P
.

Claim 12.3
.

For
\ell\geq 2k
and any path
W=e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}
in
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},w)
such that

\prod^{\ell-k}_{i=1}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|\neq 0
,
we have

\displaystyle\prod^{\ell-k}_{i=1}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|

\displaystyle=\uppsi(e_{\ell-k})\cdot\frac{|\upalpha_{P}(e_{1})|}{\uppsi(e_{1})}\cdot\prod^{\ell-k}_{i=2}\frac{\uppsi(e_{i-1})}{\uppsi(e_{i})}\cdot|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt,

where
\uppsi(e)=\chi(\upalpha_{P}(e))
and
\chi={\Psi}^{\prime}
.

Claim

12.3
is very similar to
Claim

7.2
. For this reason, we omit its proof.

Recall that we fix
{\bf q}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}_{\geq 0}
. For each vertex
v
at level
h
of
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},w)
, where

0\leq h\leq\ell
, we let
{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}
be defined as follows:
For
h=\ell
, we let

(12.14)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}:P,Q\textrm{ compatible}}{\bf q}(Q)\cdot\mathds{1}\{v\in{\tt A}(Q,\ell)\}\kern 5.0pt.

For
\ell-k\leq h\leq\ell-1
, letting
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}
be the children of
v
, we let

(12.15)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{v_{i}}{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v_{i}}\kern 5.0pt.

For a vertex
v
at level
0<h<\ell-k
of the tree
\overline{T}
, we have

(12.16)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}

\displaystyle=\uppsi(b_{v})\cdot\sum\nolimits_{i}\frac{|\upalpha_{P}(b_{i})|}{\uppsi(b_{i})}\cdot{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v_{i}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
b_{i}
is the edge that connects
v
to its child
v_{i}
and edge
b_{v}
connects
v
to its parent in tree
\overline{T}
.

Finally, for
h=0
, i.e., vertex
v
is identical to the root of
\overline{T}
, we let

(12.17)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}={{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}

\displaystyle=\max_{e_{a},e_{b}\in\overline{T}}\left\{\uppsi(e_{a})\cdot\frac{|\upalpha_{P}(e_{b})|}{\uppsi(e_{b})}\right\}\cdot{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\pi}\kern 5.0pt,

where vertex
\pi
is the single child of the root of
\overline{T}
.

Claim 12.4
.

We have
\upz_{{\bf q},\ell}(P)\leq{{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}
.

The proof of
Claim

12.4
is tedious but very similar to that of
Claim

7.3
in the proof of
Theorem

7.1
.
For this reason we omit it.

Hölder’s inequality and (
12.15
) imply, for a vertex
v
at level
\ell-k\leq h\leq\ell-1
in
\overline{T}
, that we have

(12.18)

\displaystyle\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\leq{\Delta}^{s-1}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{v_{i}}\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v_{i}}\right)^{s}\kern 5.0pt.

The contraction property of
(s,\delta,c)
-potential
{\Psi}
, together with (
12.16
),
imply that for a vertex
v
at level
0<h<\ell-k
, we have

(12.19)

\displaystyle\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}\right)^{s}\leq\delta\cdot\sum\nolimits_{v_{i}}\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v_{i}}\right)^{s}\kern 5.0pt.

In light of the above two inequalities and (
12.14
),
for a vertex
v
at level
0<h<\ell-k
, we have

\displaystyle\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{v}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\leq{\Delta}^{k\cdot(s-1)}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}:P,Q\textrm{ compatible}}{\bf q}^{s}(Q)\cdot\delta^{\ell-h-k}\cdot|{\tt A}(Q,\ell)\cap\overline{T}_{v}|\kern 5.0pt.

For the single child of the root of
\overline{T}
, vertex
\pi
, the above implies

(12.20)

\displaystyle\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\pi}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\leq{\Delta}^{k\cdot(s-1)}\cdot\delta^{\ell-k-1}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}:P,Q\textrm{ compatible}}{\bf q}^{s}(Q)\cdot|{\tt A}(Q,\ell)|

(12.21)

\displaystyle={\Delta}^{k\cdot(s-1)}\cdot\left({\textstyle\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}}\right)^{\ell-k-1}\cdot(\UpZ^{(\ell)}\cdot{\bf q}^{s})(P)

(12.22)

\displaystyle\leq\left({\textstyle\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}}\right)^{-k-1}\cdot{\Delta}^{k\cdot(s-1)}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/3)^{\ell}\cdot\upg^{s}_{{\bf q},\ell}(P)\kern 5.0pt.

For (
12.20
) we use the observation that since the root of
\overline{T}
has only one child, i.e., vertex
\pi
,
and
\ell\geq 2k
, we have
{\tt A}(Q,\ell)\cap\overline{T}_{\pi}={\tt A}(Q,\ell)
. For (
12.21
) we use the definition
of matrix
\UpZ^{(\ell)}
and recall that
\delta=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}
.
The last inequality follows from the definition of
\upg_{{\bf q},\ell}
in (
12.3
).

The boundedness property of the
(s,\delta,c)
-potential and (
12.17
) imply

(12.23)

\displaystyle\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\rm root}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\leq c^{s}\cdot\left({{\mathcal{R}}}^{(\ell)}_{\pi}\right)^{s}\leq c^{s}\left({\textstyle\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}}\right)^{-k-1}\cdot{\Delta}^{k\cdot(s-1)}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/3)^{\ell}\cdot\upg^{s}_{{\bf q},\ell}(P)\kern 5.0pt,

where for the second equality, we use (
12.22
).
We get (
12.13
) by combining (
12.23
)
and
Claim

12.4
while setting

\widehat{c}_{2}=c^{s}\cdot{\Delta}^{k\cdot(s-1)}\cdot\left({\textstyle\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}}\right)^{-k-1}
.

All the above conclude the proof of
Proposition

12.1
.

\square

Proof
Claim

12.2
.

The definition of
\upf_{{\bf q},\ell}
implies that, for any
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, we have

(12.24)

\displaystyle\upf_{{\bf q},\ell}(P)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}:P,Q\textrm{ compatible}}{\bf q}(Q)\cdot\sum\nolimits_{W=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})}\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}|\upalpha_{P,Q}(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt,

where
W
varies over the paths from the root to the vertices in
{\tt A}(Q,\ell)
in tree
T_{P,Q}
.

The edge weights of path
W
are obtained using
\zeta^{P,Q}
, the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of
the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.
To obtain our result, we need to express
\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}|\upalpha_{P,Q}(e_{i})|

using the weights
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
, i.e., using the edge-weights obtained
using
\zeta^{P}
, i.e., the
P
-extension of the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

To this end, we use
Theorem

8.4
. This theorem implies that, there is
\widehat{c}_{1}>0
such that
for
W=e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}
and
\ell\geq 2k
we have

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{1\leq k\leq\ell}|\upalpha_{P,Q}(e_{i})|\leq\widehat{c}^{\ 1/s}_{1}\cdot(1+\varepsilon/3)^{\ell/s}\cdot\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell-k}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging the above into (
12.24
), we obtain

\displaystyle\upf_{{\bf q},\ell}(P)

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{c}^{\ 1/s}_{1}\cdot(1+\varepsilon/3)^{\ell/s}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}:P,Q\textrm{ compatible}}{\bf q}(Q)\cdot\sum\nolimits_{W=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})}\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell-k}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|

\displaystyle=\widehat{c}^{\ 1/s}_{1}\cdot(1+\varepsilon/3)^{\ell/s}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}:P,Q\textrm{ compatible}}{\bf q}(Q)\cdot{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\widehat{c}^{\ 1/s}_{1}\cdot(1+\varepsilon/3)^{\ell/s}\cdot\upz_{{\bf q},\ell}(P)\kern 5.0pt.

For the second equality we use the definition of matrix
{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}
.
The third equality follows from the definition of
\upz_{{\bf q},\ell}
in (
12.11
) and recalling that for
P
and
Q
which
are not compatible we have
{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}(P,Q)=0
.

Claim

12.2
follows.
∎

13.
Proof of
Theorem

8.4

Let us briefly recall the setting for
Theorem

8.4
. We have
w,u\in V\setminus\Lambda
,
such that
{\rm dist}(w,u)\geq 2k
and walk
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanates from
w
. Note that our assumptions imply
that
u\notin P
.

We apply the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction on
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
and obtain the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}

for tree
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
. We also use the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction for the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}

and get the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
for tree
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
.

We let set
{\tt A}_{P}
consist of each vertex in
T
which is a copy of a vertex
v\in P
. Also, let set
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}

consist of each vertex in
\overline{T}
which is either a copy of a vertex
v\in P
, or a copy of a split-vertex in
v\in\UpS_{P}
.

We use
Lemma

8.3
and identify
\overline{T}
as a subtree of
T
. This allows us to consider
(L,\eta)
as a pinning
at the vertices
T
and compare it with the pinning
(M,\sigma)
. Among other results, this is carried out in the following lemma.

Lemma 13.1
.

We identify
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
as a subtree of
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
and we consider
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
as a subset of vertices of
T

and

(L,\eta)
as a pinning of vertices in
T
.

Then, the following is true:

(1)

the pinnings
\sigma
and
\eta
agree on the assignment of the vertices in
L\cap M
,

(2)

we have
{\tt A}_{P}\cap\overline{T}=\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
,

(3)

we, also, have
L\setminus M\subseteq{\tt A}_{P}
and
M\cap\overline{T}\subseteq L
,

(4)

letting
K=L\cup\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
and
\widehat{\eta}\in\{\pm 1\}^{K}
, the distribution

\mu^{K,\widehat{\eta}}_{\overline{T}}
and the marginal of
\mu^{K,\widehat{\eta}}_{T}
at the subtree
\overline{T}
are identical.

The proof of
Lemma

13.1
appears in
Section

19.3
.

Let
\{\upalpha(e)\}
be the collection of weights over the edges of
T
that arise from
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
, i.e.,
as specified in (
5.6
).
Also, let
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
be the collection of weights over the edges of
\overline{T}
that arise from
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
.
We identify
\overline{T}
as a subtree of
T
and we endow
\overline{T}
with the collection of weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in\overline{T}}
.
Now, we have two sets of weights for the edges of
\overline{T}
, i.e.,
\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in\overline{T}}
and
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}_{e\in\overline{T}}
.

The following proposition implies that, provided that we have an appropriately contracting potential function
{\Psi}
,
for any edge
f\in\overline{T}
which is far from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, weights
\upalpha(f)
and
\upalpha_{P}(f)
are
close to each other. Recall that set
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
consists of each vertex in
\overline{T}
which is either a copy of a vertex

v\in P
or a copy of a split-vertex in
\UpS_{P}
.

Proposition 13.2
.

Let
{\Delta}>1
,
k\geq 1
and
N>1
be integers,
c>0
,
\uprho>1
,
s\geq 1
, while
let
b,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Also, let
\beta,\gamma,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
\gamma>0
,

0\leq\beta\leq\gamma
and
\lambda>0
.

Consider graph
G=(V,E)
of maximum degree
{\Delta}
such that

\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.
Assume that
\mu
, the Gibbs distribution on
G
specified by the parameters
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
,
is
b
-marginally bounded.
For
\delta=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}
, suppose that there
is a
(s,\delta,c)
-potential function
{\Psi}
with respect to
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
.

There exists a bounded number
\ell_{0}>2k
such that,
for any
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
,
for any
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
with
{\rm dist}(u,w)\geq 2k
and for
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}

that emanates from
w
the following is true:

For any edge
f
in
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
which is at distance
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, we have

(13.1)

\displaystyle\upalpha_{P}(f)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon/3)^{1/s}\cdot\upalpha(f)\kern 5.0pt,

where the set
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
and the weights
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
,
\{\upalpha(e)\}
are specified above.

The proof of
Proposition

13.2
appears in
Section

13.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

8.4
.

We consider path
W=e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}
from the root of
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
to a copy of vertex
w
.
Note that we specify
W
it terms of its edges.

Recall the two sets of edge-weights for
\overline{T}
, i.e., set
\{\upalpha(e)\}
is induced by the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
,
and set
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
is induced by the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
.

Also, recall that set
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
consists of each vertex in
\overline{T}
which is either a copy of a vertex

v\in P
, or a copy of a split-vertex of
v\in P
.

Let
\upalpha_{\rm min}=\min_{e}\{|\upalpha_{P}(e)|,|{\upalpha}(e)|\}
,
where
e
varies over the set of edges in
\overline{T}
such that
\upalpha_{P}(e),\upalpha(e)\neq 0
.
Similarly, we define
\upalpha_{\rm max}=\max_{e}\{|\upalpha_{P}(e)|,|\upalpha(e)|\}

where
e
varies over the same set of edges.
It is an easy exercise to show that our assumption about
b
-marginal boundedness, for fixed
b>0
,
implies that
\upalpha_{\rm min}
and
\upalpha_{\rm max}
are constants bounded away from zero.

Consider
\ell_{0}
as specified in
Proposition

13.2
.
Let set
H
consist of the edges
e_{i}\in W
such that
i>\ell-k
. Also, let set
K
consist of the edges
e_{i}\in W
such that

1\leq i\leq\ell-k
and
{\rm dist}(e_{i},\overline{{\tt A}}_{P})<\ell_{0}
. Similarly, let set
R
consist of the edges
e_{i}\in W
such
that
1\leq i\leq\ell-k
and
{\rm dist}(e_{i},\overline{{\tt A}}_{P})\geq\ell_{0}
.
We have

(13.2)

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|

\displaystyle=\prod\nolimits_{e_{i}\in H}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|\cdot\prod\nolimits_{e_{i}\in K}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|\cdot\prod\nolimits_{e_{i}\in R}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt.

For our subsequent arguments, we use the following result.

Claim 13.3
.

For
W=e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell}
and
\{\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})\}
,
\{\upalpha(e_{i})\}
,
the following is true: if
\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})\neq 0
, then we have
\upalpha(e_{i})\neq 0
, for each
e_{i}\in K\cup R
.

For
\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})
such that
e_{i}\in H
, we use the standard bound

(13.3)

\displaystyle|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq 1\kern 5.0pt.

For each edge
e_{i}\in K
, we have

(13.4)

\displaystyle|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|\leq\frac{\upalpha_{\rm max}}{\upalpha_{\rm min}}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\ \leq\ (1+\varepsilon/3)^{{1}/{s}}\cdot\frac{\upalpha_{\rm max}}{\upalpha_{\rm min}}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt.

We use
Claim

13.3
for the first inequality. The second inequality
uses that
1<(1+\varepsilon/2)^{{1}/{s}}
for
\varepsilon,s>0
.

Proposition

13.2
implies that, for every
e_{i}\in R
, we have

(13.5)

\displaystyle|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|\leq(1+\varepsilon/3)^{{1}/{s}}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging (
13.3
), (
13.4
) and (
13.5
)
into (
13.2
), we get

(13.6)

\displaystyle\prod^{\ell}_{i=1}|\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\upalpha_{\rm max}}{\upalpha_{\rm min}}\right)^{|K|}\cdot\prod^{\ell-k}_{i=1}(1+\varepsilon/3)^{{1}/{s}}\cdot|\upalpha(e_{i})|\kern 5.0pt.

For the cardinality of set
K
, we use the following claim.

Claim 13.4
.

There are at most
(k+1)\cdot{\Delta}^{\ell_{0}+1}
edges in path
W
that are at distance
<\ell_{0}
from set
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
.

The above implies that
|K|\leq(k+1)\cdot{\Delta}^{\ell_{0}+1}
.
Hence,
Theorem

8.4
follows from (
13.6
) by setting

\widehat{c}_{1}=\left(\frac{\upalpha_{\rm max}}{\upalpha_{\rm min}}\right)^{(k+1)\cdot{\Delta}^{\ell_{0}+1}}
.
∎

Proof of
Claim

13.3
.

We use
Lemma

8.3
and identify
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
as a subtree of
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)

such that both trees have the same root. Then,
W
is also a path in
T
starting from the root.

Having
\overline{T}
as a subtree of
T
, we endow the subset of edges of
T
that correspond to those of
\overline{T}

with the edge weights
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
. Note that we do not necessarily have
\upalpha_{P}(e)
for all edges
e\in T
.
Also, recall that the set of weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}
and
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
are induced by

\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
and
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
, respectively.

For
e_{i}\in W
, let
e_{i}=\{x_{i},z_{i}\}
while let
\uppi_{i}
be the set that consists of
x_{i},z_{i}
and their
children in
T
.
Furthermore, we have the following observations:

(1)

Lemma

13.1
implies that
{\tt A}_{P}\cap\overline{T}=\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
,

(2)

Lemma

8.3
implies that each vertex in
T\setminus\overline{T}
corresponds to a descendant of a vertex in
{\tt A}_{P}
,

(3)

Lemma

13.1
implies that
M\cap\overline{T}\subseteq L
,

(4)

Lemma

13.1
implies that the pinnings
\sigma
and
\eta
agree on the assignment in
L\cap M
.

Since we assume that our Gibbs distributions are
b
-marginally bounded, we can have
\upalpha(e_{i})=0
(resp.
\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})=0
) only if at least one vertex in

\uppi_{i}
has a fixed configuration under
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
(resp.
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
).
Note that for Gibbs distributions with parameter
\beta=0
, it is possible that a pinning on a child of
x_{i}
or
z_{i}

determines the configuration of its parent with probability 1, implying that
\upalpha(e_{i})=0
(resp.
\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})=0
).

Consider
1\leq i\leq\ell-k
. If there is
v\in\uppi_{i}
which is not in
\overline{T}
, this means that at least one of

x_{i}
and
z_{i}
is in
{\tt A}_{P}
, i.e., due to observation
2
.
But then, since
x_{i},z_{i}\in\overline{T}
, observation
1
implies that the parent of
v
, i.e., one of
x_{i},z_{i}
,
is in
{\tt A}_{P}\cap\overline{T}=\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
.
Since
i\leq\ell-k
, if any of
x_{i},z_{i}
is in
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, then it should be a split-vertex in
\UpS_{P}
, which means that is of degree
1
.
This can only happen if
i=1
, however in this case we have
x_{i},z_{i}\notin\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
because the root is a copy of vertex
u
which is
at distance at least
k
from any copy of a vertex
v\in\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
. We then conclude that this event cannot happen.

Hence, all elements in
\uppi_{i}
are vertices in
\overline{T}
. The fact that
e_{i}\in\overline{T}
and
observations
3
and
4
imply that if any vertex in
\uppi_{i}
is pinned, then the
pinning is the same under both
\sigma,\eta
. Hence, if
\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})\neq 0
, then we also have that
\upalpha(e_{i})\neq 0
.

Claim

13.3
follows.
∎

Proof of
Claim

13.4
.

It is not hard to verify that the number of edges at distance
\ell
from the vertices in
P\cup\UpS_{P}
in
{G}_{P}
is
the same as the number of edges at distance
\ell
from the vertices in
P
in graph
G
.

Furthermore, it is not hard to check that there are at most
{\Delta}^{\ell+1}
edges in
G
which are at distance

\ell
a from vertex
v\in P
. A simple geometric series calculation implies that the total number of edges at
distance
<\ell_{0}
from all vertices
v\in P
is at most
(k+1)\cdot{\Delta}^{\ell_{0}+1}
. Recall that there
are in total
k+1
vertices in
P
.

Hence, we conclude that the number of edges at distance
<\ell_{0}
from the vertices in
P\cup\UpS_{P}
in

{G}_{P}
is at most
(k+1)\cdot{\Delta}^{\ell_{0}+1}
. The claim follows by noting that each one of these,
at most
(k+1)\cdot{\Delta}^{\ell_{0}+1}
edges can have at most one copy in path
W
.
∎

13.1.
Proof of
Proposition

13.2

We use
Lemma

8.3
and identify
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
as a subtree of

T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
.

Recall that the edge-weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}
in
T
arise from
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
. Also, the
edge-weights
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
in
\overline{T}
arise from
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
. Having identified

\overline{T}
as a subtree of
T
, we endow
\overline{T}
with the edge-weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in\overline{T}}
.
Hence now we have two sets of weights for
\overline{T}
.

Also, recall that set
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
consists of each vertex in
\overline{T}
which is either a copy of vertex
v\in P
, or
a copy of a split-vertex in
\UpS_{P}
.

We start with the following claim.

Claim 13.5
.

For any edge
f
in
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
, at distance
\ell>10
from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
,
if
\upalpha(f)=0
, then
\upalpha_{P}(f)=0
.

Claim

13.5
implies that (
13.1
) is true for any edge
f
in
\overline{T}
at
distance
\ell>10
from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
such that
\upalpha(f)=0
. For the rest of the proof, we consider edge

f
in
\overline{T}
such that
\upalpha(f)\neq 0
which is at distance
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, where
the quantity
\ell_{0}>10
is specified later in the proof.

Let edge
f=\{v,x\}
while w.l.o.g. assume that
v
is a child of
x
. Also, let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}

be the children of
v
in
\overline{T}
. We take
f
at distance
\ell>10
from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
. This implies that
vertex
v
has the same set of children in both trees
T
and
\overline{T}
. If
v
had children in

T
which are not in
\overline{T}
, then according to
Lemma

8.3
, we would have had

v\in{\tt A}_{P}
. But since
v\in\overline{T}
,
Lemma

13.1
(statement

2
) implies that
v\in{\tt A}_{P}\cap\overline{T}=\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
.
This cannot happen since we assume that
f
is at distance at least
10
from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
. Hence,
v

indeed has the same set of children in both trees
T
and
\overline{T}
.

Let function
\Phi_{d}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be such that

(13.7)

\displaystyle{\bf x}

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{(\beta\gamma-1)\cdot F_{d}({\bf x})}{(\beta\cdot F_{d}({\bf x})+1)(F_{d}({\bf x})+\gamma)}\kern 5.0pt,

while
F_{d}({\bf x})=F_{d}({\bf x}_{1},\ldots,{\bf x}_{d})
is defined in (
3.2
).
Recall that
d
is the number of children of vertex
v
.

For each child
v_{i}
, consider the ratios of marginals
{R}^{M,\sigma}_{v_{i}}
and

\overline{{R}}^{L,\eta}_{v_{i}}
defined as described in
Section

3
.
Specifically, the ratio
{R}^{M,\sigma}_{v_{i}}
is defined with respect to the Gibbs distribution

\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
, while the ratio
\overline{{R}}^{L,\eta}_{v_{i}}
is defined with respect to the
Gibbs distribution
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
.

Using (
5.6
), it is not hard to verify that we have

\upalpha(f)=\Phi_{d}({R}^{M,\sigma}_{v_{1}},\ldots,{R}^{M,\sigma}_{v_{d}})

and similarly
\upalpha_{P}(f)=\Phi_{d}(\overline{{R}}^{L,\eta}_{v_{1}},\ldots,\overline{{R}}^{L,\eta}_{v_{d}})
.
From the
mean value theorem
, there exists
{\bf z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

(13.8)

\displaystyle\upalpha_{P}(f)

\displaystyle=\upalpha(f)+\sum^{d}_{i=1}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf x}_{i}}\Phi_{d}({\bf x})\right|_{{\bf x}={\bf z}}\cdot\left(\overline{{R}}^{L,\eta}_{v_{i}}-{R}^{M,\sigma}_{v_{i}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.
