Recall that we take
f
such that
\upalpha(f)\neq 0
. Our
b
-boundedness assumption, for fixed

b>0
, and having
\upalpha(f)\neq 0
, imply that
|\upalpha(f)|
is a constant bounded away
from zero. Hence, (
13.1
) is true if the sum on the r.h.s. in (
13.8
)
is a sufficiently small constant.

A standard calculation implies that for
i=1,\ldots,d
, we have

(13.9)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf x}_{i}}\Phi_{d}({\bf x})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{d}({\bf x})\cdot{\textstyle\frac{\beta\gamma-1}{(\beta{\bf x}_{i}+1)({\bf x}_{i}+\gamma)}}\cdot{\textstyle\left(1-\frac{1+\beta\gamma+2F_{d}({\bf x})}{\beta\gamma-1}\cdot\Phi_{d}({\bf x})\right)}\kern 5.0pt.

The above implies that
\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf x}_{i}}\Phi_{d}({\bf x})
is bounded only
if both
\Phi_{d}({\bf x})
and
F_{d}({\bf x})
are bounded for any
{\bf x}\in[0,\infty]^{d}
and

d\in[{\Delta}]
.
From the definition of the functions
\Phi_{d}({\bf x})
and
F_{d}({\bf x})
, it follows that they are indeed bounded, implying that

\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf x}_{i}}\Phi_{d}({\bf x})
is also bounded for
i=1,\ldots,d
.

In light of all the above, (
13.1
) follows by showing that for any fixed number

\upkappa>0
, there is
\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(\upkappa)
such that for any edge

f
at distance
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
from set
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, we have

(13.10)

\displaystyle|\overline{{R}}^{L,\eta}_{v_{i}}-{R}^{M,\sigma}_{v_{i}}|

\displaystyle\leq\upkappa

\displaystyle\textrm{for }1\leq i\leq d\kern 5.0pt.

Note that assuming that
f
is at distance
\ell
from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
implies that the ball of radius

\ell-1
from each vertex
v_{i}
is inside tree
\overline{T}
. This follows from

Lemmas

8.3
and
13.1
.

Furthermore, statement
3
in
Lemma

13.1
implies that there is a pair of
configurations
\xi^{(i)}_{1}
and
\xi^{(i)}_{2}
at
N=L\cup\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, which only disagree at
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, such that

(13.11)

\displaystyle|\overline{{R}}^{L,\eta}_{v_{i}}-{R}^{M,\sigma}_{v_{i}}|

\displaystyle\leq|\overline{{R}}^{N,\xi^{(i)}_{1}}_{v_{i}}-\overline{{R}}^{N,\xi^{(i)}_{2}}_{v_{i}}|

\displaystyle\textrm{for }1\leq i\leq d\kern 5.0pt,

where
\overline{{R}}^{N,\xi^{(i)}_{1}}_{z}
and
\overline{{R}}^{N,\xi^{(i)}_{2}}_{z}
are the ratios of marginals at vertex
z
with respect
to the Gibbs distributions
\mu^{N,\xi^{(i)}_{1}}_{\overline{T}}
and
\mu^{N,\xi^{(i)}_{2}}_{\overline{T}}
, respectively.
We bound the r.h.s. of (
13.11
) by using the following result.

Claim 13.6
.

For
\delta=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}
, suppose that there
is a
(s,\delta,c)
-potential function
{\Psi}
with respect to
(\beta,\gamma,\lambda)
,
where
\varepsilon,\beta,\gamma,\lambda,\uprho
are defined in
Proposition

13.2
.

Then, there are constants
c_{A},c_{B}>0
and
t_{0}>0
, such that for any vertex
z
in tree
\overline{T}
which is at
distance
\ell\geq t_{0}
from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, the following is true:

For
N=L\cup\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
and for any two configurations
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}^{N}

that differ only at
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, we have

(13.12)

\displaystyle|\overline{{R}}^{N,\eta_{1}}_{z}-\overline{{R}}^{N,\eta_{2}}_{z}|\leq c_{A}\cdot\exp\left(-c_{B}\cdot\ell\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
\overline{{R}}^{N,\eta_{1}}_{z}
and
\overline{{R}}^{N,\eta_{2}}_{z}
are the ratios of marginals at vertex
z
with respect
to the Gibbs distributions
\mu^{N,\eta_{1}}_{\overline{T}}
and
\mu^{N,\eta_{2}}_{\overline{T}}
, respectively.

We get (
13.10
) from (
13.11
) and
Claim

13.6
.

Proposition

13.2
follows.
\square

13.1.1.
Proof of
Claim

13.5

The proof is not too different from that of
Claim

13.3

In this proof, we identify
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
as a subtree of
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
.
W.l.o.g., let edge
f
in
\overline{T}
be such that
f=\{v,x\}
. Also, let
\uppi
be the set of vertices that consists
of
v,x
and their children in
T
.

Arguing as in
Claim

13.3
, the assumption of having
f
at distance
>10
from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
implies
that all elements in
\uppi
are vertices in
\overline{T}
.

Our assumption for
b
-boundedness, for fixed
b>0
, implies that for edge
f
in
\overline{T}
, we either
have
\upalpha(f)=0
, or
|\upalpha(f)|
is bounded away from zero. The same holds for

\upalpha_{P}(f)
. Furthermore, we have
\upalpha(f)=0
(resp.
\upalpha_{P}(f)=0
) only if at
least one of the vertices in
\uppi
has a pinning under
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
(resp.
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
).
Note that for the case where the parameter of the Gibbs distribution
\beta=0
, it is possible that by pinning one
of the children of
v
or
x
we get
\upalpha(f)=0
(resp.
\upalpha_{P}(f)=0
).

Recall from
Lemma

13.1
, statement
3
,
that the pinnings imposed by
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
and
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
on the vertices in

\overline{T}
can only differ on the vertices in
L\setminus M\subseteq{\tt A}_{P}
. But since
L\setminus M\subseteq\overline{T}
,
we have that
L\setminus M\subseteq\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, i.e., since statement
2
in

Lemma

13.1
implies that
{\tt A}_{P}\cap\overline{T}=\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
.

Taking
f
at a sufficiently large distance from
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
, e.g., distance greater than
10
, we have that
\uppi\not\subset\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
.
Hence, if the two distributions
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
and
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
impose pinnings to vertices in
set
\uppi
, these pinning should be identical.

This leads us to conclude that if
\upalpha(f)=0
, this is because
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}
fixes the configuration
of a vertex in
\uppi
. But then,
\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
imposes identical pinning to
\uppi
. Hence, we also have

\upalpha_{P}(f)=0
.

All the above conclude the proof of
Claim

13.5
.
\square

13.1.2.
Proof of
Claim

13.6

In this proof, we identify
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
as a subtree of
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
.

Firstly, we upper bound the number of paths in
\overline{T}
from vertex
z
to the set of vertices
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
as a function of

\ell
, i.e., the distance of
z
from the set. If
z
is a copy of vertex
\widehat{z}
in
{G}_{P}
, then the paths from

z
to
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
correspond to the self-avoiding walks from
\widehat{z}
to the set of vertices
P\cup\UpS_{P}
.
Let
K(\ell)
be the number of these walks. We assume that
\ell\geq t_{0}
for a large constant
t_{0}
, e.g.,
t_{0}>100k
.

Note that
K(\ell)
is upper bounded by the number of self-avoiding walks in
G
from vertex
\widehat{z}
to
P
.
Furthermore, since each self-avoiding path of length
\ell>k
is also a
k
-non-backtracking walk, we have

(13.13)

\displaystyle K(\ell)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{W,Q}\UpH_{G,k}^{\ell-k}(W,Q)\kern 5.0pt.

Variable
W
varies over the walks in
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
which emanate from vertex
\widehat{z}
. Variable
Q
varies over

{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
such that its last vertex is in
P
. The exponent
\ell-k
has to do with how we measure distances
between
k
-non-backtracking walks with
\UpH_{G,k}
.

A crude estimation implies that the number of summands in
(
13.13
) is
\leq(k+2)\cdot{\Delta}^{k}
.
We have

(13.14)

\displaystyle K(\ell)

\displaystyle\leq(k+2)\cdot{\Delta}^{k}\cdot\max\nolimits_{W,Q}\{\UpH_{G,k}^{\ell-k}(W,Q)\}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore,
Claims

5.7
and
5.8
imply that for any
\zeta\in(0,1)
,
there exists integer
\hat{r}_{0}=\hat{r}_{0}(N,{\Delta},k,\zeta)>1
such that for any
r\geq\hat{r}_{0}
we have

(13.15)

\displaystyle\UpH_{G,k}^{r}(W,Q)\leq\left((1+\zeta)\cdot\uprho\right)^{r}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
.
Combining (
13.14
) and (
13.15
), for
\ell\geq\max\{\hat{r}_{0},k\}
, we have

(13.16)

\displaystyle K(\ell)

\displaystyle\leq(k+2)\cdot{\Delta}^{k}\cdot\left((1+\zeta)\cdot\uprho\right)^{\ell-k}\kern 5.0pt.

We choose
\zeta
such that
\zeta=(1+\varepsilon/2)(1-\varepsilon/2)
, where
\varepsilon
is from the
parameter
\delta
of the
(s,\delta,c)
-potential function
{\Psi}
, i.e., recall that we have
\delta=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}
.
We choose
t_{0}
to be the maximum between the quantities
2\varepsilon^{-1}\log((k+2)\cdot{\Delta}^{k})

and
\hat{r}_{0}
.
With our choices of
\zeta
and
t_{0}
, for any
\ell\geq t_{0}
, we have

K(\ell)\leq\left((1+\varepsilon/2)\cdot\uprho\right)^{\ell}
.

With the above in mind, standard arguments imply that for (
13.12
), it suffices to show
that there exists a differentiable, increasing potential function
\Upxi:[-\infty,+\infty]\to(-\infty,+\infty)
such that

(13.17)

\displaystyle\phi\left(F({\bf z})\right)\cdot\sum^{d}_{i=1}\frac{1}{\phi({\bf z}_{i})}\cdot\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf x}_{i}}F({\bf x})\right|_{{\bf x}={\bf z}}\cdot{\bf m}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}\right)^{1/s}\cdot\|{\bf m}\|_{s}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\phi=\Upxi^{\prime}
.
Using the standard chain rule for derivatives, (see also
[
10
]
) we conclude that (
13.17
) is true by using

\Upxi={\Psi}\circ\log
, where
{\Psi}
is the
(s,\delta,c)
-potential function.
Claim

13.6
follows.

\square

14.
Proof of rapid mixing results in
Section

1
- Hard-core Model

We start by introducing the potential function

{\Psi}
. We define
{\Psi}
in terms of
\chi={\Psi}^{\prime}
by having

(14.1)

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}

such that

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\mapsto{\textstyle{\sqrt{\frac{e^{y}}{1+e^{y}}}}}\kern 5.0pt,

while
{\Psi}(0)=0
.

The potential function
{\Psi}
was proposed in a more general form in
[
10
]
.
It is standard to show that
{\Psi}
is well-defined (see also
[
10
]
).

For any given
\lambda>0
, we define, implicitly, function
{\Delta_{c}}(\lambda)
to be the positive
number
z>1
such that
\frac{z^{z}}{(z-1)^{(z+1)}}=\lambda
.
From its definition it is not hard to see that
{\Delta_{c}}(\cdot)
is the inverse map of
{\lambda_{c}}(\cdot)
, i.e., we have that

{\Delta_{c}}(x)={\lambda_{c}}^{-1}(x)
. In that respect,
{\Delta_{c}}(x)
is well-defined as
{\lambda_{c}}(x)
is monotonically
decreasing in
x
.

Theorem 14.1
.

For
\lambda>0
, let
{\Delta_{c}}={\Delta_{c}}(\lambda)
. The function
{\Psi}
defined in (
14.1
) is a

(s,\delta,c)
-potential function such that

(14.2)

\displaystyle s^{-1}

\displaystyle=1-{\textstyle\frac{{\Delta_{c}}-1}{2}}\log\left(1+\textstyle{\frac{1}{{\Delta_{c}}-1}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\leq{\textstyle\frac{1}{{\Delta_{c}}}}

and

\displaystyle c\leq{\textstyle\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda}}\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of
Theorem

14.1
appears in
Section

16
.
We also have the following claim.

Claim 14.2
.

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
R\geq 2
and
0<\lambda<(1-\varepsilon){\lambda_{c}}(R)
the following is true:
There is
0<z<1
, which only depend on
\varepsilon
, such that for
{\Delta_{c}}={\Delta_{c}}(\lambda)
, we have

(14.3)

\displaystyle{\textstyle\frac{1-z}{R}}

\displaystyle\geq{\textstyle\frac{1}{{\Delta_{c}}}}

and

\displaystyle{\textstyle\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda}}

\displaystyle<\textstyle{\frac{e^{3}}{R}}\kern 5.0pt.

Claim

14.2
follows from elementary calculations,
for a proof see
Section

A.5
.

14.1.
Proof of
Theorem

1.2

Combining
Theorem

14.1
,
Theorem

3.7
and
Claim

14.2
,
with our assumption that
{\Delta}
and
\uprho
being
\Theta(1)
,
we have

\displaystyle{\rho}{\textstyle\left({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\right)}

\displaystyle=O(1)\kern 5.0pt,

for any
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
.
Furthermore, our assumptions about
\lambda
imply that
\mu
is
b
-marginally bounded,
for fixed
b>0
.

Considering all the above,
Theorem

1.2
follows as a corollary from
Theorem

5.4
.

\square
