Proof of
Theorem

1.7

The proof of
Theorem

1.7
is almost identical to that of
Theorem

1.2
.

Combining
Theorem

14.1
,
Theorem

3.6
and
Claim

14.2
,
with our assumption about
{\Delta}
and
{\varrho}
being
\Theta(1)
,
we have

\displaystyle{\rho}\left({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\right)

\displaystyle=O(1)\kern 5.0pt,

for any
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
.
Furthermore, our assumptions about
\lambda
imply that
\mu
is
b
-marginally bounded,
for fixed
b>0
.

Considering all the above,
Theorem

1.7
follows as a corollary from
Theorem

5.4
.

\square

14.3.
Proof of
Theorem

1.1

To prove
Theorem

1.1
, we use the following proposition.

Proposition 14.3
.

For integers
k,N>0
and graph
G=(V,E)
, we have that

\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|_{2}\leq\left(\upd_{k}\right)^{N+k}
.

The proof of
Proposition

14.3
appears in
Section

14.4
.

Given the parameters
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\upd_{k}
,
let
z=z(\varepsilon,\upd_{k})>0
be such that

(14.4)

\displaystyle{\lambda_{c}}((1+z)\upd_{k})=(1-\varepsilon/3){\lambda_{c}}(\upd_{k})\kern 5.0pt.

Such
z
exists and it is unique, since
{\lambda_{c}}(x)
is monotonically decreasing in
x
.

Proposition

14.3
implies that there exists
M=M(z,\upd_{k})
such that for any
N\geq M
,
we have

(14.5)

\displaystyle\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}\leq\left(\upd_{k}\right)^{\frac{N+k}{N}}\leq(1+z)\upd_{k}\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\uprho=\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{M}\|^{1/M}_{2}
, where
M
is defined above. From (
14.4
) and (
14.5
)
we have

(14.6)

\displaystyle{\lambda_{c}}(\uprho)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\varepsilon/3){\lambda_{c}}(\upd_{k})\kern 5.0pt.

For the above, we use once more that
{\lambda_{c}}(x)
is monotonically decreasing in
x
.

Theorem

1.1
follows by noting that the above implies that for
\lambda<(1-\varepsilon){\lambda_{c}}(\upd_{k})
we also have that

\lambda<(1-\varepsilon/3){\lambda_{c}}(\uprho)
. For such
\lambda
,
Theorem

1.2
implies that
there is a constant
C>0
, such that Glauber dynamics on the Hard-core model on
G

has mixing time at most
Cn\log n
.

\square

14.4.
Proof of
Proposition

14.3

Let integer
M>1
which we will specify later. Let a length
k\cdot M
walk

P=w_{0},\ldots,w_{k\cdot M}
in
G
. We say that
P
is
k
-wise self-avoiding,
if the sub-walk
P_{i}=w_{i\cdot k},w_{i\cdot k+1},\ldots,w_{i\cdot k+k}
is
self-avoiding, for all
i=0,\ldots,M-1
. Note that
P
is not necessarily a self-avoiding walk
since the same vertex
v
can appear in both
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
, for
i\neq j
.

For each
v\in V
, let
\uppi_{k}(v,kM)
be the set of
k
-wise self-avoiding walks of length

k\cdot M
that emanate from
v
. Let
\upchi_{k,M}=\sup\{\uppi_{k}(v,kM)\ |\ v\in V\}
.
It is an easy exercise to show that

(14.7)

\displaystyle\upchi_{k,M}

\displaystyle\leq(\upd_{k})^{kM}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, let
Q=x_{0},\ldots,x_{kM}
be a
k
-non-backtracking walk in
G
.
Q
is also a
k
-wise
self-avoiding walk. To see this, note that for all
j=0,\ldots,k\cdot(M-1)
, the sub-walk
x_{j},\ldots,x_{j+k}

in
Q
is self-avoiding. For
Q
to be
k
-wise self-avoiding, we only need the sub-walks
x_{j},\ldots,x_{j+k}

to be self-avoiding, for
j
being a multiple of
k
. Hence,
Q
is a
k
-wise self-avoiding walk.

For
W,S\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
, i.e., two self-avoiding walks of length
k
in
G
, recall that
\UpH_{G,k}^{k(M-1)}(W,S)
is equal to the number of

k
-non-backtracking walks of length
k\cdot(M-1)
that start from
W
and end at
S
.

Every
k
-non-backtracking walk of length
k\cdot(M-1)
from
W
to
S
is also a
k
-wise self-avoiding
walk of length
k\cdot M
starting at
v
, the first vertex in
W
. Hence, we have

(14.8)

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{S\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}\UpH_{G,k}^{k(M-1)}(W,S)\leq\uppi_{k}(v,kM)\leq\upchi_{k,M}\kern 5.0pt.

From all the above, we obtain

\displaystyle\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{k(M-1)}\cdot\UpR\|_{\infty}=\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{k(M-1)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\upchi_{k,M}=(\upd_{k})^{kM}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\UpR
is the involution defined in (
5.5
).
For any integer
\ell>0
, we have that

(14.9)

\displaystyle\|((\UpH_{G,k})^{k(M-1)}\cdot\UpR)^{\ell}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{k(M-1)}\cdot\UpR\|^{\ell}_{\infty}\leq(\upd_{k})^{k\cdot M\cdot\ell}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that
(\UpH_{G,k})^{k(M-1)}\cdot\UpR
is a symmetric matrix, e.g., see (
5.10
).
From Gelfand’s formula e.g., see
[
30
]
, we get

\displaystyle\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{k(M-1)}\cdot\UpR\|_{2}=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\|{\textstyle((\UpH_{G,k})^{k(M-1)}\cdot\UpR)^{\ell}}\|^{1/\ell}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left((\upd_{k})^{k\cdot M\cdot\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}=(\upd_{k})^{k\cdot M}\kern 5.0pt.

where the second derivation follows from (
14.9
).
Since
\UpR
is an involution, we have
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{k(M-1)}\cdot\UpR\|_{2}=\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{k(M-1)}\|_{2}
.
From the above, we conclude that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{k(M-1)}\|_{2}\leq\left(\upd_{k}\right)^{k\cdot M}
.

Proposition

14.3
follows by setting
M=\frac{N}{k}+1
.

\square

15.
Proof of results in
Section

1
- Ising Model

For
d>0
, consider the function
H_{d}
defined in (
3.3
).
For the Ising model, this correspond to the function
H_{d}:[-\infty,+\infty]^{d}\to[-\infty,+\infty]
such that

(15.1)

\displaystyle(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\mapsto\log\lambda+\sum\nolimits_{i\in[d]}\log\left(\frac{\beta\exp(x_{i})+1}{\exp(x_{i})+\beta}\right)\kern 4.62497pt.

The following is a folklore result.

Lemma 15.1
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
d>0
,
R>1
and
\beta\in{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(R,\varepsilon)

we have

(15.2)

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{\bf y}\|\nabla H_{d}({\bf y})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq{\frac{1-\varepsilon}{R}}\kern 4.62497pt.

For the sake of our paper being self-contained, we present a proof of
Lemma

15.1
in

Section

A.4
.

15.1.
Proof of
Theorem

1.5

For our choice of
\beta
and
\lambda
,
Lemma

15.1
implies
that the corresponding Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
exhibits
\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\uprho}\right)
-contraction
for any
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
. That is,

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{\bf y}\|\nabla H_{d}({\bf y})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq({1-\varepsilon})/{\uprho}

\displaystyle\forall d\in[{\Delta}]\kern 5.0pt.

The above, together with
Theorem

3.4
, imply that
for any
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
, the pairwise influence
matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, induced by
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, satisfies that

\displaystyle{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})\leq\widehat{C}\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}\kern 5.0pt,

for fixed number
\widehat{C}>0
. Clearly, we have
{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})\in O(1)
.

Furthermore, our assumptions about
\beta
and
\lambda
imply that
\mu_{G}
is
b
-marginally bounded,
for fixed
b>0
.

Considering all the above,
Theorem

1.5
follows as a corollary from
Theorem

5.4
.

\square

15.2.
Proof of
Theorem

1.8

The proof of
Theorem

1.8
is almost identical to
Theorem

1.5
.

For our choice of
\beta
and
\lambda
,
Lemma

15.1
implies
that the corresponding Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
exhibits
\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon}{{\varrho}}\right)
-contraction
for all
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
. That is,

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{\bf y}\|\nabla H_{d}({\bf y})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\varepsilon)/{\varrho}

\displaystyle\forall d\in[{\Delta}]\kern 5.0pt.

The above, combined with
Theorem

3.2
imply that
for
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Lambda}
, the pairwise influence
matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, induced by
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, satisfies that

\displaystyle{\rho}({\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G})\leq\varepsilon^{-1}=O(1)\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, our assumptions about
\beta
and
\lambda
imply that
\mu
is
b
-marginally bounded,
for fixed
b>0
.

Considering all the above,
Theorem

1.8
follows as a corollary from
Theorem

5.4
.

\square

15.3.
Proof of
Theorem

1.4

The proof of
Theorem

1.4
is conceptually similar to
Theorem

1.1
.
We are using
Proposition

14.3
once more in this proof.

It is straightforward to show that for any
z<\hat{z}
, we have
{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(\hat{z},\delta)\subset{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(z,\delta)
.
Similarly, for any
\delta<\hat{\delta}
, we have
{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(z,\hat{\delta})\subset{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(z,\delta)
.

Given the parameters
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\upd_{k}
,
let
z=z(\varepsilon,\upd_{k})>0
be such that

(15.3)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(\upd_{k},\varepsilon)={\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}((1+z)\upd_{k},\varepsilon/3)\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore,
Proposition

14.3
implies that there exists
M=M(z,\upd_{k})
such that for any
N\geq M
,
we have

(15.4)

\displaystyle\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}\leq\left(\upd_{k}\right)^{\frac{N+k}{N}}\leq(1+z)\upd_{k}\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\uprho=\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{M}\|^{1/M}_{2}
, where
M
is defined above. The above relations imply that

(15.5)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(\upd_{k},\varepsilon)\subseteq{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(\uprho,\varepsilon/3)\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem

1.4
follows by noting that the above relation implies that for
\beta\in{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(\upd_{k},\varepsilon)
,
we also have
\beta\in{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(\uprho,\varepsilon/3)
. For such
\beta
and for
\lambda>0
,
Theorem

1.4
implies that there is
a constant
C>0
, such that Glauber dynamics on the Ising model on
G
has mixing time at most
Cn\log n
.

\square

16.
Proof of
Theorem

14.1

Recall that the ratio of Gibbs marginals
{R}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{u}
, defined in

Section

3
, is possible to be equal to zero, or
\infty
. Typically, this
happens if vertex
u
with respect to which we consider the ratio is pinned, i.e.,
u\in\Lambda
,
or has a neighbour in
\Lambda
. When we deal with the Hard-core model,
there is a standard way to avoid these infinities and zeros in our calculations and make the derivation
much simpler.

Suppose we have the Hard-core model with fugacity
\lambda>0
on a tree
T
, while at the
set of vertices
\Lambda
we have a pinning
\tau
. Then, it is elementary that
this distribution is identical to the Hard-core model with the same fugacity on the tree (or forest)
T^{\prime}

which is obtained from
T
by removing every vertex
w
which either belongs to
\Lambda
, or
has a neighbour in
\Lambda
which is pinned to 1.

From now on, for the instances we consider, assume that we have applied the above steps and
removed any pinnings.

It is useful to write down the functions that arise from the tree recursions
in
Section

3
, specifically for
the Hard-core model with fugacity
\lambda
.
Recall that, in this case, we have
\beta=0
and
\gamma=1
.
In the following definitions, we assume that there are no pinnings.

For integer
d\geq 1
, we have
F_{d}:\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}\to(0,\lambda)
such that
for any
{\bf x}=({\bf x}_{1},\ldots,{\bf x}_{d})
, we have

(16.1)

\displaystyle{\bf x}\mapsto\lambda\prod\nolimits_{i\in[d]}\frac{1}{{\bf x}_{i}+1}\kern 4.62497pt.

We also define
F_{d,{\rm sym}}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to(0,\lambda)
the
symmetric
version
of the above function, that is

(16.2)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto F_{d}(x,x,\ldots,x)\kern 5.0pt.

We also have
H_{d}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for any

{\bf x}=({\bf x}_{1},\ldots,{\bf x}_{d})
, we have

(16.3)

\displaystyle{\bf x}\mapsto\log\lambda+\sum\nolimits_{i\in[d]}\log\left(\frac{1}{\exp({\bf x}_{i})+1}\right)\kern 4.62497pt.

For
\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf x}_{i}}H_{d}({\bf x})=h({\bf x}_{i})
,
we have
h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(16.4)

\displaystyle{\bf x}\mapsto-\frac{e^{{\bf x}_{i}}}{e^{{\bf x}_{i}}+1}\kern 4.62497pt.

Finally, the set of log-ratios
J
, defined in (
3.8
),
satisfies that

(16.5)

\displaystyle J=(-\infty,\log(\lambda))\kern 5.0pt.

Set
S_{{\Psi}}
, i.e., the image of
{\Psi}
, satisfies
S_{{\Psi}}=(-\infty,\infty)
.

Theorem

14.1
follows by showing that
{\Psi}
satisfies the Contraction
and the Boundedness conditions of
Definition

3.5
, for the parameters indicated in the statement.

We start with the
Contraction
. For any integer
d>0
, we let

{\mathcal{R}}_{d}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be such that
for
{\bf m}=({\bf m}_{1},\ldots,{\bf m}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
, and

{\bf y}=({\bf y}_{1},\ldots,{\bf y}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{d}({\bf m},{\bf y})={\textstyle\chi(H_{d}({\bf y}))}\sum^{d}_{j=1}\frac{|h({\bf y}_{j})|}{\chi({\bf y}_{j})}\times{\bf m}_{j}\kern 4.62497pt,

where recall that
\chi={\Psi}^{\prime}
.

The following result implies that
{\Psi}
satisfies the contraction condition.

Proposition 16.1

(Contraction)
.

For
\delta
and
s
as defined in
Theorem

14.1
, the following is true:
for any integer
d>0
, for
{\bf m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
, we have

(16.6)

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{{\bf y}\in(Q_{{\Psi}})^{d}}\{{\mathcal{R}}_{d}({\bf m},{\bf y})\}\leq\delta^{\frac{1}{s}}\cdot\|{\bf m}\|_{s}\kern 5.0pt,

where
Q_{{\Psi}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
contains every
y\in\mathbb{R}
such that there is

\tilde{y}\in S_{{\Psi}}
for which we have
{y}={\Psi}^{-1}(\tilde{y})
.

The proof of
Proposition

16.1
appears in
Section

16.1
.

We now focus on establishing the
Boundedness
property of
{\Psi}
.

Lemma 16.2

(Boundedness)
.

We have that
\textstyle\max_{y_{1},y_{2}\in J}\left\{\chi(y_{2})\cdot\frac{|h(y_{1})|}{\chi(y_{1})}\right\}\leq\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda}
.

Proof.

Using the definitions of the functions
\chi
and
h
from (
16.8
) and
(
16.4
), respectively, we have

\displaystyle\max_{y_{1},y_{2}\in J}\left\{\chi(y_{2})\cdot\frac{|h(y_{1})|}{\chi(y_{1})}\right\}

\displaystyle=\max_{y_{1},y_{2}\in J}\left\{\sqrt{h(y_{1})h(y_{2})}\right\}\ =\ \max_{y_{1},y_{2}\in J}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{e^{y_{1}}}{1+e^{y_{1}}}\frac{e^{y_{2}}}{1+e^{y_{2}}}}\right\}\ =\ {\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda}}\kern 4.62497pt.

The last inequality follows from the observation that the function
g(x)=\frac{e^{x}}{1+e^{x}}
is increasing in
x
,
while, from (
16.5
), we have that
e^{y_{1}},e^{y_{2}}\leq\lambda
.
The claim follows.
∎

In light of
Proposition

16.1
and
Lemma

16.2
,
Theorem

14.1
follows.

\square

16.1.
Proof of
Proposition

16.1

The proposition follows by using results from
[
35
]
. However, in order to apply these results, we need
to bring
{\mathcal{R}}_{d}({\bf m},{\bf y})
into an appropriate form.

For any
d>0
, we let
{\mathcal{J}}_{d}:\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
be such that
for
{\bf m}=({\bf m}_{1},\ldots,{\bf m}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
and
{\bf z}=({\bf z}_{1},\ldots,{\bf z}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{d}({\bf m},{\bf z})

\displaystyle=\chi(\log F_{d}({\bf z}))\sum\nolimits_{j\in[d]}\frac{|h(\log{\bf z}_{j})|}{\chi(\log{\bf z}_{j})}\times{\bf m}_{j}\kern 4.62497pt.

Using the definitions in (
16.1
) and (
16.3
), it is elementary to verify that for any
d>0
, for any

{\bf m}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
,
{\bf z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}
and
{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
{\bf z}_{j}=e^{{\bf y}_{j}}
,
we have that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{d}({\bf m},{\bf z})={\mathcal{R}}_{d}({\bf m},{\bf y})\kern 5.0pt.

In light of the above, the proposition follows by showing that

(16.7)

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{{\bf z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}}\{{\mathcal{J}}_{d}({\bf m},{\bf z})\}\leq\delta^{{1}/{s}}\cdot\|{\bf m}\|_{s}\kern 5.0pt,

for
s
and
\delta
indicated in
Theorem

14.1
.

In order to prove (
16.7
), we let
{\psi}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}
be such that

(16.8)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1}{y(1+y)}}\kern 4.62497pt.

Claim 16.3
.

For any
{\bf m}=({\bf m}_{1},\ldots,{\bf m}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
and

{\bf z}=({\bf z}_{1},\ldots,{\bf z}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}
,
we have

(16.9)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{d}({\bf m},{\bf z})

\displaystyle={\psi}(F_{d}({\bf z}))\times\sum\nolimits_{i\in[d]}\frac{{\bf m}_{i}}{{\psi}({\bf z}_{i})}\left.\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf x}_{i}}F_{d}({\bf x})\right|_{{\bf x}={\bf z}}\right|\kern 5.0pt,

where
F_{d}
and
{\psi}
are defined
in (
16.1
) and (
16.8
), respectively.

Proof.

The claim follows by using simple rearrangements. We have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{d}({\bf m},{\bf z})

\displaystyle=\chi\left(\log F_{d}({\bf z})\right)\sum\nolimits_{j\in[d]}\frac{|h(\log{\bf z}_{j})|}{\chi\left(\log{\bf z}_{j}\right)}\times{\bf m}_{j}

(16.10)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{F_{d}({\bf z})}{1+F_{d}(\bf z)}}\sum^{d}_{j=1}\sqrt{\frac{{\bf z}_{j}}{1+{\bf z}_{j}}}\times{\bf m}_{j}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{F_{d}({\bf z})(1+F_{d}(\bf z))}}\sum^{d}_{j=1}\sqrt{{\bf z}_{j}(1+{\bf z}_{j})}\times\frac{F_{d}(\bf z)}{1+{\bf z}_{j}}\times{\bf m}_{j}\kern 5.0pt.

In (
16.10
), we substitute
\chi
and
h
according to
(
14.1
) and (
16.4
), respectively.
Using the definition of
{\psi}
from (
16.8
), we get that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}_{d}({\bf m},{\bf z})

\displaystyle={\psi}(F_{d}({\bf z}))\sum\nolimits_{j\in[d]}\frac{1}{{\psi}({\bf z}_{j})}\times\frac{F_{d}(\bf z)}{1+{\bf z}_{i}}\times{\bf m}_{j}\kern 5.0pt.

The above implies (
16.9
), i.e., since

\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf x}_{i}}F_{d}({\bf x})\right|=\frac{F_{d}({\bf x})}{1+{\bf x}_{i}}
,
for any
i\in[d]
.
The claim follows.
∎

In light of
Claim

16.3
, (
16.7
) follows by showing that
for any
{\bf m}=({\bf m}_{1},\ldots,{\bf m}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
and

{\bf z}=({\bf z}_{1},\ldots,{\bf z}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}
we have

(16.11)

\displaystyle{\psi}(F_{d}({\bf z}))\times\sum\nolimits_{i\in[d]}\frac{{\bf m}_{i}}{{\psi}({\bf z}_{i})}\left.\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial{\bf x}_{i}}F_{d}({\bf x})\right|_{{\bf x}={\bf z}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{\frac{1}{s}}\cdot\|{\bf m}\|_{s}\kern 5.0pt.

From our choice of the potential function, the above follows by using standard results form
[
35
]
.
For any
s\geq 1,d>0
and
x\geq 0
, we let the function

\displaystyle{\Xi}(s,d,x)=d^{-1}\cdot\left(\frac{{\psi}(F_{d,{\rm sym}}(x))}{{\psi}(x)}F^{\prime}_{d,{\rm sym}}(x)\right)^{s}\kern 5.0pt,

where the functions
F_{d,{\rm sym}}
,
{\psi}
are defined in (
16.2
) and (
16.8
),
respectively.

Lemma 16.4

(
[
35
]
)
.

For any
\lambda>0
, for integer
d\geq 1
, for
s\geq 1
, for
{\bf x}=({\bf x}_{1},\ldots,{\bf x}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{>0}

and
{\bf m}=({\bf m}_{1},\ldots,{\bf m}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{\geq 0}
, the following holds:
there exists
\bar{x}>0
and integer
0\leq k\leq d
such that

\displaystyle{\psi}(F_{d}({\bf x}))\times\sum\nolimits_{i\in[d]}\frac{{\bf m}_{i}}{{\psi}({\bf x}_{i})}\left|\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}F_{d}({\bf z})\right|_{{\bf z}={\bf x}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left({\Xi}(s,k,\bar{x})\right)^{1/s}\times\|{\bf m}\|_{s}\kern 5.0pt.

In light of the above lemma, our proposition follows as a corollary from the following result.

Lemma 16.5

(
[
35
]
)
.

For
\lambda>0
, consider
{\Delta_{c}}={\Delta_{c}}(\lambda)
and
F_{{\Delta_{c}},{\rm sym}}
with fugacity
\lambda
.
Let
s\geq 1
be such that

\displaystyle s^{-1}

\displaystyle=1-{\textstyle\frac{{\Delta_{c}}-1}{2}}\cdot\log\left(1+{\textstyle\frac{1}{{\Delta_{c}}-1}}\right)\kern 4.62497pt.

For any
x>0
,
d>0
, we have that
{\Xi}(s,d,x)\leq({\Delta_{c}})^{-1}
.

By combining
Lemmas

16.4
and
16.5
we get
(
16.11
) for
s
as indicated in
Lemma

16.5
and
\delta=({\Delta_{c}})^{-1}
. This concludes the proof of
Proposition

16.1
.

\square

17.
Proof of
Theorems

1.3
and
1.6

The proofs of
Theorems

1.3
and
1.6
are almost identical.
We only show how to prove
Theorem

1.3
and we omit the proof of
Theorem

1.6
.

For a graph
G=(V,E)
and
\lambda>0
, consider the Hard-core model with fugacity
\lambda
.
We define
\mathcal{Z}(G,\lambda)
, the
partition function
of this distribution by

(17.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(G,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\sigma}\lambda^{|\sigma|}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\sigma
varies over all the independent sets of
G
.
For the proof of
Theorem

1.3
we use the following result.

Theorem 17.1

(
[
20
,
39
]
)
.

Unless
{\rm NP}={\rm RP}
, for the Hard-core model, for all
{\Delta}\geq 3
for all

\lambda>{\lambda_{c}}({\Delta}-1)

there does not exist an FPRAS for the partition function with fugacity
\lambda
for graphs of
maximum degree at most
{\Delta}
.

Theorem

1.2
yields (via
[
27
,
37
]
) an FPRAS for estimating
the partition function
\mathcal{Z}(G,\lambda)
with running time
O^{*}(n^{2})
,
for any
\lambda\leq(1-\varepsilon){\lambda_{c}}(\uprho)
. Recall that
\uprho=\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}
.
Also, recall that
O^{*}()
hides multiplicative
\log n
-factors.

The negative part, i.e., the non-existence of FPRAS for
\lambda\geq(1+\varepsilon){\lambda_{c}}(\lceil\uprho\rceil)
, follows
from
Theorem

17.1
. For graph
G
, such that
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
, it is standard that

{\Delta}\geq\lceil\uprho\rceil+1
. Then,
it suffices to apply
Theorem

17.1
for graphs
G
with
\|(\UpH_{G,k})^{N}\|^{1/N}_{2}=\uprho
and
maximum degree at most
{\Delta}=\lceil\uprho\rceil+1
.

The above concludes the proof of
Theorem

1.3
.

18.
Proof of
Theorem

9.1

For the material of this section, the reader needs to be familiar with the results in
Section

8.3.1
.

18.1.
Proof of
Theorem

9.1

For
G=(V,E)
, the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
and integer
k\geq 1
,
we introduce the
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times(V\setminus\Lambda)
matrix

{\mathcal{F}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
such that, for any
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}

and
u\in V\setminus\Lambda
, the entry
{\mathcal{F}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(P,u)
is
defined as follows:

Letting
w
be the starting vertex of walk
P
, we have

(18.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}(P,u)

\displaystyle=0\kern 5.0pt,

if vertices
u
and
w
are at distance
<2k
.

Consider
u
and
w
which are at distance
\geq 2k
. Let
\upzeta^{P}
be the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.
We have

(18.2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}(P,u)

\displaystyle=\upzeta^{P}_{u}(+1\ |\ (w,+1))-\upzeta^{P}_{u}(+1\ |\ (w,-1))\kern 5.0pt,

where
\upzeta^{P}_{u}
is the marginal of
\upzeta^{P}
at vertex
u
.

Letting
{\mathcal{I}}^{P}
be the influence matrix induced by
\upzeta^{P}
, (
18.2
)
implies

(18.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}(P,u)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{I}}^{P}(w,u)\kern 5.0pt.

In light of all the above,
Theorem

9.1
follows as a corollary from the following two results.

Lemma 18.1
.

There is an
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times(V\setminus\Lambda)
matrix
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}
such that

(18.4)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}=\UpD_{k}\cdot\left({\mathcal{N}}_{A}\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}\right)+{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2k}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, there is a fixed number
C_{A}>0
, which is independent of
\Lambda
and
\tau
, such that, for any

P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
and any
v\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have
|{\mathcal{N}}_{A}(P,v)|\leq C_{A}
.

In (
18.4
), the matrices
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2{k}}
and
\UpD_{k}
are defined in
(
9.4
) and (
9.5
), respectively.
Also,
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
is the Hadamard product of the two matrices.
The proof of
Lemma

18.1
appears in
Section

18.2
.

Lemma 18.2
.

There exists an
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
matrix
{\mathcal{N}}_{B}
such that

(18.5)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}=\left({\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}\circ{\mathcal{N}}_{B}\right)\cdot\UpC_{k}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, there is a fixed number
C_{B}>0
, which is independent of
\Lambda
and
\tau
, such that
for any
Q,P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, we have
|{\mathcal{N}}_{B}(P,Q)|\leq C_{B}
.

In (
18.5
), matrix
\UpC_{k}
is defined in (
9.6
).
The proof of
Lemma

18.2
appears in
Section

18.3
.

Theorem

9.1
follows as a corollary from
Lemmas

18.1
and
18.2
.
Specifically,
{\mathcal{N}}
is given by the Hadamard product
{\mathcal{N}}={\mathcal{N}}_{C}\circ{\mathcal{N}}_{B}
,
where
{\mathcal{N}}_{B}
is from
Lemma

18.2
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{C}
is the
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
matrix such that
for any
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
we have
{\mathcal{N}}_{C}(P,Q)={\mathcal{N}}_{A}(P,u)
,
where
u
is the starting vertex of
Q
. Matrix
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}
is from
Lemma

18.1
.

We conclude that
Theorem

9.1
is true for
\widehat{C}=C_{A}\cdot C_{B}
.

\square

18.2.
Proof of
Lemma

18.1

In this proof, we use the properties of extensions from
Lemmas

8.1
and
8.2
.
Also, we abbreviate
\UpD_{k}
,
{\mathcal{F}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
,

{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2k}
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
to

\UpD
,
{\mathcal{F}}
,
{\mathcal{I}}_{<2k}
and
{\mathcal{I}}
, respectively.

Firstly, we note that the matrices at the two sides of the equation in (
18.4
) are of the same dimension,
i.e., they are
(V\setminus\Lambda)\times(V\setminus\Lambda)
.
We show that their corresponding entries are equal to each other.

For any
v\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

(18.6)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{<2{k}}(v,v)

\displaystyle=1

and

\displaystyle\left(\UpD\cdot\left({\mathcal{N}}_{A}\circ{\mathcal{F}}\right)\right)(v,v)

\displaystyle=0\kern 5.0pt.

The equality on the right is true because
\left(\UpD\cdot\left({\mathcal{N}}_{A}\circ{\mathcal{F}}\right)\right)(v,v)=\sum_{P}\UpD(v,P)\cdot{\mathcal{N}}_{A}(P,v)\cdot{\mathcal{F}}(P,v)

and the variable
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
runs over walks that emanate from
v
. For such walks
{\mathcal{F}}(P,v)=0
. Hence,
for any matrix
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}
the above is true.

From the definition of
{\mathcal{I}}
and (
18.6
) we conclude that
{\mathcal{I}}
and

{\mathcal{I}}_{<2k}+\UpD\cdot\left({\mathcal{N}}_{A}\circ{\mathcal{F}}\right)

have ones at their diagonal.
Now, we focus on the off-diagonal elements. Let

(18.7)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\geq 2k}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{I}}-{\mathcal{I}}_{<2k}\kern 5.0pt.

It suffices to show that there exists an
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times V\setminus\Lambda
matrix
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}
,
with its entries bounded as specified in the statement of
Lemma

18.1
,
such that for any
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
, which are different from each other,
we have

(18.8)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\geq 2k}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{P}{\mathcal{N}}_{A}(P,u)\cdot{\mathcal{F}}(P,u)\kern 5.0pt,

where
P
varies in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, such that its first vertex is
w
.

For vertices
w,u\in V\setminus\Lambda
at distance
<2k
, The definition of
{\mathcal{F}}
implies that
for
P
emanating from
w
, we have
{\mathcal{F}}(P,u)=0
. Also, we have
{\mathcal{I}}_{\geq 2k}(w,u)=0
.
We conclude that for pairs of vertices
w,u\in V\setminus\Lambda
at distance
<2k
,
(
18.8
) is true for any choice of matrix
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}
.

We consider
w
and
u
at distance
\geq 2k
from each other. Let
T=T_{{\rm SAW}}(G,w)
. Also, let
\{\upalpha(e)\}

be the collection of weights over the edges of
T
we obtain by applying the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction on the Gibbs
distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. Then,
Proposition

5.9
implies

(18.9)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\geq 2k}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\sum\nolimits_{W=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})\in T}\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\upalpha(e_{i})\kern 5.0pt,

where
W
varies over the length-
\ell
paths in
T
from the root of the tree to the copies of vertex
u
.

For each
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, which emanates from vertex
w
, consider
T_{P}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},w)
.
Using
Lemma

8.1
, we identify each
T_{P}
as a subtree of
T
, while note that both trees
have the same root. Recall also the weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}
on the edges of
T
that are induced by the

T_{\rm SAW}
-construction on
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. Since
T_{P}
is a subtree of
T
, we endow

T_{P}
with the edge-weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in T_{P}}
.

Each path
W
in
T
that emanates from the root and is of length
\geq 2k
belongs to exactly one of the subtrees

T_{P}
of
T
we define above. Using this observation, we write (
18.9
) as
follows:

(18.10)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{\geq 2k}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{P}\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\sum\nolimits_{W=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})\in T_{P}}\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\upalpha(e_{i})\kern 5.0pt,

where variable
P
varies over the walks in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanate from vertex
w
and
variable
W
varies over the length-
\ell
paths in
T_{P}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},w)
from the root of the
tree to the copies of vertex
u
.

For each
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
whose first vertex is
w
,
and
T_{P}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},w)
, we let
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}_{e\in T_{P}}
be the
collection of weights over the edges of
T_{P}
specified with respect to the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

The definition of matrix
{\mathcal{F}}
in (
9.2
) and
Proposition

5.9
imply

(18.11)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(P,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\sum\nolimits_{W=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})\in T_{P}}\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})\kern 5.0pt,

where
W
varies over the length-
\ell
paths from the root to the copies of
u
in
T_{P}
.

Letting
P=x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}
,
there is a path
R_{P}=u_{0},\ldots,u_{k}
in tree
T
, which emanates from the root such that

u_{i}
is a copy of
x_{i}
, for all
0\leq i\leq k
.
We have that
R_{P}
is in
T_{P}
, too. Furthermore,
all paths
W\in T_{P}
of length
\ell\geq 2k
that emanate from the root of
T_{P}
overlap with
R_{P}
in their first
k
edges.

The above observation implies that fixing
\ell\geq 2k
and path
W=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})
, the corresponding summand in (
18.11
) trivially satisfies

(18.12)

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})

\displaystyle=\UpK\times\prod\nolimits_{k<i\leq\ell}\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})\kern 5.0pt,

where
\UpK=\prod\nolimits_{e\in R_{P}}\upalpha_{P}(e)
.

In a previous step, we endowed each
T_{P}
with the edge weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in T_{P}}
since it is a subtree of

T
. Overall now, for each
T_{P}
we have two sets of edges weights i.e., we have
\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in T_{P}}
and

\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}_{e\in T_{P}}
.

Lemma

8.2
implies that for any edge
e\in T_{P}
, which is at distance
\geq k
from
the root of
T_{P}
, we have

(18.13)

\displaystyle\upalpha_{P}(e)

\displaystyle=\upalpha(e)\kern 5.0pt.

Combining (
18.13
) and (
18.12
) we get the following:
fixing
\ell\geq 2k
and path
W=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})
, the corresponding summand in (
18.11
) satisfies

(18.14)

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\upalpha_{P}(e_{i})

\displaystyle=\UpK\times\prod\nolimits_{k<i\leq\ell}\upalpha(e_{i})\kern 5.0pt.

We define the
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times V\setminus\Lambda
matrix
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}

such that for each
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
and
u\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{A}(P,u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}1&&\textrm{if distance from $u$ to the starting vertex of $P$ is $<2k$}\\
\prod_{e\in R_{P}}\frac{\upalpha(e)}{\upalpha_{P}(e)+\mathds{1}\{\upalpha_{P}(e)=0\}}&&\textrm{otherwise}\kern 5.0pt,\end{array}\right.

where
R_{P}
is the path in
T_{P}
defined earlier.
The above definition of
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}
implies

(18.17)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{A}(P,u)\cdot{\mathcal{F}}(P,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 2k}\sum\nolimits_{W=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell})\in T_{P}}\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\upalpha(e_{i})\kern 5.0pt,

where
W
varies over the paths in
T_{P}
from the root to the copies of vertex
u
at distance
\ell

from the root.

Verifying the above equality is a matter of simple calculations when
\upalpha_{P}(e)\neq 0

for all
e\in R_{P}
. That is, it follows from (
18.11
), (
18.14
)
and the definition of
{\mathcal{N}}_{A}
.
When there is at least one
e\in R_{P}
such that
\upalpha_{P}(e)=0
, then it is not hard to
see that we also have
\upalpha(e)=0
; hence both sides in (
18.17
) are
equal to zero. All the above imply that (
18.17
) is true.

Then, plugging (
18.17
) into (
18.10
), we obtain
(
18.8
). Hence, we conclude that (
18.4
) is
true.

The assumption that
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
is
b
-marginally bounded implies that
there is
\upc\in(0,1)
, bounded away from zero such that we either have
\upalpha_{P}(e)=0
, or

\upc\leq|\upalpha_{P}(e)|\leq 1
and similarly for
\upalpha(e)
.
Hence, for any
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
and any
vertex
u\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have
|{\mathcal{N}}_{A}(P,u)|\leq\max\{1,\upc^{-k}\}
.

We conclude that
Lemma

18.1
is true for
C_{A}=\max\{1,\upc^{-k}\}
.

\square

18.3.
Proof of
Lemma

18.2

In what follows, we abbreviate
{\mathcal{F}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}

and
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
to
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}
, respectively.

Firstly, we note that the matrices at the two sides of the equation in (
18.5
) are
of the same dimension, i.e., they are
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times(V\setminus\Lambda)
.

Lemma

18.2
follows by showing that there is an
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
matrix

{\mathcal{N}}_{B}
, with entries bounded as specified in the statement of
Lemma

18.2
,
such that for any
u\in V\setminus\Lambda
and
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, we have

(18.18)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(P,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{Q}{\mathcal{N}}_{B}(P,Q)\cdot{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)\kern 5.0pt,

where
Q
varies over all walks in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanate from vertex
u
.

Consider vertices
w,u\in V\setminus\Lambda
at distance
\geq 2k

and
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
which emanates from
w
. We start by showing that
for such
P
and
w,u
, (
18.18
) is true.

Let
\upzeta^{P}
be the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. Let
{\mathcal{I}}^{P}

be the influence matrix induced by
\upzeta^{P}
.
From (
18.3
), we have

(18.19)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(P,u)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{I}}^{P}(w,u)={\mathcal{W}}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{P}(u,w)\kern 5.0pt,

where
{\mathcal{W}}={\mathcal{W}}(P,u)=\frac{\upzeta^{P}_{u}(+1)\cdot\upzeta^{P}_{u}(-1)}{\upzeta^{P}_{w}(+1)\cdot\upzeta^{P}_{w}(-1)+\mathds{1}\{\upzeta^{P}_{w}(+1)\in\{0,1\}\}}
.

To verify the second equality in (
18.19
) note the following:
when
\upzeta^{P}_{u}(+1),\upzeta^{P}_{w}(+1)\notin\{0,1\}
, the second equality in (
18.19
)
is true due to
Claim

5.1
.
When at least one of
\upzeta^{P}_{u}(1),\upzeta^{P}_{w}(1)
is in
\{0,1\}
,
the definitions of
{\mathcal{F}}
and the influence matrix
{\mathcal{I}}^{P}

imply

{\mathcal{F}}(P,u)=0
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{P}(u,w)=0
.
Hence, the second equality in (
18.19
) is true.

The
b
-marginal boundedness assumption implies

(18.20)

\displaystyle 0\leq{\mathcal{W}}(P,u)

\displaystyle\leq b^{-2}\kern 5.0pt.

For
Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanates from
u
, let
\upzeta^{P,Q}
be the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of

\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. Note that
P
and
Q
are compatible because we assume that
u
and
w

are at distance
\geq 2k
; hence,
\upzeta^{P,Q}
is well-defined.

In what follows, denote
{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}
the influence matrix induced by
\upzeta^{P,Q}
.

Claim 18.3
.

There is an
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}\times(V\setminus\Lambda)
matrix
\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}
such that
for
w,u\in V\setminus\Lambda
with
{\rm dist}_{G}(w,u)\geq 2k
and any
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanates from
w
,
we have

(18.21)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{P}(u,w)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{Q}\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}(Q,w)\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}(u,w)\kern 5.0pt,

where
Q
varies over the walks in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
, which emanate from
u
.
Furthermore, we have

(18.22)

\displaystyle\max\nolimits_{Q,z}\{|\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}(Q,z)|\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{A}\kern 5.0pt,

where
C_{A}
is specified in
the statement of
Lemma

18.1
.

The definition of matrix
{\mathcal{L}}
and in particular (
9.3
), imply
5
5

5

The definition of the
\{P,Q\}
-extension of

\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
imply that
{\mathcal{I}}^{Q,P}={\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}
.
that,
for any
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanate from the vertices
w
and
u
, respectively, we have

(18.23)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}(u,w)={\mathcal{L}}(Q,P)\kern 5.0pt.

Combining (
18.23
) and (
18.21
), we obtain

(18.24)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{P}(u,w)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{Q}\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}(Q,w)\cdot{\mathcal{L}}(Q,P)\kern 5.0pt,

where recall that
Q
varies over the walks in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
which emanate from
u
.

Working as in (
18.19
), we use (
18.23
) to obtain that
for any
Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanates from
u
, we have

(18.25)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(Q,P)

\displaystyle=\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}\cdot{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)\kern 5.0pt,

where
\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}=\widehat{W}(P,Q)=\frac{\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(+1)\cdot\upzeta^{P,Q}_{w}(-1)}{\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1)\cdot\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(-1)+\mathds{1}\{\upzeta^{P,Q}_{u}(+1)\in\{0,1\}\}}
.

The
b
-marginal boundedness assumption implies

(18.26)

\displaystyle 0\leq\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}(P,Q)\leq b^{-2}\kern 5.0pt.

We get (
18.18
) by
plugging (
18.24
) and (
18.25
) into (
18.19
) while setting

(18.27)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{B}(P,Q)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{W}}(P,u)\cdot\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}(Q,w)\cdot\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}(P,Q)\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that
u
is the first vertex of path
Q
.
Plugging (
18.20
), (
18.22
) and (
18.26
)
into (
18.27
) we obtain that
|{\mathcal{N}}_{B}(P,Q)|\leq b^{-4}\cdot C_{A}
.

We consider (
18.18
) for
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
which are at distance
<2k

and
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanates from
w
.
For such
u,w
and
P
, we have

(18.28)

\displaystyle\left(\left({\mathcal{L}}\circ{\mathcal{N}}_{B}\right)\cdot\UpC_{k}\right)(P,u)

\displaystyle=0

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(P,u)

\displaystyle=0\kern 5.0pt.

The equality on the left is true because
\left(\left({\mathcal{L}}\circ{\mathcal{N}}_{B}\right)\cdot\UpC_{k}\right)(P,u)=\sum_{Q}{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)\cdot{\mathcal{N}}_{B}(P,Q)\cdot\UpC_{k}(Q,u)

and the variable
Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
varies over the walks that emanate from
u
. In this case, we have

{\mathcal{L}}(P,Q)=0
since
P
and
Q
are not compatible. The equality on the right follows from the
definition of matrix
{\mathcal{F}}
. Hence, for any choice of matrix
{\mathcal{N}}_{B}
, the above is true.

For matrix
{\mathcal{N}}_{B}
to be well defined, for
u,w
at distance
<2k
and any
P,Q\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}

that emanate from
w
and
u
, respectively,
we let
{\mathcal{N}}_{B}(P,Q)=1
.
This choice of
{\mathcal{N}}_{B}
and (
18.28
) imply that (
18.18
) is also true
for any
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
which are at distance
<2k
and
P
that emanates from
w
.

Hence (
18.18
) is true for any
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
.
We conclude that
Lemma

18.2
is true for
C_{B}=\max\{1,b^{-4}\cdot C_{A}\}
.

\square

Proof of
Claim

18.3
.

The claim follows by utilising the equality in (
18.8
) from the proof of
Lemma

18.1
.
We apply this equality with respect to the influence matrices
{\mathcal{I}}^{P}
,
{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}
, i.e., instead of matrices
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}

and
{\mathcal{I}}^{P}
that are considered there.
More specifically, we have

(18.29)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{P}(u,w)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{Q}\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}(Q,w)\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{P,Q}(u,w)\kern 5.0pt,

where
Q
varies over the walks in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanate from vertex
u
and matrix

\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}
is defined as in
Lemma

18.1
, with respect to the Gibbs distributions

\upzeta^{P}
and
\upzeta^{P,Q}
.

Applying the same arguments as in the proof of
Lemma

18.1
,
we obtain
\max_{Q,z}\{|\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}(Q,z)\}|\leq C_{A}
, where
C_{A}
is specified in
the statement of
Lemma

18.1
.
Note that the bound
C_{A}
depends only on the parameters
k
and
b
for the
marginal boundedness.

Claim

18.3
follows.
∎

19.
Remaining Proofs

19.1.
Proofs of
Lemmas

8.1
and
8.2

We introduce a few useful notions for the proofs of
Lemmas

8.1
and
8.2
.
Recall walk
P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}
that emanates from vertex
w
.

We let set
\Omega
consist of all walks
W
in
G
that correspond to the vertices in
t_{P}
. Similarly, we define set

\Omega_{P}
that consists of all walks that correspond to the vertices in
T_{P}
.

Since each of
t_{P}
and
T_{P}
is a (part of) tree of self-avoiding walks starting from vertex
w
,
the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction implies that each walk
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
in
\Omega
or
\Omega_{P}

is such that
v_{0}=w
, while it is one of the following two types:

Type I:

v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
is a self-avoiding walk,

Type II:

v_{0},\ldots,v_{r-1}
is a self-avoiding walk, while there is
j\leq r-3
such that
v_{r}=v_{j}
.

Let set
\Omega^{(a)}\subseteq\Omega
consist of all the elements in
\Omega
which are
self-avoiding walks, i.e., they are of Type I. Let set
\Omega^{(b)}\subseteq\Omega
consist of all
walks
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega
, which are of Type II such that
v_{r}\in P
. Finally,
let set
\Omega^{(c)}\subseteq\Omega
consist of all walks
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega

which is of Type II, while
v_{r}\notin P
. It is immediate that
\Omega^{(a)}
,
\Omega^{(b)}

and
\Omega^{(c)}
partition set
\Omega
.

Similarly, let set
\Omega^{(a)}_{P}\subseteq\Omega_{P}
consist of all the elements in

\Omega_{P}
, which are of Type I, and do not use any of the split-vertices in
\UpS_{P}
. Let
set
\Omega^{(b)}_{P}\subseteq\Omega_{P}
consist of all the elements in
\Omega_{P}
,
which are of Type I, such that the last vertex is in
\UpS_{P}
. Finally, let set

\Omega^{(c)}_{P}\subseteq\Omega_{P}
consist of all walks
W
of Type II.
Recall that the split-vertices in
\UpS_{P}
are of degree one; hence, there can be no path

W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega^{(c)}_{P}
such that
v_{r}\in\UpS_{P}
.
It is immediate that
\Omega^{(a)}_{P}
,
\Omega^{(b)}_{P}
and
\Omega^{(c)}_{P}

partition set
\Omega_{P}
.

Proof of
Lemma

8.1
.

Our result follows by showing that there is an appropriate bijection
\uph
from the vertex set of

t_{P}
to that of
T_{P}
that allows us to identify
t_{P}
with
T_{P}
.

Since the vertices in
t_{P}
correspond to the walks in
\Omega
and the vertices in
T_{P}

correspond to the walks in
\Omega_{P}
, we equivalently establish the bijection
\uph
from
set
\Omega
to
\Omega_{P}
.

For concreteness, let
P=x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}
, while
x_{0}=w
. Let set
{\mathcal{O}}
consist of each
edge
e
in
G
, which does not connect two consecutive vertices in
P
but has at least one end in the set

\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{k-1}\}
. Similarly, let set
{\mathcal{O}}_{P}
consist of each edge in
{G}_{P}

which is between a split-vertex in set
\UpS_{P}
and another vertex of
{G}_{P}
.

The only edges in
G
that do not appear in
{G}_{P}
are exactly those in
set
{\mathcal{O}}
. Similarly, the only edges in
{G}_{P}
that do not appear in
G
are exactly those in set

{\mathcal{O}}_{P}
. This implies that the elements in
\Omega\cap\Omega_{P}
correspond to the walks
that do not use edges from sets
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{P}
.

The definitions of the two trees
t_{P}
and
T_{P}
imply that

\displaystyle\Omega^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{(a)}_{P}

and

\displaystyle\Omega^{(c)}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{(c)}_{P}\kern 5.0pt.

To see this, recall that we have
P=x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}
. Then each
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega^{(a)}

corresponds to a self-avoiding walk that emanates from vertex
w
, while
v_{i}=x_{i}
, for
i=0,\ldots,\min\{k,r\}
.
It is immediate to check that each such walk
W
also belongs to
\Omega^{(a)}_{P}
and vice versa. This implies that

\Omega^{(a)}=\Omega^{(a)}_{P}
. With a very similar argument, we also get
\Omega^{(c)}=\Omega^{(c)}_{P}
.

It is only the walks in
\Omega^{(b)}
and
\Omega^{(b)}_{P}
that use edges from
{\mathcal{O}}
and

{\mathcal{O}}_{P}
, respectively. For each walk
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega^{(b)}
, there is a
walk
W_{P}=z_{0},\ldots,z_{r}\in\Omega^{(b)}_{P}
such that
v_{i}=z_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,r-1
,
while
z_{r}
is a split-vertex that is generated by vertex
v_{r}
and vice versa. This holds since
the definition of
{G}_{P}
implies that when constructing
{G}_{P}
, we substitute the edge

\{v_{r-1},v_{r}\}\in{\mathcal{O}}
with the edge
\{z_{r-1},z_{r}\}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{P}
.

Considering all the above, we specify
\uph
that

\displaystyle\uph(W)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}W&&\textrm{if }W\in\Omega^{(\alpha)}\cup\Omega^{(c)}\kern 5.0pt,\\
W_{P}&&\textrm{if }W\in\Omega^{(b)}\kern 5.0pt,\\
\end{array}\right.

where
W_{P}
is as described in the paragraph above (
19.1
).

It is easy to check that for each
W_{P}\in\Omega^{(b)}_{P}
there is a unique
W\in\Omega^{(b)}

such that
\uph(W)=W_{P}
. This observation and (
19.1
) imply
that
\uph
is a bijection from
\Omega
to
\Omega_{P}
. Using the correspondence
between sets
\Omega
,
\Omega_{P}
and the sets of vertices of trees
t_{P}
and
T_{P}
,
respectively, we identify
\uph
as a bijection from the set of vertices in
t_{P}

to those in
T_{P}
.

For vertex
u
in
t_{P}
that corresponds to walk
W\in\Omega
, we have that
\uph(u)
is
a vertex in
T_{P}
that corresponds to walk
\uph(W)\in\Omega_{P}
. From (
19.1
),
it is easy to verify that if
u
is a copy of
v
in
G
, then
\uph(u)
must be a copy of
v
or a split-vertex in

{G}_{P}
generated by
v
.

We now show that
\uph
preserves adjacencies. That is, for two vertices
x
,
z
in
t_{P}

which are adjacent, we have that
\uph(x)
and
\uph(z)
are two adjacent vertices in
T_{P}
.
Suppose
x
and
z
correspond to walks
W,\overline{W}\in\Omega
, respectively. W.l.o.g.,
suppose walk
W
extends walk
\overline{W}
by one vertex. Then, the vertices
\uph(x)

and
\uph(z)
correspond to walks
\uph(W)
and
\uph(\overline{W})
, respectively.
The definition of
\uph
implies that
\uph(W)
extends
\uph(\overline{W})
by one
vertex. To see this, note that in this setting we have
\overline{W}\in\Omega^{(a)}
while
W\in\Omega
,
hence we have
\uph(\overline{W})=\overline{W}
. If
W\in\Omega^{(a)}\cup\Omega^{(c)}
, then

\uph(W)=W
implying that
W
extends
\overline{W}
. If on the other hand
we have
W\in\Omega^{(b)}
then
\uph(W)=W_{P}
, where
W_{P}
is defined as in
the paragraph above (
19.1
). It is easy to verify that
W_{P}
extends
\overline{W}
in this case too.
Hence,
\uph(x)
and
\uph(z)
are two adjacent vertices in
T_{P}
. This implies that

\uph
preserves adjacencies between the two trees,
t_{P}
and
T_{P}
.

From all the above, we conclude that
\uph
has all the necessary properties to allow us to identify

t_{P}
with
T_{P}
.
Lemma

8.1
follows.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

8.2
.

Let
\mu^{M,\sigma}
be the Gibbs distribution on
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,w)
induced by the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction on

\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. Similarly, let the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{K,\eta}_{P}
on
T_{P}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},w)

be induced by the same construction with respect to the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

Consider the edge-weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}
and
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
induced by the two Gibbs distributions

\mu^{M,\sigma}
and
\mu^{K,\eta}_{P}
respectively. Note that the edge-weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}
are on
the edges of
T
, while the edge-weights
\{\upalpha_{P}(e)\}
are on the edges of
T_{P}
.
Using
Lemma

8.1
, we identify
T_{P}
as a subtree of
T
, and we endow the
edges of
T_{P}
with the edge-weights
\{\upalpha(e)\}_{e\in T_{P}}
.

Fix an edge
e=\{x,z\}
in
T_{P}
such that
z
is the child of
x
. Suppose
e
is at distance
\geq k

from the root.
Lemma

8.2
follows by showing that
\upalpha(e)=\upalpha_{P}(e)
.

The edge weights
\upalpha(e)
and
\upalpha_{P}(e)
only depend on the
parameters of the initial Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
and the pinnings
(M,\sigma)
and

(K,\eta)
that the two Gibbs distributions
\mu^{M,\sigma}
,
\mu^{K,\eta}_{P}
impose, respectively,
at the vertices of
T_{z}
. As per standard notation,
T_{z}
is the subtree of
T_{P}
that consists
of vertex
z
and its descendants. Since
z
is also a vertex in
T
, it is not hard to verify that
the subtree of
T
which consists of
z
and its descendants is exactly the same as
T_{z}
.
This is due to the assumption that edge
e
is at distance
\geq k
from the root of
T
.

Since, by definition, both
\mu^{M,\sigma}
and
\mu^{K,\eta}_{P}
have the same parameters,
it suffices to show the pinnings
(M\cap T_{P},\sigma)
and

(K,\eta)
are identical. Here, we let
(M\cap T_{P},\sigma)
stand for the restriction of pinning

(M,\sigma)
to the vertices in
T_{P}
.

Let set
\Omega^{\Lambda}\subseteq\Omega^{(\alpha)}
consist of the self-avoiding walks in
\Omega^{(\alpha)}

whose last vertex is in set
\Lambda
. Similarly, we define
\Omega^{\Lambda}_{P}\subseteq\Omega^{(\alpha)}_{P}
.
The
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction implies that set
M\cap T_{P}
consists of the vertices that correspond to the walks in

\Omega^{\Lambda}\cup\Omega^{(b)}\cup\Omega^{(c)}
. Similarly, set
K
in
\mu^{K,\eta}_{P}
consists of the
vertices that correspond to the walks in
\Omega^{\Lambda}_{P}\cup\Omega^{(b)}_{P}\cup\Omega^{(c)}_{P}
.

We start by showing that
M\cap T_{P}\subseteq K
while for every
v\in M\cap T_{P}
, we have
\sigma(v)=\eta(v)
.
Since we identify
T_{P}
as a subtree of
T
, each vertex in
T_{P}
corresponds to two walks, one from set

\Omega
and one from set
\Omega_{P}
. Particularly, for vertex
v\in T_{P}
which corresponds to walk

W\in\Omega
, it also corresponds to walk
\uph(W)\in\Omega_{P}
, where
\uph
is the bijection between

\Omega
and
\Omega_{P}
described in (
19.1
), i.e., the proof of
Lemma

8.1
.

Each vertex
v\in M\cap T_{P}
, which corresponds to a walk
W\in\Omega^{\Lambda}\subseteq\Omega^{(\alpha)}
, also corresponds to
the same walk
W\in\Omega^{\Lambda}_{P}
since (
19.1
) implies
\uph(W)=W\in\Omega^{\Lambda}_{P}
.
We then obtain that vertex
v\in K
. Furthermore, the pinning rules of the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction
imply
\eta(v)=\sigma(v)=\tau(v)
. Recall that
\tau
is from the pinning of the initial Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
.

Arguing as above, for each vertex
v\in M\cap T_{P}
, which corresponds to walk
W\in\Omega^{(c)}
,
we have
\uph(W)=W\in\Omega^{(c)}_{P}
. Hence, we obtain that
v\in K
. For such vertex
v
,
the pinning rules of the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction imply that
\eta(v)=\sigma(v)
.

Each vertex
v\in M\cap T_{P}
which corresponds to walk
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega^{(b)}
also
corresponds to walk
W_{P}=z_{0},\ldots,z_{r}
such that
v_{i}=z_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq r-1
, while

z_{r}
is a split-vertex in
\UpS_{P}
generated by vertex
v_{r}
. That is, we have
\uph(W)=W_{P}\in\Omega^{(b)}
.
This implies that
v\in K
.

For vertex
v
in the paragraph above, the pinning
\sigma(v)
is induced by the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction on
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, while
the pinning
\eta(v)
is because we consider the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
, which pins the split-vertices in
\UpS_{P}
.
From the description of the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction and the definition of the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
it not hard to verify that

\eta(v)=\sigma(v)
, e.g., see (
8.2
).

All the above imply that
M\cap T_{P}\subseteq K
, while for each
v\in M\cap T_{P}
, we have
\sigma(v)=\eta(v)
.
With a very similar line of arguments, we also deduce that
K\subseteq M\cap T_{P}
, while for each
v\in K
,
we have
\sigma(v)=\eta(v)
. We omit these arguments because they are almost identical to those we presented above.

We conclude that the two pinnings
(M\cap T_{P},\sigma)
and
(K,\eta)
are identical. Consequently, we have that

\upalpha(e)=\upalpha_{P}(e)
for any edge
e
at distance
\geq k
from the root of
T_{P}
.

Lemma

8.2
follows.
∎

19.2.
Proof of
Lemma

8.3

Let
\Omega
be the set of walks in
G
which correspond to the vertices in
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
.
Also, let
\Omega_{P}
be the set of walks in
{G}_{P}
which correspond to the vertices in

\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
.

For concreteness, let
P=x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}
. Recall that
x_{0}=w
and
u\notin P
.
Let set
{\mathcal{O}}
consist of each edge
e
in
G

which does not connect two consecutive vertices in
P
but has at least one end in the set of vertices

\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{k-1}\}
. Let set
{\mathcal{O}}_{P}
consist of the set of edges in
{G}_{P}
which
are between a split-vertex in set
\UpS_{P}
and another vertex of
{G}_{P}
.

The only edges in
G
that do not appear in
{G}_{P}
are exactly those in set
{\mathcal{O}}
.
Similarly, the only edges in
{G}_{P}
that do not appear in
G
are exactly those in set
{\mathcal{O}}_{P}
.
Hence, the elements in
\Omega\cap\Omega_{P}
correspond to paths that do not use edges from
set
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{P}
.

We let the map
\upphi:\Omega_{P}\to\Omega
be defined as follows:

(1)

For each
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega_{P}
such that
v_{r}\notin\UpS_{P}

we set
\upphi(W)=W
.

(2)

For each
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega_{P}
such that
v_{r}\in\UpS_{P}
, we set

\upphi(W)=W_{P}
, where
W_{P}=z_{0},\ldots,z_{r}
is such that
z_{i}=v_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,r-1
,
while the split-vertex
v_{r}
is generated from
z_{r}
.

We proceed to show that
\upphi
is well-defined.

Since each vertex from
\UpS_{P}
is of degree one, it can only appear in a walk in
{G}_{P}

as a first or as a last vertex. Hence, each
W\in\Omega_{P}
such that
v_{r}\notin\UpS_{P}

does not use edges from
{\mathcal{O}}_{P}
, implying
W\in\Omega
. Hence, item (a) for map

\upphi
is well-defined.

For each walk
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega_{P}
such that
v_{r}\in\UpS_{P}
, there
is walk
W_{P}=z_{0},\ldots,z_{r}\in\Omega
such that
v_{i}=z_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,r-1
,
while
z_{r}
is a split-vertex that is generated by vertex
v_{r}
. This holds since the definition of

{G}_{P}
implies that when constructing
{G}_{P}
, we substitute the edge
\{z_{r-1},z_{r}\}\in{\mathcal{O}}

with the edge
\{v_{r-1},v_{r}\}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{P}
. Hence, item (b) for
\upphi
is well-defined.

We let
\widehat{\Omega}\subseteq\Omega
be the image set of map
\upphi
. The discussion in the
previous paragraph implies that for each
W\in\widehat{\Omega}
, there is a (unique) well-defined walk

\upphi^{-1}(W)\in\Omega_{P}
. Furthermore, the definition of
\widehat{\Omega}
implies that

\upphi
is a surjective map from
\Omega_{P}
to
\widehat{\Omega}
. We conclude that

\upphi
is a bijection from set
\Omega_{P}
to set
\widehat{\Omega}
.

Recalling that
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
, let
\widehat{T}
be the subtree (perhaps forest) of
T

induced by the vertices that correspond to the walks in
\widehat{\Omega}
. We extend the definition
of
\upphi
and identify it as a map from the set of vertices in
\overline{T}
to that in
\widehat{T}
.
Particularly,
\upphi
is bijection between the sets of vertices of the two trees.

We show that
\upphi
preserves adjacencies. That is, for two vertices
x
,
z
in
\overline{T}

which are adjacent, we have that
\upphi(x)
and
\upphi(z)
are two adjacent vertices in

\widehat{T}
. Suppose that
x
and
z
correspond to walks
W,\overline{W}\in\Omega_{P}
,
respectively. W.l.o.g., suppose walk
W
extends walk
\overline{W}
by one vertex. Then, the
vertices
\upphi(x)
and
\upphi(z)
correspond to walks
\upphi(W)
and

\upphi(\overline{W})
, respectively. In what follows we show that
\upphi(W)

extends
\upphi(\overline{W})
.

Let
\overline{W}=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
and
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r+1}
. First note that we cannot have

v_{r}\in\UpS_{P}
because this would imply that walk
\overline{W}
cannot be extended as
v_{r}
is of
degree 1. Hence we always have
v_{r}\notin\UpS_{P}
. We consider cases for
v_{r+1}
. If

v_{r+1}\notin\UpS_{P}
, then we have
\upphi(\overline{W})=\overline{W}
and

\upphi(W)=W
.
Clearly, in this case,
\upphi(W)
extends path
\upphi(\overline{W})
. If
v_{r+1}\in\UpS_{P}
,
then we have
\upphi(W)=W_{P}
, where
W_{P}=z_{0},\ldots,z_{r+1}

is such that
z_{i}=v_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,r
, while the split-vertex
v_{r+1}
is generated from

z_{r+1}
. Clearly we have that
W_{P}
extends walk
\overline{W}
. The above implies that

\upphi(W)
extends
\upphi(\overline{W})
.

The conclusion of the previous paragraph implies that
\upphi(x)
and
\upphi(z)
are two adjacent
vertices in
\widehat{T}
. Hence,
\upphi
preserves adjacencies between the two trees
\widehat{T}
and

\overline{T}
.

The definition of
\upphi
also implies the following: if vertex
v
in
\overline{T}
is a copy of vertex
\overline{v}
in

{G}_{P}
and
\overline{v}
is not a split-vertex, then
\upphi(v)
is also a copy of
\overline{v}
. If
v
is a copy
of the split-vertex which is generated by vertex
\overline{v}\in P
, then
\upphi(v)
is a copy of
\overline{v}
.

All the above imply that we can identify
\overline{T}
with the tree
\widehat{T}
. Consequently, we can identify

\overline{T}
as a subtree of
T
. The two trees
\overline{T}
and
T
have the same root, since they both
consider the walks starting from vertex
u
.

We now consider the set of vertices
v
in
T\setminus\overline{T}
.
The vertiex
v
corresponda to a walk
W\in\Omega\setminus\widehat{\Omega}
.
Recall that we set
P=x_{0},\ldots,x_{k}
. Then, for walk
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega\setminus\widehat{\Omega}

there exists
j<r
such that
v_{j}
is a copy of a vertex in
P\setminus\{x_{k}\}
while
v_{j+1}
is a copy of a vertex
outside
P
. This implies that vertex
v
in
T
is a descendant of a copy of
v_{j}
in
{\tt A}_{P}
. Recall
that set
{\tt A}_{P}
consist of each vertex in
T
which is a copy of a vertex in
P
.

All the above conclude the proof of
Lemma

8.3
.

\square

19.3.
Proof of
Lemma

13.1

The setting in this proof is the same as in the proof of
Lemma

8.3
. Recall that we have vertices

w,u
in graph
G
such that
{\rm dist}_{G}(u,w)\geq 2k
and a self-avoiding path
P
of length
k
that
emanates from vertex
w
; hence, we have
u\notin P
.

Let
\Omega
be the set of walks in
G
which correspond to the vertices in
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
. Also, let

\Omega_{P}
be the set of walks in
{G}_{P}
which correspond to the vertices in

\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
.

We use the same map
\upphi:\Omega_{P}\to\Omega
as in the proof of
Lemma

8.3
.
That is,

(1)

For each
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega_{P}
such that
v_{r}\notin\UpS_{P}

we set
\upphi(W)=W
.

(2)

For each
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega_{P}
such that
v_{r}\in\UpS_{P}
, we set

\upphi(W)=W_{P}
, where
W_{P}=z_{0},\ldots,z_{r}
is such that
z_{i}=v_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,r-1
,
while the split-vertex
v_{r}
is generated from
z_{r}
.

We let
\widehat{\Omega}\subseteq\Omega
be the image set of
\upphi
. Let
\widehat{T}
be the
subtree of
T
induced by the vertices that correspond to the walks in
\widehat{\Omega}
. We showed in
the proof of
Lemma

8.3
that we can identify
\overline{T}
as a subtree of
T
, in particular, we identify

\overline{T}
as the subtree
\widehat{T}
of
T
, using
\upphi
. Every vertex in
\overline{T}
corresponds
to two walks, one walk in
{G}_{P}
and another in
G
, e.g., the walk
W\in\Omega_{P}
and the
walk
\upphi(W)\in\widehat{\Omega}\subseteq\Omega
.

Furthermore, we apply the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction on
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
and obtain the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{M,\sigma}_{T}

for tree
T=T_{\rm SAW}(G,u)
. We proceed to identify the walks in
\Omega
that correspond to the vertices in
M
.
Specifically, consider the following subsets of
\Omega
,

(1)

set
\Omega^{\Lambda}\subseteq\Omega
consists of all self-avoiding walks whose last vertex is in
\Lambda
,

(2)

set
\Omega^{(b)}\subseteq\Omega
consists of all walks
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega
such that
v_{0},\ldots,v_{r-1}
is self-avoiding and

v_{r}
repeats, while
W\cap P\neq\emptyset
,

(3)

set
\Omega^{(c)}\subseteq\Omega
consists of all walks
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega
such that
v_{0},\ldots,v_{r-1}
is self-avoiding and

v_{r}
repeats, while
W\cap P=\emptyset
.

The
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction implies that set
M
consists of the vertices that correspond to the walks in the three aforementioned categories.

Also, we use the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction for the
P
-extension of
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
and get the Gibbs distribution

\mu^{L,\eta}_{\overline{T}}
for tree
\overline{T}=T_{\rm SAW}({G}_{P},u)
.
We proceed to identify the walks in
\Omega_{P}
that correspond to the vertices in
L
.
Consider the following subsets of
\Omega_{P}
,

(4)

set
\Omega^{\Lambda}_{P}\subseteq\Omega_{P}
consist of all self-avoiding walks whose last vertex is in
\Lambda
,

(5)

set
\Omega^{(b)}_{P}\subseteq\Omega_{P}
consists of all self-avoiding walks whose last vertex is in
\UpS_{P}
,

(6)

set
\Omega^{(c)}_{P}\subseteq\Omega_{P}
consists of all walks
W=v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}\in\Omega

such that
v_{0},\ldots,v_{r-1}
is self-avoiding and

v_{r}
repeats, while
W\cap P\neq\emptyset
.

It is not hard to verify that set
L
consists of the vertices that correspond to the walks of the above three categories.

We use all the above to prove statements
1
-
4

of
Lemma

13.1
.

We start with statement
1
. For each vertex

v\in M\cap L
, there are two alternatives. In the first one, vertex
v
corresponds to a walk

W\in\Omega^{\Lambda}_{P}
and
\upphi(W)\in\Omega^{\Lambda}
. In the second
alternative, vertex
v
corresponds to a walk
W\in\Omega^{(c)}_{P}
and
\upphi(W)\in\Omega^{(c)}
.

To see the above, note that for each walk
W\in\Omega^{\Lambda}_{P}\cup\Omega^{(c)}_{P}
, the definition
of
\upphi
implies that we should have
\upphi(W)\in\Omega^{\Lambda}\cup\Omega^{(c)}
.
On the other hand, for each walk
W\in\Omega^{(b)}_{P}
we have

\upphi(W)\notin(\Omega^{\Lambda}\cup\Omega^{(b)}\cup\Omega^{(c)})
. This is because

\upphi(W)
is a self-avoiding walk in
G
from
u
to a vertex in
P
. Hence, every
vertex
v
which corresponds to walk
W\in\Omega^{(b)}_{P}
does not belong to
M
.
Also, for every
W\in\Omega^{(b)}
, we have that
\upphi^{-1}(W)
is not defined,
implying that the vertex
v
which corresponds to walk
W
does not belong to
\overline{T}
;
hence it does not belong to
L
.

Statement
1
follows by noting that
the definition of the
T_{\rm SAW}
-construction implies that all vertices in
M\cap L
take on the same
pinning, i.e., there are no copies of split-vertices in this intersection.

As far as statement
2
is concerned, note
that each vertex
v\in{\tt A}_{P}\cap\overline{T}
can only
correspond to a self-avoiding walk
W\in\Omega
which ends at a vertex in
P
. The definition of

\upphi
implies that
\upphi^{-1}(W)
is well-defined and corresponds to a self-avoiding walk in

\Omega_{P}
whose last vertex is either in
P
or in
\UpS_{P}
. Hence, it follows from the
definition of set
\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
that
v\in\overline{{\tt A}}_{P}
. This proves
2
.

We proceed to prove statement
3
. The previous discussion
shows that each vertex
v\in L\setminus M
corresponds to a walk
W\in\Omega^{(b)}_{P}
.
Furthermore, the definition of
\upphi
implies that the last vertex of walk
\upphi(W)
is in
P
. Hence, we have

v\in{\tt A}_{P}
. We conclude that
L\setminus M\subseteq{\tt A}_{P}
.

If vertex
v\in M\cap\overline{T}
corresponds to a walk
W\in\Omega^{\Lambda}
, then the definition
of
\upphi
implies that
\upphi^{-1}(W)\in\Omega^{\Lambda}_{P}
; hence
v\in L
. Similarly, if vertex

v\in M\cap\overline{T}
corresponds to a walk
W\in\Omega^{(c)}
, then it also corresponds to walk

\upphi^{-1}(W)\in\Omega^{(c)}_{P}
; hence
v\in L
.
We conclude that
M\cap\overline{T}\subseteq L

by noting that there is no vertex
v\in M\cap\overline{T}
which corresponds to a walk
W\in\Omega^{(b)}

(this was also noted in the proof of
1
).

The two conclusions that
L\setminus M\subseteq{\tt A}_{P}
and that
M\cap\overline{T}\subseteq L

imply that statement
3
is true.

We proceed to prove statement
4
. This follows directly by recalling
that all vertices in
T\setminus\overline{T}
are descendants of the vertices in
{\tt A}_{P}
. Hence, after pinning the vertices in
L\cup{\tt A}_{P}
,
the vertices in
T\setminus\overline{T}
have no effect on the configurations of the vertices in
T\cap\overline{T}
.

All the above conclude the proof of
Lemma

13.1
.
\square

19.4.
Proof of
Proposition

9.2

Before proving
Proposition

9.2
, we present two useful results.

Claim 19.1
.

We have
\|\UpD_{k}\|_{2}\leq{\Delta}^{k/2}
and
\|\UpC_{k}\|_{2}\leq{\Delta}^{k/2}
.

Claim 19.2
.

We have
\|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2k}\|_{2}\leq\frac{{\Delta}^{2k+1}-1}{{\Delta}-1}
.

In what follows, we abbreviate
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
,
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2k}
,
{\mathcal{L}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,k}
,

\UpD_{k}
and
\UpC_{k}
to

{\mathcal{I}}
,
{\mathcal{I}}_{<2k}
,
{\mathcal{L}}
,
\UpD
and
\UpC
, respectively.
From
Theorem

9.1
we have that

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{I}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|\UpD\cdot\left({\mathcal{L}}\circ{\mathcal{N}}\right)\cdot\UpC+{\mathcal{I}}_{<2k}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\UpD\|_{2}\cdot\|\UpC\|_{2}\cdot\|{\mathcal{L}}\circ{\mathcal{N}}\|_{2}+\|{\mathcal{I}}_{<2k}\|_{2}

(19.3)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm max}\cdot\|\UpD\|_{2}\cdot\|\UpC\|_{2}\cdot\||{\mathcal{L}}|\|_{2}+\|{\mathcal{I}}_{<2k}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt,

where
{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm max}=\max_{P,Q}\{|{\mathcal{N}}(P,Q)|\}
.
Theorem

9.1
implies that
{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm max}

is a constant.

Proposition

9.2
follows from (
19.3
) and

Claims

19.1
and
19.2
, while we set

C_{1}=\frac{{\Delta}^{2k+1}-1}{{\Delta}-1}

and
C_{2}={\mathcal{N}}_{\rm max}\cdot{\Delta}^{k}
.

\square

Proof of
Claim

19.1
.

We start with
\|\UpD_{k}\|_{2}
.
Consider the product
\UpD_{k}\cdot\overline{\UpD}_{k}
, where
\overline{\UpD}_{k}
is the matrix transpose of
\UpD_{k}
.
Matrix
\UpD_{k}\cdot\overline{\UpD}_{k}
is
(V\setminus\Lambda)\times(V\setminus\Lambda)
, while for any
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\UpD_{k}\cdot\overline{\UpD}_{k}\right)(u,w)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}\UpD_{k}(u,P)\times\overline{\UpD}_{k}(P,w)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}\mathds{1}\{\textrm{$u$ is the starting vertex in $P$}\}\times\mathds{1}\{\textrm{$w$ is the starting vertex in $P$}\}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}\{w=u\}\times\sum\nolimits_{P\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\Lambda,k}}\mathds{1}\{\textrm{$u$ is the starting vertex in $P$}\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{1}\{w=u\}\times{\Delta}^{k}\kern 5.0pt.

We conclude that
\UpD_{k}\cdot\overline{\UpD}_{k}
is a non-negative and diagonal matrix. For
w\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

(\UpD_{k}\cdot\overline{\UpD}_{k})(w,w)\leq{\Delta}^{k}
.
Hence, we have
\|\UpD_{k}\cdot\overline{\UpD}_{k}\|_{2}\leq{\Delta}^{k}
, which implies that
\|\UpD_{k}\|_{2}\leq{\Delta}^{k/2}
.
Working similarly, we get
\|\UpC_{k}\|_{2}\leq{\Delta}^{k/2}
.

Claim

19.1
follows.
∎

Proof of
Claim

19.2
.

Let
{\UpA}^{(2k)}
be a
(V\setminus\Lambda)\times(V\setminus\Lambda)
, zero-one matrix such that
for any
w,u\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

(19.4)

\displaystyle{\UpA}^{(2k)}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}\{{\rm dist}_{G}(w,u)\leq 2k\}\kern 5.0pt.

Noting that each entry in
{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2k}
is in the interval
[-1,1]
, we have

(19.5)

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2k}|

\displaystyle\leq{\UpA}^{(2k)}\kern 5.0pt,

where the inequality is entrywise. We have

(19.6)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2k}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\||{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G,<2k}|\|_{2}\leq\|{\UpA}^{(2k)}\|_{2}\leq\|{\UpA}^{(2k)}\|_{\infty}\kern 5.0pt.

The first inequality above is elementary, e.g. see
Lemma

B.2
. The second inequality follows from
(
19.5
). The last inequality follows by noting that matrix
{\UpA}^{(2k)}
is symmetric.

Since
G
is of maximum degree
{\Delta}
, we have that
\|{\UpA}^{(2k)}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{{\Delta}^{2k+1}-1}{{\Delta}-1}
.
The claim follows by plugging the bound on
\|{\UpA}^{(2k)}\|_{\infty}
into (
19.6
).
∎

Appendix A
Some Standard Proofs

A.1.
Proof of
Claim

5.1

Here we restate
Claim

5.1
and provide its proof.

See
5.1

Proof.

Let
\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
be the
(V\times\Lambda)\times(V\times\Lambda)
covariance matrix defined with respect to
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}
. That is, for any
u,v\in V\setminus\Lambda
we have

\displaystyle\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(v,u)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{(v,u)}((+1,+1))-\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{v}(+1)\cdot\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{u}(+1)\kern 5.0pt.

A straightforward observation is that
\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
is symmetric. W.l.o.g. in this proof, assume that
the diagonal entries of
\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
are non-zero.
Note that for any
v\in V\setminus\Lambda
we have that

(A.1)

\displaystyle\UpM(v,v)=\sqrt{\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(v,v)}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, it is standard to show, e.g. see
[
10
]
, that
\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
and

{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
satisfy that

{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(u,v)=\frac{\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(u,v)}{\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(u,u)}.

Since
\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
is symmetric, this relation implies that

\displaystyle\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(u,u)\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\Upsigma^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(v,v)\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(v,u)\kern 5.0pt.

Then, a simple rearrangement and (
A.1
) imply that

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\UpM(u,u)}{\UpM(v,v)}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(u,v)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\frac{\UpM(v,v)}{\UpM(u,u)}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(v,u)\kern 5.0pt.

The l.h.s. in the above equation corresponds to the entry

(\UpM\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\cdot\UpM^{-1})(u,v)
, while the r.h.s. corresponds to

(\UpM\cdot{\mathcal{I}}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}\cdot\UpM^{-1})(v,u)
.
The claim follows.
∎

A.2.
Proof of
Claim

5.7

We restate
Claim

5.7
before presenting its proof.
See
5.7

Proof.

In what follows, we abbreviate
\UpH_{G,k}
to
\UpH
.

Let the vector
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{k}}
be such that for any
R\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
we have

(A.2)

\displaystyle{\bf x}(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\mathds{1}\{R=P\}+\mathds{1}\{R=Q^{-1}\}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

We have that

\displaystyle{\bf x}^{T}(\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR){\bf x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left((\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR)(P,Q^{-1})+(\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR)(Q^{-1},Q^{-1})+(\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR)(P,P)+(\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR)(Q^{-1},P)\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Recalling the definition of matrix
\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR
, we have

\displaystyle\UpH^{\ell}(P,Q)=(\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR)(P,Q^{-1})=(\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR)(Q^{-1},P)\kern 5.0pt,

the last equation follows since
\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR
is symmetric.
All the above imply that

\displaystyle\UpH^{\ell}(P,Q)\leq{\bf x}^{T}\cdot(\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR)\cdot{\bf x}\leq\|\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR\|_{2}=\sigma_{k,\ell}\kern 5.0pt.

The last inequality above follows from the fact that
\UpH^{\ell}\cdot\UpR
is symmetric, with maximum
eigenvalue
\sigma_{k,\ell}
.

The claim follows.
∎

A.3.
Proof of
Claim

5.8

We restate
Claim

5.8
before presenting its proof.
See
5.8

Proof.

In what follows, we abbreviate
\UpH_{G,k}
to
\UpH

Let the integers
t=\lfloor\frac{\ell}{N}\rfloor
and
r\geq 0
be such that
\ell=N\cdot t+r
.
We have

(A.3)

\displaystyle\left(\upsigma_{k,\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}

\displaystyle=\|\UpH^{\ell}\|^{\frac{1}{\ell}}_{2}\leq\|\UpH^{\ell-r}\|^{\frac{1}{\ell}}_{2}\cdot\|\UpH^{r}\|^{\frac{1}{\ell}}_{2}\leq\|\UpH^{\ell-r}\|^{\frac{1}{\ell-r}}_{2}\cdot\|\UpH^{r}\|^{\frac{1}{\ell}}_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, noting that
\ell-r=N\cdot t
, we get

(A.4)

\displaystyle\|\UpH^{\ell-r}\|^{\frac{1}{\ell-r}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\|\UpH^{N\cdot t}\|^{\frac{1}{N\cdot t}}_{2}\leq\|\UpH^{N}\|^{\frac{1}{N}}_{2}=(\upsigma_{k,N})^{\frac{1}{N}}\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging the above into (
A.3
) we have

\displaystyle\left(\upsigma_{k,\ell}\right)^{1/\ell}

\displaystyle\leq(\upsigma_{k,N})^{\frac{1}{N}}\cdot\|\UpH^{r}\|^{\frac{1}{\ell}}_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Noting that
\|\UpH^{r}\|^{\frac{1}{\ell}}_{2}\leq({\Delta}-1)^{\frac{r}{\ell}}

and recalling that
{\Delta}
is bounded, there is fixed
\ell_{0}
such that
for
\ell>\ell_{0}
we have
\|\UpH^{r}\|^{\frac{1}{\ell}}_{2}\leq(1+z)
.

Claim

5.8
follows.
∎

A.4.
Proof of
Lemma

15.1

We restate
Lemma

15.1
and provide its proof.

See
15.1

Proof.

It suffices to show that any
d>0
and any

{\bf y}\in[-\infty,+\infty]^{d}
we have

(A.5)

\displaystyle\|\nabla H_{d}({\bf y})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\beta-1|}{\beta+1}\kern 5.0pt.

Before showing that (
A.5
) is true, let us show how it implies
(
15.2
). That is, we show that for any
\beta\in{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(R,\varepsilon)
,
we have that
\frac{|\beta-1|}{\beta+1}\leq\frac{1-\zeta}{R}
.

Consider the function
f(x)=\frac{|x-1|}{x+1}
defined on the closed interval

\left[\frac{R-1}{R+1},\frac{R+1}{R-1}\right]
.
Taking derivatives, it is elementary to verify that
f(x)
is increasing in the interval

1<x<\frac{R+1}{R-1}
, while it is decreasing in the interval
\frac{R-1}{R+1}<x<1
.
Furthermore, noting that
f(1)=0
, it is direct that

\displaystyle\sup\nolimits_{\beta\in{\mathbb{U}_{\rm Ising}}(R,\varepsilon)}f(\beta)=f\left(\frac{R-1+\varepsilon}{R+1-\varepsilon}\right)=f\left(\frac{R+1-\varepsilon}{R-1+\varepsilon}\right)=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{R}\kern 4.62497pt.

We conclude that (
A.5
) implies (
15.2
).
It remains to show that (
A.5
) is true.

Since
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}H_{d}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})=h(x_{i})
,
it suffices to show that for any
x\in[-\infty,+\infty]
we have that

(A.6)

\displaystyle|h(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|1-\beta|}{1+\beta}\kern 4.62497pt.

For the distribution we consider here, the function
h(\cdot)
is given by

(A.7)

\displaystyle h(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(1-\beta^{2})\exp(x)}{(\beta\exp(x)+1)(\exp(x)+\beta)}\kern 5.0pt.

From the above we get that

\displaystyle|h(x)|

\displaystyle=\frac{|1-\beta^{2}|\exp(x)}{(b\exp(x)+1)(b+\exp(x))}\ =\ \frac{|1-\beta^{2}|}{\beta^{2}+1+\beta(\exp(-x)+\exp(x))}\kern 4.62497pt.

It is straightforward to verify that
\phi(x)=e^{-x}+e^{x}
is convex and for any
x\in[-\infty,+\infty]

and attains its minimum at
x=0
, i.e., we have that
\phi(x)\geq 2
. Consequently, we get

\displaystyle|h(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|1-\beta^{2}|}{\beta^{2}+1+2\beta}\ =\ \frac{|1-\beta|}{1+\beta}\kern 4.62497pt,

for any
x\in[-\infty,+\infty]
.
The above proves that (
A.6
) is true and concludes our proof.
∎

A.5.
Proof of
Claim

14.2

We restate
Claim

14.2
and provide its proof.

See
14.2

Proof.

It elementary to verify that the function
{\Delta_{c}}(x)
is decreasing in
x
. This implies that for any
\lambda\leq(1-\varepsilon){\lambda_{c}}(R)
,
we have
{\Delta_{c}}(\lambda)\geq{\Delta_{c}}({\lambda_{c}}(R))=R
. Particularly, this implies that there is
0<z<1
, which only depends on

\varepsilon
such that
{\Delta_{c}}(\lambda)\geq\frac{R}{(1-z)}
.
This proves the leftmost inequality in (
14.3
).

As far as the rightmost inequality is concerned, we have

(A.8)

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda}\leq\lambda<{\lambda_{c}}(R)\kern 5.0pt.

The first inequality follows since
\lambda>0
, while the second follows since
\lambda<{\lambda_{c}}(R)
.
From the definition of
{\lambda_{c}}(\cdot)
, we have

(A.9)

\displaystyle{\lambda_{c}}(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{R^{R}}{(R-1)^{(R+1)}}\ =\ \frac{1}{R}\left(1+\frac{1}{R-1}\right)^{R+1}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{R}\exp\left(\frac{R+1}{R-1}\right)\ \leq\ \frac{e^{3}}{R}\kern 4.62497pt.

For the one before the last inequality we use that
1+x\leq e^{x}
. For the last inequality we note that
\frac{R+1}{R-1}
is decreasing in

R
, hence, for
R\geq 2
, we have that
\frac{R+1}{R-1}\leq 3
.

Plugging the above bound into (
A.8
), gives the rightmost inequality in
(
14.3
).
The claim follows.
∎

Appendix B
Standard linear algebra

For the matrix
\UpL\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
we follow the convention to call it non-negative, if all its entries are non-negative numbers, i.e., every entry
\UpL_{i,j}\geq 0
.

For an
N\times N
matrix
\UpL
, we let
\upxi_{i}(\UpL)
, for
i=1,\ldots,N
, denote the eigenvalues of
\UpL
such that

\upxi_{1}(\UpL)\geq\upxi_{2}(\UpL)\geq\ldots\geq\upxi_{N}(\UpL)
.
Also, we let
{\Upphi}(\UpL)
denote the set of distinct eigenvalues of
\UpL
. We also refer to
{\Upphi}(\UpL)

as the
spectrum
of
\UpL
.

We define the
spectral radius
of
\UpL
, denoted as
{\rho}(\UpL)
, to be the real number
such that

\displaystyle{\rho}(\UpL)=\max\{|\upxi|\ :\ \upxi\in{\Upphi}(\UpL)\}\kern 5.0pt.

It is a well-known result that the spectral radius of
\UpL
is the greatest lower bound for all
of its matrix norms, e.g. see Theorem 6.5.9 in
[
26
]
.
That is, letting
\|\cdot\|
be a matrix norm on
N\times N
matrices, we have that

(B.1)

\displaystyle{\rho}(\UpL)\leq\|\UpL\|\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, we let
\overline{\UpL}
be the standard matrix transpose of
\UpL
.
It is useful to mention that when
\UpL
is symmetric, i.e.,
\overline{\UpL}=\UpL
,
we have that
{\rho}(\UpL)=\|\UpL\|_{2}
.

For
\UpD,\UpB,\UpC\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
, we let
|\UpD|
denote the matrix having entries
|\UpD_{i,j}|
.
For the matrices
\UpB,\UpC
we define
\UpB\leq\UpC
to mean that
\UpB_{i,j}\leq\UpC_{i,j}
for each
i
and
j
.
The following is a folklore result e.g. see
[
30
,
26
]
.

Lemma B.1
.

For integer
N>0
, let
\UpD,\UpB\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
. If
|\UpD|\leq\UpB
, then

{\rho}(\UpD)\leq{\rho}(|\UpD|)\leq{\rho}(\UpB)
.

Lemma B.2
.

For integer
N>0
, let
\UpD\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
. We have that
\|\UpD\|_{2}\leq\||\UpD|\|_{2}
.

Proof.

If
\UpD
is symmetric, then
{\rho}(\UpD)=\|\UpD\|_{2}
and
{\rho}(|\UpD|)=\||\UpD|\|_{2}
.
Hence,
Lemma

B.2
follows as a corollary from
Lemma

B.1
.

Let us consider the general case, i.e., where
\UpD
is not necessarily symmetric.
Let
\overline{\UpD}
be the standard matrix transpose of
\UpD
. We have that

(B.2)

\displaystyle\|\UpD\|^{2}_{2}=\|\UpD\cdot\overline{\UpD}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\||\UpD\cdot\overline{\UpD}|\|_{2}\leq\||\UpD|\cdot|\overline{\UpD}|\|_{2}=\||\UpD|\|^{2}_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The first inequality follows by noting that
\UpD\cdot\overline{\UpD}
is a symmetric matrix. The second inequality follows by noting that

|\UpD\cdot\overline{\UpD}|\leq|\UpD|\cdot|\overline{\UpD}|
.

Lemma

B.2
follows.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

For integer
N>0
, let
\UpD,\UpB\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}_{\geq 0}
. If
\UpD\leq\UpB
, then
\|\UpD\|_{2}\leq\|\UpB\|_{2}
.

Proof.

We have that
\|\UpD\|_{2}=\sup_{{\bf z}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\|{\bf z}\|_{2}=1}{\|\UpD{\bf z}\|_{2}}
.
Hence, we have that

(B.3)

\displaystyle\|\UpD\|_{2}=\sup\nolimits_{{\bf z}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\|{\bf z}\|_{2}=1}\sqrt{\sum\nolimits_{i=1,\ldots,N}\sum\nolimits_{j=1,\ldots,N}\UpD(i,j)\cdot{\bf z}(j)}\kern 5.0pt.

Since the entries of
\UpD
are assumed to be non-negative, it is elementary to verify that supremum, above, is achieved for

{\bf z}
whose entries are non-negative.

Let
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
be such that
\|{\bf x}\|_{2}=1
and
\|\UpD\cdot{\bf x}\|_{2}=\|\UpD\|_{2}
, while
let
{\bf v}=\UpD{\bf x}
and
{\bf w}=\UpB{\bf x}
. Since the entries of
\UpB
are assumed to be non-negative, while
\UpD\leq\UpB
,
for any
i\in[N]
we have that
{\bf w}(i)\geq{\bf v}(i)
. Clearly, this implies that

(B.4)

\displaystyle\|\UpD\|_{2}=\|{\bf v}\|_{2}\leq\|{\bf w}\|_{2}\leq\|\UpB\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The above concludes the proof of
Lemma

B.3
.
∎
